**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous path integral approach to apply the Truncated Wigner Approximation (TWA) to open, dissipative spin systems. By incorporating the necessary SU(2) star product into the Keldysh formulation, the authors overcome inconsistencies found in previous methods. The resulting framework provides a systematic and theoretically sound way to simulate complex quantum dynamics on curved phase spaces.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the limitations of applying semiclassical methods like TWA to spin systems where non-commutativity is crucial; correctly handling the star product is essential for obtaining physically accurate results beyond simple mean-field treatments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a consistent path integral formulation for applying the Truncated Wigner Approximation (TWA) to open, driven-dissipative interacting spin systems.

**Main result.** The authors establish that incorporating the SU(2) star product into the path integral yields stochastic equations that correctly reproduce the exact dynamics of single dissipative spins, providing a unified field-theoretic foundation for TWA in this regime.

**Method.** The work employs a Keldysh path-integral formulation on the continuous SU(2) phase space, adapting techniques from bosonic systems to handle spin operators via a non-commutative star product ($\star$).

**Model / system.** The system under study is interacting open spin-1/2 systems, which can be realized in platforms like Rydberg atom arrays. The dynamics are governed by the Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Expectation values of spin operators ($\langle\hat{O}angle$), represented as phase-space averages over the Wigner function or flattened symbol $\chi(\Omega)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The approximation is semiclassical, involving truncations related to quantum fluctuations and the order of corrections from the star product.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption involves mapping operator products onto the curved spin phase space using a 'star product' ($\star$), which must be treated carefully as it generates $\mathcal{O}(1)$ corrections unlike in simpler bosonic cases.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes reference Figure 1 showing the Keldysh path integral contour and equations detailing the evolution step (Equation 20).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first defining the SU(2) phase space, then constructing the path integral for Lindblad evolution, focusing critically on deriving and implementing the necessary star product corrections to ensure consistency with known single-spin dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Phase-space approaches such as the truncated Wigner approximation (TWA) provide an efficient semiclassical framework for performing approximate simulations of the dynamics of open quantum many-body systems outside the reach of exact numerical methods but beyond the mean-field level. For bosonic systems, TWA is known to be equivalent to a Keldysh path-integral formulation truncated at second order in the so-called quantum fluctuations. This semiclassical approach provides an alternative transparent route towards approximate stochastic equations of motion, which can be efficiently solved. Here we establish the corresponding path-integral formulation for interacting open spin-$1/2$ systems using the continuous $\mathrm{SU}(2)$ phase space. We show, in particular, that a consistent treatment of dissipation requires correctly mapping operator products onto the curved spin phase space, leading to stochastic equations that coincide with those obtained from the continuous TWA formulation and thus reproduce the exact dynamics of a single dissipative spin. Our results provide a unified field-theoretic foundation for the TWA to dissipative spin dynamics and offer a systematic starting point for extensions beyond the semiclassical approximation.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors theoretically demonstrate that ordered atomic rings can act as robust quantum memories and processors for photonic excitations. By controlling dipole orientation and inter-ring spacing, they achieve coherent transport and controlled interactions between stored subradiant light quanta. This establishes the platform's potential for fault-tolerant quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the use of solid-state or structured matter platforms (atomic arrays) for building scalable photonic quantum circuits by leveraging engineered collective decay suppression.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper demonstrates how coherent control can be achieved for subradiant excitations trapped within ordered atomic rings.

**Main result.** They show that the system supports adiabatic transport around a ring, geometry-controlled transfer between neighboring rings, and interaction-induced conditional phase shifts, enabling photonic quantum information processing.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical modeling based on the master equation and effective Hamiltonians derived for collective excitations in atomic arrays.

**Model / system.** The system consists of ordered subwavelength atomic arrays, specifically ring-shaped structures containing two-level emitters. Dynamics are governed by coupling terms ($V_{\alphaeta}$) and dissipation ($\Gamma_{\alphaeta}$), analyzed within the single or two-excitation manifold.

**Key observables.** Collective excitation survival probability ($P_{	ext{sur}}$), single-excitation transfer probability ($P_{tr}$), and interaction-induced excess phase ($\Delta\phi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interatomic separation ($r_{\alphaeta}$), dipole orientation ($	heta$), ring separation ($f$), and the requirement for subradiant protection.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system remains largely confined to the single-excitation manifold, and adiabatic evolution requires slow changes relative to energy gaps.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate excitation transport during dipole rotation (Fig. 1), controlled exchange dynamics between two rings based on geometry (Fig. 2), and the dependence of phase accumulation and fidelity on separation distance (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing single-ring adiabatic transport, then demonstrating controllable inter-ring transfer using symmetric vs. shifted geometries, and finally analyzing two-excitation dynamics to realize controlled-phase quantum gates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Collective excitations in ordered subwavelength atomic arrays can exhibit strongly suppressed radiative decay due to interference between light scattered by neighboring emitters. These so-called subradiant states make these systems a promising platform for storing and manipulating photonic excitations. The external geometry of the array, combined with dynamical control of the atomic dipole orientation, enables localized trapping and coherent transport of these subradiant excitations. Here, we theoretically demonstrate these capabilities in ring-shaped atomic arrays. Specifically, we show adiabatic transport of a localized excitation around a single ring, coherent transfer of a single excitation between two neighboring rings with geometry-controlled selectivity, and interaction-induced conditional phase shifts between two simultaneously trapped excitations in neighboring rings. The latter can be interpreted as effective controlled-phase operations between stored excitations. Together, these results demonstrate the potential of ordered atomic arrays as a platform for coherent photonic quantum information processing with dissipation-protected collective excitations.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates the complex dynamics of bosons in a 1D lattice subject to pumping and dissipation. By employing advanced theoretical tools like the Keldysh formalism and tensor networks, the authors characterize how the system behaves when transitioning from weakly to strongly interacting regimes. The findings reveal universal scaling behaviors (KPZ) in some limits while demonstrating fundamental differences between quantum and classical descriptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium dynamics in bosonic systems, which is central to modern AMO physics research involving driven quantum simulators or optical lattices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study characterizes driven-dissipative bosonic systems in one dimension across varying filling and interaction strengths, focusing on the transition between weakly and strongly interacting regimes.

**Main result.** The system exhibits Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) scaling in the weak interaction/large filling regime, while strong interactions reveal a change of nature in excitations compared to mean-field predictions.

**Method.** A combination of Keldysh formalism for deriving actions, stochastic semi-classical simulations, and tensor network methods are employed to analyze quantum dynamics.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional Bose-Hubbard system subjected to incoherent pumping and various particle losses (one-body and two-body). The evolution is governed by the Lindblad master equation or its semi-classical/Keldysh approximations.

**Key observables.** Two-point space-time correlations, spectral function linewidth $A(\omega, k)$, and first-order correlation function $g^{(1)}(x, t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling (density), interaction strength ($U$), pumping rate ($\gamma_p$), and loss rates ($\gamma_l, \gamma_{2l}$). Key regimes include large filling/weak interaction and weak filling/strong interaction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The semi-classical limit is used for some analyses, which neglects certain non-linear quantum field terms. The analysis often focuses on stationary or quasi-stationary correlation regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 14 compares semiclassical and quantum predictions for density distributions ($ho$) and occupation probabilities ($p_N$) across different system sizes and loss mechanisms.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the dynamics by first establishing the theoretical framework (Keldysh formalism, Lindblad equation), then investigating specific regimes (weak vs. strong coupling) using appropriate tools (semi-classical vs. tensor networks), and finally comparing results to highlight deviations from mean-field theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider a one-dimensional driven-dissipative Bose-Hubbard model subjected to incoherent pump and one- and two-body losses, and analyze it by studying its two-point space-time correlations. By employing a combination of numerical methods, such as stochastic semi-classical simulations and tensor network methods, as well as perturbation theory within the Keldysh formalism, we characterize the system at varying filling and interaction strength. We present results for two complementary regimes: i) at large filling and weak interactions, where we show that Kardar-Parisi-Zhang scaling is visible in the linewidth of the spectral function and ii) at weak filling and strong interactions, where we analyze what remains of the mean field transition and identify a change of nature of the excitations. Our study covers two important regions of the phase diagram of such a system.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses error-mitigated quantum computing on superconducting hardware to study prethermal dynamics in a Floquet Ising magnet. The researchers successfully measured long-lived oscillations up to 74 qubits, demonstrating that these phenomena persist into the thermodynamic limit where classical simulations fail. It establishes error-mitigated quantum processors as reliable tools for discovering new physics in non-equilibrium matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides concrete evidence that quantum computation can probe non-equilibrium physics regimes (prethermalization) inaccessible to current best classical computational resources, directly impacting the study of open quantum systems dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is accessing and characterizing prethermal dynamics in periodically driven interacting quantum many-body systems, where classical methods fail at required system sizes/times.

**Main result.** The authors successfully measured long-lived subharmonic prethermal oscillations on up to 74 qubits using error mitigation, suggesting these oscillatory responses persist into the thermodynamic limit for heavy-hex ladders.

**Method.** The work combines advanced quantum hardware measurements (using QESEM error mitigation) with rigorous comparison against state-of-the-art classical simulations like tensor networks and Pauli-path methods.

**Model / system.** The study investigates a Floquet Ising magnet realized on a heavy-hex lattice, focusing on its non-equilibrium dynamics under periodic driving.

**Key observables.** Magnetization ($M$), prethermal oscillation amplitude decay with system size, and simulation fidelity measures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System sizes up to 74 qubits; specific parameter regimes ($	heta_x \approx \pi/6$, $	heta_z \approx \pi/27$, etc.) that yield structured dynamics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reliability of results hinges on the successful application and validation of advanced error mitigation techniques (QESEM) across multiple quantum hardware platforms.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare simulation runtimes and accuracy frontiers between classical methods (PEPS-BP, Statevector) and QESEM measurements; other figures show parameter scans and universal collapse plots validating error models.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem, details the advanced quantum measurement techniques required to overcome hardware noise, presents results showing measurable prethermal features beyond classical simulation limits, and concludes by establishing error-mitigated quantum processors as quantitative scientific tools.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Periodically driven interacting quantum many-body systems can exhibit long-lived prethermal dynamics, where local observables retain coherent structure even as entanglement and operator complexity grow. Accessing this regime at the system sizes and times needed to determine physical properties of the prethermal state remains a central challenge: state-of-the-art classical methods become unreliable, while noise in quantum hardware degrades observable expectation values. Here we overcome these limitations for a Floquet Ising magnet realized on a heavy-hex lattice. Using the advanced error mitigation software QESEM on an IBM Heron r3 superconducting quantum processor, we measure magnetization dynamics with percent-level precision and resolve long-lived subharmonic prethermal oscillations in systems of up to 74 qubits. These experiments reach regimes for which leading tensor-network simulations fail to converge, while sparse Pauli-path simulations remain strongly truncation dependent despite extensive computations on advanced GPUs and the Fugaku supercomputer. Leveraging this quantum-accessible regime, we extend finite-size scaling to larger systems and find an unexpectedly slow decrease of the oscillation amplitude with system size, providing strong evidence that this oscillatory response persists in the thermodynamic limit of heavy-hex ladders. A hierarchy of mitigation and validation tests, including unbiased error mitigation, agreement between independent mitigation estimators, noise-model validation on the superconducting hardware, and cross-platform corroboration at selected Floquet cycles on Quantinuum System Model H2 and Quantinuum Helios trapped-ion hardware, supports the reliability of these findings. Our work establishes error-mitigated quantum processors as quantitative scientific instruments for discovering new physics in non-equilibrium quantum matter.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an agentic workflow designed to automate the complex process of turning theoretical quantum protocols into executable experiments on neutral-atom quantum processors. By successfully running multiple case studies and screening hundreds of papers, it proves that AI tools can bridge the gap between theory and hardware. This significantly lowers the barrier for researchers in AMO physics to conduct cutting-edge quantum simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical implementation gap for AMO theorists. By automating the engineering steps—from protocol extraction to noise modeling on specific hardware like Pasqal QPUs—it allows theorists to rapidly test feasibility across a much broader range of physical models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The major bottleneck in quantum research is translating theoretical protocols into executable hardware experiments due to required expertise across multiple domains.

**Main result.** Agentic workflows provide a practical bridge by automating the pipeline from scientific paper to QPU campaign, significantly accelerating the path from theory to physical realization.

**Method.** The authors developed and demonstrated an agentic workflow that automates protocol extraction, hardware mapping, simulation (noiseless/noise-aware), and execution on neutral-atom quantum processors.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on neutral-atom Quantum Processing Units (QPUs) utilizing Rydberg arrays to realize programmable many-body Hamiltonians. The system is controlled by parameters like Rabi frequency, detuning, and qubit geometry.

**Key observables.** Density-density correlator G(r), structure factor, mean magnetization m, and comparison of measured data against noiseless simulation envelopes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Device constraints include maximum atom number, achievable detuning range ($	ext{FC1}: |\delta|/2\pi \le 10 	ext{ MHz}$), and interaction types (e.g., Ising vs XY).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Human domain expertise remains crucial for validating scientific assumptions, selecting physically meaningful observables, and interpreting complex results.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares literature values of the density-density correlator G(r) with measurements on FC1 and SA1 for the $Z_2$ phase. Figure 3 (mentioned) compares QMC data to QPU data for the $1/3$ plateau.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the agentic workflow, demonstrates its use in three case studies from literature (many-body physics and optimization), details the process of screening existing arXiv papers, and concludes by outlining the shift in the research bottleneck to scientific judgment.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computers are moving from research laboratories to industrial machines accessible via the cloud and integrated into high-performance computing facilities. However, translating theoretical quantum protocols into hardware experiments remains a major bottleneck, requiring expertise across protocol design, compilation, simulation, and cloud execution. Here, we introduce an agentic workflow that automates this pipeline on neutral-atom quantum processors (here two Pasqal QPUs available on the cloud) while keeping the researcher in the loop for critical validation. In three case studies from many-body physics and optimization, the agent went from published paper or patent to a QPU campaign run overnight. In particular, human intervention was crucial to ensure scientific validity: the agent selected an inadequate observable in one experiment and constructed a plausible but incorrect hardware diagnosis in another, with both failures detected only through domain-expert review. Finally, we use a second agent to classify a corpus of 633 Rydberg-array arXiv papers and show that nearly half are implementable on present-day QPUs while identifying specific hardware upgrades needed for the rest. Together, these results demonstrate that agentic workflows provide a practical bridge between theoretical ideas and physical hardware, opening quantum experimentation to a much broader scientific community.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a unified theoretical framework to describe the stochastic dynamics of interacting particles subjected to nonequilibrium driving, especially when interactions are nonreciprocal. The key finding is that the effective diffusion constant exhibits a nonmonotonic dependence on the driving strength, leading to both enhanced and suppressed transport regimes. By analyzing these effects in active matter models, the authors establish a general mechanism governing transport under various forms of external forcing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides quantitative tools to analyze transport phenomena driven by non-equilibrium forces, which is highly relevant to understanding energy dissipation and particle motion in complex systems studied using AMO techniques (e.g., engineered potentials or external fields).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the fundamental challenge of understanding stochastic dynamics for interacting particles operating far from thermodynamic equilibrium, particularly when nonreciprocal interactions are present.

**Main result.** The effective diffusivity exhibits a nonmonotonic dependence on driving activity, showing both enhancement and suppression of transport. This behavior is captured by a unified framework applicable to active and passive nonequilibrium driving.

**Method.** The authors develop a theoretical framework using Langevin dynamics approximations (harmonic approximation) and analyze the Mean-Squared Displacement (MSD) via two-time force correlations.

**Model / system.** The model consists of interacting particles on a periodic ring, where one particle is passive (coupled to thermal noise) and the other is active (e.g., Run-and-Tumble dynamics). The interaction strength and reciprocity are controlled by parameters like $\mu$ and $v_0$.

**Key observables.** Effective diffusivity ($D_{	ext{eff}}$), Mean-Squared Displacement ($\langle\Delta x^2angle(t)$), and the two-time force correlation function.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Self-propulsion speed ($v_0$), bare diffusivities ($D, D_0$), persistence time ($	au$), and the reciprocity parameter ($\mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as using the harmonic approximation for interactions and neglecting interaction forces in certain high-diffusivity limits.

**Figures summary.** Figures 1 and 2 numerically demonstrate $D_{	ext{eff}}$ vs. $v_0$, showing nonmonotonic behavior (increase, peak, decrease) compared to analytical predictions derived from the low-activity regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing a general framework for interacting particles with both reciprocal and nonreciprocal couplings. It then derives specific analytical results for the effective diffusivity in the weak coupling limit, before generalizing the findings to compare active driving against passive (high-temperature) nonequilibrium driving scenarios.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The stochastic dynamics of interacting particles far from equilibrium remains a fundamental challenge in statistical physics. While reciprocal interactions often permit effective one-body descriptions, such reductions generally fail for nonreciprocal interactions, which are ubiquitous in driven and active systems. We develop a unified theoretical framework for interacting particles with reciprocal and nonreciprocal couplings, applicable to the principal classes of active matter, including run-and-tumble, active Brownian, and active Ornstein-Uhlenbeck particles. As a minimal example, we study a passive particle driven by an active particle. On a periodic ring, we show analytically that the driven particle is always diffusive at long times, independent of the microscopic driving mechanism. Analytical predictions and numerical simulations reveal a nonmonotonic dependence of the effective diffusivity on the driving activity, giving rise to both enhanced and suppressed transport. Remarkably, the same behavior occurs in an equilibrium system driven out of equilibrium by coupling the driving particle to a higher local temperature. Our framework quantitatively captures both systems, identifies the common mechanism underlying the nonmonotonic transport, and establishes a unified description of transport under active and passive nonequilibrium driving.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes the late-time relaxation of subsystem correlations following a quantum quench in the 1D transverse-field Ising chain. Using the Bures distance, they demonstrate that this decay follows a discrete power law $t^{-\lambda}$. The exponent $\lambda$ is not arbitrary but is rigidly determined by the specific parameters defining the initial and final states, revealing universal structure in local equilibration dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep quantitative understanding of non-equilibrium dynamics in integrable quantum systems, offering universal rules for how local information spreads and thermalizes after a sudden change in Hamiltonian parameters.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how local quantum correlations decay over time following a global quench in an integrable many-body system.

**Main result.** The subsystem distance decays universally according to a discrete power law, $B_A(t) \sim t^{-\lambda}$, where the exponent $\lambda$ is restricted to specific rational values determined by the initial and final Hamiltonian parameters.

**Method.** The analysis uses numerical simulations of the Bures distance between the time-evolved reduced density matrix and its generalized Gibbs ensemble counterpart.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the infinite one-dimensional transverse-field Ising chain, governed by a Hamiltonian dependent on pre-quench ($h_0$) and post-quench ($h$) transverse fields. The quench involves evolving an initial eigenstate under the new Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Bures distance $B_A(t)$, which quantifies deviation from equilibrium; decay exponents $\lambda$ and $	ilde{\lambda}$; subsystem size scaling exponent $\alpha$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pre-quench field ($h_0$), post-quench field ($h$), and the properties of the symmetric excitation-fraction function $m_S(\varphi)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on numerical studies across various parameter regimes, and while robust, a complete analytical derivation for all exponents is pending.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate representative $(h_0, h)$ pairs covering distinct physical regimes; flowcharts guide the determination of $\lambda$ based on the continuity and differentiability of $m_S(\varphi)$; numerical plots confirm power-law decay with discrete exponents.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the model and quench protocol, then classifies representative field pairs $(h_0, h)$ using flowcharts derived from the analytic properties of the excitation function $m_S(\varphi)$, culminating in the presentation of the main results on discrete power-law decay.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a numerical study of subsystem distance decay following a global quantum quench in the infinite one-dimensional transverse-field Ising chain, using the mathematically rigorous Bures distance $B_A(t)$ to quantify the deviation of the time-evolved reduced density matrix from its stationary generalized Gibbs ensemble state. We show that the late-time decay follows a discrete power law $B_A(t) \sim t^{-λ}$, with the exponent $λ$ confined to discrete values: $1$, $5/4$, $3/2$, $7/4$, $2$, $5/2$, and potentially further values. The specific exponent is jointly determined by the pre- and post-quench transverse fields, as well as by properties of the symmetric excitation-fraction function $m_S(\varphi)$, defined on $\varphi\in[0,π]$ to characterize the pre-quench Hamiltonian eigenstates, including continuity, boundary values, and first-derivative boundary values, among others. The previously established $t^{-3/2}$ decay for the initial ground state of the pre-quench Hamiltonian is naturally recovered as a special case of this general classification. Our results reveal a universal discrete structure governing local equilibration dynamics in integrable quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The research presents a novel, monolithic photonic integrated circuit designed to overcome bandwidth limitations in trapped-ion quantum computing. By combining planar waveguides with 3D-printed micromirrors, the device achieves broadband, individually addressable light delivery across multiple wavelengths. This breakthrough significantly enhances the scalability and complexity of future trapped-ion quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering challenge of scaling quantum processors. The integration of additive manufacturing (2PP) into established photonic platforms is a major advancement for realizing complex, scalable quantum hardware architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Delivering multiple, distinct wavelengths across large networks of interaction zones for trapped-ion qubit control is a major bottleneck due to scalability issues with conventional free-space or narrow-bandwidth on-chip optics.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a monolithic photonic integrated circuit that successfully achieves broadband addressing (405–880 nm) and individual ion addressing while maintaining low optical crosstalk. This integrates 3D micro-optics fabricated via two-photon polymerization with surface electrode traps on silicon wafers.

**Method.** The system combines lithographically defined waveguides with custom micromirrors created using two-photon polymerization (2PP) to focus light onto trapped ions ($	ext{Ca}^+$ and $	ext{Ba}^+$). Characterization involves measuring beam profiles, crosstalk, and ion repumping across the specified wavelength range.

**Model / system.** The platform uses trapped ions ($	ext{Ca}^+$, $	ext{Ba}^+$) controlled by a hybrid photonic integrated circuit. This PIC integrates planar waveguides with 3D-printed micromirrors to deliver light efficiently and broadly across multiple wavelengths onto the ion trap region.

**Key observables.** Broadband addressing capability (405–880 nm); low optical crosstalk ($\le -27 	ext{ dB}$ at $5 \mu	ext{m}$ pitch); tight, broadband focusing profiles; successful single-ion repumping demonstration.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Wavelength range: 405 nm to 880 nm; Crosstalk: $-27 	ext{ dB}$; Pitch: $5 \mu	ext{m}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The relative crosstalk profile measured at one wavelength (e.g., 455 nm) can be assumed to scale linearly for estimating coherence times at other wavelengths (e.g., 493 nm).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device architecture, showing planar lenses and $\mu$-mirrors; beam profiles confirm tight focusing across the broadband range; crosstalk measurements quantify low signal leakage between addressed ions.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the fabrication process flow (combining LPCVD/ALD with 2PP), presents optical characterization results demonstrating broadband focusing and low crosstalk, validates ion trapping for $	ext{Ca}^+$ and $	ext{Ba}^+$, and concludes by showing individual addressing capability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Trapped ions provide a high-fidelity platform for quantum information processing, yet delivery of multiple, distinct wavelengths across large networks of interaction zones remains a bottleneck. Conventional free-space light delivery lacks scalability, while on-chip grating couplers suffer from narrow operational bandwidth that increases circuit footprint and optical interfacing complexity. Here we show a broadband photonic integrated circuit capable of addressing individual ions. The circuit combines a planar waveguide lens with a micromirror fabricated using two-photon polymerization at wafer scale. This implementation can address three individual ions from $λ$ = 405 - 880 nm with -27 dB average intensity crosstalk at $5\,μ\mathrm{m}$ pitch. We trap $^{40}\mathrm{Ca}^{+}$ and $^{138}\mathrm{Ba}^{+}$ ions above such devices, characterize optical crosstalk with barium ions, and demonstrate individual repumping of calcium ions. This monolithic photonic architecture brings broadband addressing in an on-chip modality to trapped-ion technology. More generally, integrating additive manufacturing into quantum devices is poised to unlock expanded design space for implementing novel quantum architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study explores superradiant phase transitions in Fermi gases coupled to optical cavities, demonstrating that the transition point is controlled by a competition between scalar and vectorial light-matter couplings. By tuning the relative polarization angle of the driving fields, researchers can manipulate this coupling balance, leading to insights applicable to both fermionic and bosonic quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges concepts from cavity QED (superradiance) with strongly interacting quantum matter (Fermi gases), providing tunable control over collective quantum phenomena using light polarization—a highly relevant area for experimental AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the superradiant quantum phase transition (QPT) occurring in a spin-degenerate Fermi gas coupled to an optical cavity.

**Main result.** The phase transition threshold is found to be synergistically determined by both the scalar-vectorial coupling weight and Pauli blocking effects. Furthermore, the relative polarization angle can tune the competition between these couplings.

**Method.** The analysis employs mean-field theory applied to the effective Hamiltonian derived after eliminating excited states, utilizing steady-state solutions of the Lindblad master equation and free energy minimization techniques.

**Model / system.** The system is a spin-degenerate Fermi gas confined in a high-finesse optical cavity, where interactions are mediated by light fields whose coupling strength depends on the relative polarization angle ($\phi$) between the pump and cavity fields. The model incorporates both scalar and vectorial atom-light couplings.

**Key observables.** Superradiance transition threshold, critical polarization angle ($\varphi_{	ext{crit}}$), population ratio ($n_
u$), and susceptibility ($\chi_{mF}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Relative polarization angle ($\phi$), cavity detuning ($\Delta_c$), pump lattice depth ($V_0$), and particle number imbalance ratio ($n_
u$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a mean-field approximation for photon dynamics, and the free energy calculation is used to identify continuous transition points.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup geometry (Fig. 1), show density dependence of susceptibility ($\chi_{mF}$) vs filling factor (Fig. 2(a)), map out phase diagrams showing Normal vs Superradiant phases based on polarization angle and lattice depth (Fig. 3(a)), and depict phase boundaries related to particle imbalance (Fig. 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the effective Hamiltonian, deriving mean-field equations for photon dynamics, analyzing the conditions for superradiance via free energy minimization, and finally mapping out complex phase diagrams showing how polarization angle and density control the transition order and type.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate a spin-degenerate Fermi gas coupled to a high-finesse optical cavity, where the competition between scalar and vectorial couplings is controlled by the relative polarization angle of the pump and cavity fields. We find that the phase transition threshold is synergistically determined by the scalar-vectorial coupling weight and Pauli blocking, with the latter dictating the critical pump lattice depth required for the onset of superradiance. For a two-component Fermi gas with opposite spins, the population ratio drives two distinct types of phase transitions corresponding to real-space phase separation: continuous and discontinuous. Nevertheless, the boundary of the phase transition remains fundamentally governed by the scalar-vectorial coupling competition. We clarify the impact of the relative polarization angle on phase transitions of the system; these results also apply to bosonic systems. Our results provide valuable theoretical insights for future experimental realizations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically explores collective light-matter interactions in arrays of multi-level quantum emitters. By including full multi-level interference effects into the master equation, the authors demonstrate that these interferences fundamentally alter decay dynamics compared to simpler models. The findings suggest new physical criteria for phenomena like superradiance and propose molecular systems for experimental realization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics as it pushes beyond standard two-level approximations to model complex quantum emitters (like molecules) in arrays, which is crucial for next-generation quantum light sources and quantum simulation platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how accounting for the multi-level nature of quantum emitters fundamentally alters the collective light-matter interactions compared to simplified two-level approximations.

**Main result.** Multi-level interference significantly changes collective state character, leading to a lower bound on emitter separation required for superradiance that is absent in the two-level case.

**Method.** The dynamics are modeled using a full Master Equation derived under the Born-Markov approximation, allowing for direct inclusion of multi-level interference terms.

**Model / system.** The system consists of an array of N multi-level quantum emitters (e.g., V-systems) interacting with the electromagnetic vacuum field. The theoretical framework uses a full Hamiltonian and Master Equation approach to track population dynamics.

**Key observables.** Total photon emission rate, initial photon emission rate ($\gamma$), and the difference in initial rates ($\Delta\gamma$) between interfering and non-interfering models.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Emitter separation ($d$), energy separation between excited states ($\Delta E_e$), and the ratio of $d$ to the excitation wavelength ($\lambda_e$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the Born-Markov approximation, and approximations are made for near-resonant transitions (e.g., replacing frequencies with averages).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare population dynamics and initial photon emission rates between models that include multi-level interference versus those that neglect it, showing deviations at small separations.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Master Equation incorporating full dipole interactions, analyzes the resulting equations of motion for the density matrix, and then numerically compares predictions for superradiance and population decay across various emitter separation regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We explore how collective states of light and matter differ when the multi-level nature of the quantum emitters is fully taken into account. For closely spaced emitters, interferences between near-resonant transitions completely change the character of the collective states compared to two-level approximations. In particular, we find a lower bound on the emitter separation for superradiance to occur which does not exist for two-level emitters. By contrast, for larger separations between the emitters, the collective states resemble those obtained within the widely used two-level approximation of the emitters. Both regimes may be realized by molecules trapped in optical lattices. We therefore propose molecular candidates and describe experimental signatures of emerging multi-level interference.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates how placing charged macroscopic oscillators near a conducting wire fundamentally alters their Coulomb interaction, enhancing the coupling strength at large separations. By combining this enhanced interaction with continuous position measurements, the authors show that steady-state motional entanglement can be generated over distances far exceeding free-space limits. This establishes conductor-assisted interactions as a novel resource for quantum control.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum control of massive objects by engineering long-range interactions, which is highly relevant for cavity QED and open quantum systems research involving mechanical resonators.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors propose a method to enhance the Coulomb interaction between massive mechanical oscillators by placing them near a conducting structure, aiming to generate steady-state motional entanglement over long distances.

**Main result.** They predict that the wire modifies the coupling scaling from $1/D^3$ to $1/(D\ln^2 D)$, enabling observable entanglement at separations much larger than achievable in free space.

**Method.** The analysis uses macroscopic quantum electrodynamics, deriving effective dynamics via a master equation approach and employing continuous measurement theory (Kalman filtering analogy).

**Model / system.** The system consists of two charged macroscopic mechanical oscillators coupled by both free-space Coulomb interaction and an image charge interaction mediated by a nearby conducting wire. The dynamics are described by a Hamiltonian and subsequently analyzed using a master equation.

**Key observables.** Steady-state motional entanglement, renormalized mechanical frequency ($\Omega'_f$), and the modified coupling rate ($G_{	ext{tot}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Separation $D$, oscillator mass $m$, wire geometry (cylindrical), and operating regime (low-frequency limit $\hbar\Omega \ll k_B T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the electroquasistatic limit, linearizes position dependence around equilibrium points, and utilizes approximations like Born-Markov for bath tracing.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup showing image charge generation; one figure compares coupling rates vs. distance $D$ (free space vs. wire); another shows rate dependencies on separation and proximity to instability.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from deriving the effective Hamiltonian incorporating medium effects, leading to a master equation description of dynamics, simplifying this master equation using approximations like RWA, and finally analyzing state evolution under continuous measurement via Kalman filtering analogies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a method to enhance Coulomb interaction between charged macroscopic mechanical oscillators by placing a conducting structure in their vicinity. We derive the effective motional dynamics of the two oscillators using macroscopic quantum electrodynamics and show that image charges induced in the conductor fundamentally modify the range of the electrostatic interaction. For the specific case of a cylindrical wire, we predict that the coherent motional coupling changes from the free-space scaling $1/D^3$ to an asymptotic $1/(D\ln^2 D)$ dependence on the separation $D$ between the oscillators, at the cost of only negligible additional decoherence for low-frequency oscillators. We further show that, when combined with continuous position measurements, the enhanced interaction enables the generation of steady-state motional entanglement between the oscillators over significantly larger distances than achievable in free space. For experimentally realistic milligram-scale oscillators, we predict observable entanglement at separations of several hundred microns -- more than an order of magnitude beyond free-space capabilities -- with improvements approaching two orders of magnitude in future systems. These results identify conductor-assisted Coulomb interactions as a resource for quantum control of massive objects and for the exploration of entanglement generated by fundamental central forces.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a systematic method to design solvable quantum circuits whose evolution captures non-Markovian correlations typical of interacting many-body systems. By deforming free-fermionic Clifford gates, the authors generate influence matrices that are computationally tractable while exhibiting complex temporal dependencies. This advances the field by bridging the gap between simple solvable models and realistic, strongly correlated dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical tool to simulate complex, non-Markovian dynamics in quantum many-body systems using structured circuits, which is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems and realistic quantum simulation architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors aim to develop a systematic method for constructing quantum circuits whose influence matrices can be exactly calculated, specifically achieving non-Markovian dynamics while maintaining solvability.

**Main result.** They successfully construct a broad family of solvable circuits by deforming free-fermionic Clifford gates with interaction terms, yielding non-Markovian influence matrices that mimic complex many-body correlations.

**Method.** The analysis involves using tensor network representations for influence matrices and applying operator trajectory decomposition in the Heisenberg picture to derive necessary structural conditions (CDU2/CDU3) for solvability.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum circuit acting on local subsystems of an extended quantum many-body system, described by $q$-level qudits arranged in a 1D array. The evolution is governed by gates derived from free-fermionic Clifford circuits.

**Key observables.** Influence matrices ($\mathcal{L}_t, \mathcal{R}_t$), two-point correlation function $C(x, y, t)$, and the satisfaction of structural conditions like CDU2 and CDU3.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bond dimension $\chi$, fermion velocities ($v$), and interaction strengths ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that solvability can be maintained by finding specific structural deformations (e.g., restricting hopping patterns or ensuring stabilizer commutation relations) despite the introduction of non-Markovian terms.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the tensor network structure for dynamical correlations, while Figure 2 compares correlation functions for three example circuits (I, II, III) demonstrating different solvability classes (CDU2 vs CDU3).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the need for non-Markovian solvable models beyond simple dual-unitary circuits. It then details using free-fermionic Clifford gates and systematically introduces deformations ($U_I(	heta)$) to satisfy increasingly complex solvability conditions (CDU2 $	o$ CDU3), culminating in deriving local commutation relations required for full solvability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Influence matrices encode the action exerted on local subsystems by the rest of an extended quantum many-body system during their evolution. Thus, knowledge of the influence matrix facilitates computationally efficient simulations of local dynamics. Here we propose a new systematic approach to generating quantum circuits with complex dynamics for which the influence matrices can be written down exactly. In contrast to previous frameworks of this kind, such as dual-unitary circuits, the resulting influence matrices are non-Markovian, exhibiting nontrivial temporal correlations. We explicitly construct a broad family of circuits of this kind, based on dressing free-fermion (matchgate) circuits with appropriately chosen interaction terms. We show that, contrary to previous solvable instances, these circuits produce patterns of correlations that closely resemble that of typical many-body systems. Our approach can be directly interpreted in terms of an error correction scheme where the terms breaking the solvability of the influence matrices play the role of errors.

</details>

**Authors:** Urmimala Dewan, Roson Nongthombam, Sampreet Kalita, Amarendra K. Sarma

**Summary.** The paper proposes an entanglement-swapping protocol to generate nonlocal magnonic cat states across separated subsystems. It utilizes hybrid architectures combining transmon qubits and YIG magnon modes, demonstrating how projective measurements can transfer entanglement over distance. This work advances the feasibility of long-distance quantum networking using solid-state bosonic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum networking by utilizing solid-state magnonic systems (YIG) coupled to superconducting qubits. The use of cat states in a long-distance setting is highly relevant for scalable quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to generate a bipartite magnonic cat state shared between two spatially separated subsystems.

**Main result.** The authors propose an entanglement-swapping protocol that successfully transfers magnon-qubit entanglement to create a nonlocal magnonic cat state, demonstrating a feasible route for long-distance quantum networking.

**Method.** The protocol involves generating initial magnon-qubit entanglement, using a microwave cavity interaction to generate qubit Bell states, and finally performing a projective Bell-state measurement on the qubits to project the remote magnon modes into the desired bipartite cat state.

**Model / system.** The system is a hybrid architecture combining superconducting transmon qubits with Yttrium Iron Garnet (YIG) spheres supporting magnon modes. The dynamics are modeled using a Hamiltonian and evolved via the Lindblad master equation to account for dissipation.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($F_{eta}$ or $F_{cat}$), Entanglement Negativity ($	ext{EN}(ho)$), and the joint Wigner function of the bipartite cat state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The effective detuning ($\Delta$), coupling strength ($g$), and decoherence rates ($\gamma_m, \gamma_q$) are key parameters governing the dynamics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the Rotating-Wave Approximation (RWA), and comparisons between analytical results and simulations must account for dissipation and measurement noise.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the hybrid system, show fidelity/entanglement evolution over time, and display the analytically calculated Wigner functions for the four possible bipartite cat states.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the Hamiltonian formulation, describes the entanglement-swapping protocol using Bell state measurements, provides analytical derivations for the resulting Wigner functions, and validates the scheme through simulation results.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quantum superpositions of coherent states offer an alternative to the conventional qubit-based encodings by harnessing the large Hilbert space available in bosonic modes, including those realised in microwave and optical cavities, magnons, and mechanical resonators. Beyond their advantages for local information processing, establishing long-distance quantum networks for such bosonic states is crucial for scalable quantum communication and distributed quantum computation. In this work, we propose an entanglement-swapping-based protocol to generate a bipartite magnonic cat state shared between spatially separated subsystems. Each subsystem comprises a hybrid architecture consisting of a superconducting transmon qubit coupled to a yttrium iron garnet (YIG) sphere that supports magnon modes. By performing a projective Bell-state measurement on the qubits, the initially established magnon-qubit entanglement is coherently transferred to the remote magnon modes, resulting in a nonlocal magnonic cat state. For experimental characterisation of the gener- ated states, we perform quantum state tomography through reconstruction of the Wigner function using joint displaced parity measurements of the magnon modes. Our scheme provides a feasible route towards realising long-distance magnonic entanglement and contributes to the advancement of hybrid quantum network architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel framework to validate quantum estimates of physical observables when exact classical verification is impossible. By applying this to semi-scrambling dynamics on superconducting qubits, the authors demonstrate methods that rely on internal consistency checks or precise noise model characterization rather than external ground truths. This establishes a crucial pathway toward trusted quantum computation for scientific discovery.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the 'trustworthiness' bottleneck in quantum simulation, providing concrete protocols for validating results when classical theory breaks down—a critical concern for AMO and open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is establishing a reliable method for validating quantum estimates of physical observables when direct, exact classical benchmarks are unavailable due to computational limitations.

**Main result.** The authors establish a framework using consistency checks or by rigorously characterizing the noise model (e.g., via PEC) to build confidence in quantum results independent of classical ground truth.

**Method.** They combine theoretical frameworks for error bounding (like Probabilistic Error Cancellation, PEC) with experimental measurements on superconducting hardware, utilizing advanced diagnostics like the operator Loschmidt echo.

**Model / system.** The study applies this framework to semi-scrambling dynamics of a heterogeneous Floquet Ising model simulated on a 56-qubit section of a heavy-hex lattice using superconducting transmon qubits.

**Key observables.** Operator Loschmidt echo (OLE) signal ($	ext{S}_\delta$), expectation values, and error rates for various gate types.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Scattering strength ($\eta$), perturbation strength ($\delta$), circuit depth ($L$), and Pauli error rates ($\lambda_P$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumptions involve the validity of noise models (e.g., assuming $\delta$-independence for global rescaling) and the ability to accurately characterize hardware noise channels.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate circuit diagrams for OLE computation, compare error rates across different gate types, show how classical heuristics diverge from quantum results at intermediate parameters, and detail overhead reduction achieved by advanced error mitigation techniques like SLC.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the need for validation beyond classical benchmarks to applying this framework using the OLE on a complex physical model. It then details sophisticated noise characterization methods (PEC/SLC) before concluding with tests of observable sensitivity across sub-lattices.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The predictive success of quantum mechanics underpins many areas of modern science, even as the exact simulation of large, interacting quantum systems remains beyond the reach of classical computation. This success has been enabled by the remarkable advancement of scalable numerical approximation methods, which often demonstrate practical accuracy despite the absence of formal guarantees. As quantum simulation pushes into regimes where these approximations struggle, a fundamental challenge arises: How can quantum outcomes be trusted when reliable classical benchmarks are unavailable? Here, we establish a framework for the independent validation of quantum estimates in this setting and present evidence that they provide the most credible result among several considered methods, in the absence of an immediately accessible ground-truth solution. We apply our framework to the semi-scrambling dynamics of a physical model that strains several leading classical simulation methods yet remains experimentally accessible, in part through our introduction of the \textit{operator Loschmidt echo}. We systematically design a series of experiments using quantum heuristics that, taken together, test the underlying assumptions and provide strong confidence in the observable estimates obtained from the quantum computer. We then show how this framework can be extended to place accuracy bounds on quantum estimates via careful characterization and manipulation of the device noise, transforming the problem of validating the observable estimation to validating the noise model. These results establish a route towards trusted quantum computation for scientific discovery, independent of classical verification.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that spontaneous decay during pulsed dynamical decoupling sequences fundamentally changes the expected behavior, introducing a strong dependence on detuning. This effect is modeled analytically and experimentally verified using both strontium atoms and transmon qubits. The findings establish Dissipative Carr-Purcell Spectroscopy (DCPS) as a new tool for quantum characterization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it shows that open system dynamics (dissipation) can be harnessed not just as noise, but as a source of measurable quantum information, leading to novel spectroscopy techniques for characterizing coherence in complex systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating how spontaneous dissipation (excited-state decay) fundamentally alters the behavior of pulsed dynamical decoupling sequences, potentially creating a strong detuning dependence where none existed before.

**Main result.** Dissipation can reshape coherent control in DD sequences, causing the system to exhibit a measurable interference signal whose phase and visibility are strongly dependent on the detuning from resonance.

**Method.** The study uses analytical and numerical modeling of driven two-level systems, validated by experimental measurements on both strontium atoms and superconducting transmon qubits.

**Model / system.** The core model is a driven two-level system qubit subjected to pulsed dynamical decoupling sequences (like Carr-Purcell). The physical platforms include free-space strontium atom interferometers and superconducting transmon qubits, both exhibiting relaxation ($T_1$).

**Key observables.** Phase shift ($\Delta\phi$), Visibility ($v$), and the detuning dependence of these quantities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detuning ($\delta$) relative to resonance, decay rate ($\gamma = 1/T_1$), and pulse timings (e.g., $	au_\pi$, $T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on first-order approximations in detuning error ($\delta$) and requires explicit correction factors for decay occurring during the final detection readout.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the gate-based impact of relaxation, Bloch sphere trajectories showing state distributions after decay (arcs), and measured $\Delta\phi$ and $v$ versus normalized detuning ($\delta/\Omega$) for both atomic and superconducting systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from establishing the theoretical mechanism of dissipation altering DD control to developing a new spectroscopic technique (DCPS) and finally presenting comparative experimental results across two distinct physical platforms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

One of the defining features of pulsed dynamical decoupling is its suppression of a driven qubit's sensitivity to static detuning errors between the drive field and qubit resonance. In this paper, we show that dissipation, in the form of excited-state decay, can change this behavior entirely, producing an interference signal with a strong detuning dependence. This signal arises from decay during driven evolution and relies on coherence retained by the qubit after such a decay event. We develop analytical and numerical models that capture the underlying mechanism and observe this same dissipation-induced detuning dependence experimentally in both a free-space strontium atom interferometer and a superconducting transmon qubit system. We also use this dissipation-induced detuning dependence as the basis for a new spectroscopic technique called Dissipative Carr-Purcell Spectroscopy (DCPS) and compare it with a traditional Ramsey sequence. Our results establish a regime of pulsed dynamical decoupling in which dissipation reshapes, rather than merely degrades, coherent control, and we expect these dynamics to be relevant to a wide range of quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates how spinless fermions behave far from equilibrium when subjected to dephasing noise. By analyzing the dynamics using Lindblad equations and spectral methods, the authors prove that higher-order correlation functions exhibit persistent diffusive tails at late times. This demonstrates that simple symmetry arguments are insufficient to predict transport properties in dissipative quantum many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete example of how dissipation fundamentally alters long-time transport properties in interacting quantum systems, offering insights applicable to open quantum systems studied in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study determines the non-equilibrium dynamics and asymptotic late-time tails of correlation functions for spinless fermions subjected to dephasing noise.

**Main result.** Quartic operators generically exhibit diffusive power-law decay tails, unlike bilinear operators which vanish exponentially, showing that hydrodynamic behavior depends on operator structure rather than just symmetry.

**Method.** The analysis uses the many-particle Lindblad equation, mapping it to an imaginary-time Schrödinger equation and analyzing the spectral properties of the dual Lindbladian eigenoperators.

**Model / system.** The system is spinless fermions hopping on a ring lattice governed by a tight-binding Hamiltonian, whose evolution is described by a many-particle Lindblad equation incorporating dephasing noise ($\gamma$).

**Key observables.** Time evolution of Heisenberg-picture operators, specifically quadratic and quartic correlation functions $\langle c^\dagger_{x}c_{y} angle$ and higher order terms.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dephasing rate ($\gamma$), hopping amplitude ($J$), and the asymptotic time limit ($t \gg \gamma^{-1}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on decoupling approximations (like BBGKY hierarchy) and assumes that late-time behavior is governed by eigenstates with real part of energy near zero.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes detail deriving asymptotic expressions for correlation functions $\Psi^{(0)}_{\{d,d,0\}}(x, t)$ showing power-law decay.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the governing Lindblad dynamics, analyzing quadratic operators first to show their behavior, and then extending this rigorous analysis to quartic (and higher) operators to prove the existence of non-vanishing hydrodynamic tails.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the non-equilibrium dynamics of spinless fermions with dephasing noise in the framework of a many-particle Lindblad equation. Using a mapping to the exactly solvable one-dimensional Hubbard model with purely imaginary tunneling amplitude we analyze the Heisenberg-picture dynamics of operators quartic in fermions and determine their hydrodynamic projections. We construct the relevant diffusive eigenoperators explicitly and show that, in the quartic sector, they can be interpreted as interacting pairs of bilinear hydrodynamic modes. As a consequence, translationally invariant quartic operators generically exhibit non-vanishing diffusive late-time tails, unlike translationally invariant bilinears. Our results show that the hydrodynamic tails of microscopic operators cannot in general be inferred from symmetry constraints or coarse-grained fluctuating hydrodynamics alone; they also depend crucially on the spatial structure and effective size of the hydrodynamic eigenoperators.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes the entanglement entropy in critical non-Hermitian free-fermion chains, finding that the scaling coefficient $\zeta_n$ is anomalous and depends continuously on system parameters. This anomaly is fundamentally linked to the nonorthogonality of eigenvectors at imaginary Dirac points within the energy spectrum. The findings establish a general framework for understanding entanglement in complex, non-Hermitian quantum critical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful generalization of entanglement measures beyond standard Hermitian critical theories (like CFT), showing how topological/non-Hermitian features dictate universal scaling laws in quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates anomalous entanglement scaling in critical non-Hermitian free-fermion steady states, linking this anomaly to eigenvector nonorthogonality.

**Main result.** The entanglement entropy scales logarithmically with subsystem size, $S_n(\ell) = \zeta_n(\lambda) \ln d_\ell + O(1)$, where the coefficient $\zeta_n$ forms a continuous family not predicted by standard central charges.

**Method.** Analytical derivations using low-energy expansions and correlation matrix analysis are combined with numerical calculations on 1D models to determine the scaling coefficients.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the non-Hermitian Su–Schrieffer–Heeger (SSH) chain, analyzed in its critical steady state $|\Psi_Sangle$. The physics is governed by non-Hermiticity and band crossings at imaginary Dirac points.

**Key observables.** Entanglement entropy ($S_n$), the logarithmic scaling coefficient ($\zeta_n$), and the overlap angle ($\sin 	heta$) quantifying eigenvector nonorthogonality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system parameters $\lambda$ (e.g., hopping ratios $t_1, t_2$) control the continuous variation of $\zeta_n$; weak real onsite disorder ($W$) is also a key parameter tested.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the applicability of logarithmic scaling for entanglement entropy and focuses on gapless critical regimes defined by imaginary energy crossings.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures (showing imaginary Dirac points), numerical comparisons of $S_n$ vs. distance, and plots showing $\zeta_n$ dependence on system parameters and disorder strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the steady state and entanglement entropy in non-Hermitian systems to deriving analytical expressions for $\zeta_n$ based on eigenvector nonorthogonality at imaginary Dirac points. It then verifies these results using numerical simulations across various critical structures, including checks for boundary condition dependence.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement carries universal content that labels phases and critical points. We study the entanglement entropy of the steady states of critical non-Hermitian free-fermion chains. It scales logarithmically with subsystem size, but the coefficients vary continuously with the parameters and form a Rényi family that no single central charge can reproduce. We trace this anomaly to an ``imaginary'' Dirac point, a crossing in the imaginary part of the energy where the occupied state switches between two Bloch states. Their nonorthogonality weakens the occupation discontinuity and lowers the logarithmic coefficient. A low-energy expansion yields closed-form coefficients in excellent agreement with lattice numerics in various one-dimensional critical steady states. Remarkably, weak real onsite disorder leaves this logarithmic scaling intact and enhances the entanglement. Our results provide a generic understanding of entanglement in critical non-Hermitian free-fermion steady states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an MPC-inspired hybrid quantum algorithm that merges the optimization strengths of VQAs with the feedback nature of FQAs for quantum computation. It uses a receding horizon approach, where classical simulation predicts future state evolution to optimize gate parameters layer by layer. The method successfully demonstrates superior or comparable performance to existing algorithms on key optimization models.

**Why it may be interesting.** For AMO/open systems researchers, this work provides a novel framework for controlling complex quantum dynamics by integrating predictive control theory. The focus on optimizing time evolution paths ($\langle H_p angle$ minimization) using feedback mechanisms is highly relevant to open system control.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for an advanced quantum algorithm that surpasses the limitations of existing Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs) and Feedback-Based Quantum Algorithms (FQAs). It proposes a Model Predictive Control (MPC)-inspired hybrid approach to optimize quantum circuit design.

**Main result.** The MPC-based algorithm is mathematically proven to match or exceed the performance of FQAs, and simulations show it can provide improved practical results for optimization tasks like Max-Cut and TFIM.

**Method.** It employs a layer-wise receding horizon strategy, using classical simulation initialized by observable measurements after each quantum circuit layer to predict future state evolution and optimize subsequent gate parameters.

**Model / system.** The algorithm is applied to solving optimization problems (e.g., Max-Cut) defined by a problem Hamiltonian ($H_p$). The underlying dynamics are governed by time evolution under a total Hamiltonian $H(t) = H_p + H_d eta(t)$.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the expectation value of the problem Hamiltonian, $\langle H_p angle$, which the algorithm seeks to minimize.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the prediction horizon ($N$), the time step ($\Delta t$), and the gate control parameters ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation is the computational intractability of the full optimization problem due to the need for state vector simulation, necessitating reduced-order models.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $\langle H_p angle$ trajectories comparing MPC and FALQON on Max-Cut and TFIM; Figure 13 specifically shows the prediction vs. actual evolution using the receding horizon policy.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews background (FALQON, MPC), details the quantum algorithm construction using MPC concepts, discusses necessary computational simplifications via reduced-order models, and concludes with numerical simulations on benchmark problems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a new hybrid quantum-classical algorithm inspired by an advanced control strategy known as model predictive control (MPC). This algorithm unifies the optimization-based design of variational quantum algorithms (VQAs) with the feedback-based design of feedback-based quantum algorithms (FQAs). Variational circuit parameters are optimized using a layer-wise receding horizon strategy, where observable measurements after every layer initialize a classically simulated dynamic model used to predict quantum state evolution and optimize over future parameterized gates. This hybrid algorithm can be used for applications such as ground state preparation and approximate combinatorial optimization, and presents an ideal use case for the integration of quantum computers with high-performance computing, where the latter resource can be used to increase the scale and efficiency of the predictions critical to MPC. We show through mathematical proof and numerical evidence that the MPC-based algorithm can be guaranteed to at least match the performance of FQAs. Through simulations on Max-Cut problems and a two-dimensional transverse-field Ising model, we demonstrate that relaxed implementations of the MPC-based algorithm can also provide improved performance in practice compared to an FQA.

</details>

### [Don't truncate, decompose: mean-field dynamics of long-range quantum systems from strongly correlated states](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25434v1)

**Authors:** Federico Carollo  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.25434v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **4/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `scars & prethermalization` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper reclaims the validity of mean-field theory for long-range open quantum systems by proposing a decomposition method. It shows that complex dynamics originating from strongly correlated superpositions can be exactly reconstructed from independent, uncoupled mean-field evolutions in the thermodynamic limit. This provides a powerful and transparent framework superior to truncated cumulant expansions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical tool to handle non-trivial quantum correlations in open systems where standard approximations fail, offering an exact framework that bridges the gap between mean-field theory and full many-body dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper challenges the consensus that mean-field theory fails for long-range open quantum systems, especially when starting from strongly correlated states or undergoing symmetry breaking.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that the evolution of such systems can be fully reconstructed from independent mean-field dynamics, establishing a framework where mean-field theory is exact in the thermodynamic limit.

**Method.** They propose a decomposition method that reconstructs the full quantum evolution by treating it as an ensemble average over individual mean-field evolutions corresponding to the components of a superposition state.

**Model / system.** The analysis applies to general long-range interacting quantum systems governed by a quantum master equation, using models like the open LMG model and time crystals. The dynamics are analyzed in the thermodynamic limit ($N 	o \infty$).

**Key observables.** Macroscopic observables (sample means $m^N_\mu$), moment generating functions, and various cumulants (second and third order).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The strong long-range regime, the thermodynamic limit ($N 	o \infty$), and the initial state structure (superposition vs. mixture).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that in the large-$N$ limit, quantum superpositions become indistinguishable from classical statistical mixtures, allowing for the decomposition.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the evolution of second and third cumulants for specific models (LMG, time-crystal), showing that the proposed decomposed mean-field method accurately reproduces exact dynamics where standard cumulant expansions fail or predict Gaussian approximations incorrectly.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the failure points of standard mean-field theory and cumulant expansions. It then introduces the decomposition framework, proving its equivalence to exact dynamics in the large-$N$ limit for specific state types (superpositions). Finally, it applies this method successfully to compute observables for complex physical models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We challenge the widespread consensus that mean-field theory fails to describe long-range open quantum systems in the presence of symmetry breaking and/or when starting from strongly correlated states (e.g., macroscopic superpositions). While recent literature relies on cumulant expansions to capture such systems, this approach rests on truncations with no clear justification. Here, we show that it is, at best, conceptually redundant in the strong long-range regime. We show that the evolution can be decomposed into, and fully reconstructed from, independent mean-field dynamics. This decomposition generates the entire hierarchy of cumulants and, as a byproduct, identifies---to our knowledge, for the first time---a regime in which cumulant expansions exactly predict low-order cumulants. We illustrate the power of our findings with two applications: we compute the moment generating function for nonequilibrium $\mathcal{Z}_2$ symmetry breaking, and construct states restoring time-translation symmetry in time crystals. In both cases, our method fully reproduces the exact many-body dynamics, which is out of reach of cumulant expansions. Our results reclaim the exactness of mean-field theory, offering a transparent framework for large-scale open quantum systems.

</details>
