**Summary.** This paper introduces an Invariant-based Master Equation framework to model the dynamics of driven, multi-level quantum systems like qutrits coupled to baths and cavities. It demonstrates that this new method provides a more general description than standard laboratory or rotating-frame approaches. By analyzing spectroscopic signatures, the authors show its ability to capture complex dissipative physics missed by conventional approximations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles the complex problem of non-Markovian or strongly driven open quantum systems by proposing a generalized theoretical tool (IME) that unifies several existing but limited master equation formalisms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a reliable theoretical model for externally driven quantum dynamics in open systems, overcoming fundamental issues with conventional master equation approaches.

**Main result.** The invariant-based master equation provides a unifying generalized framework that accurately captures dissipative dynamics in complex driven systems, yielding distinct predictions compared to standard laboratory or rotating-frame approximations.

**Method.** The authors employ the Invariant-based Master Equation (IME) framework, utilizing Lewis-Riesenfeld invariants derived from the isolated system Hamiltonian to model time evolution in open quantum systems.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on a driven qutrit ($N=3$ level system) coupled to an environment bath and potentially a leaky cavity. The dynamics are governed by a total Hamiltonian including time-dependent driving terms.

**Key observables.** Fluorescence spectrum $S_{12}(\omega)$, which is the Fourier transform of a first-order correlation function, used to diagnose dissipative behavior.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving strengths ($\Omega_p, \Omega_s$), detunings, and various decay/dephasing rates ($\Gamma_\perp, \Gamma_\parallel$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework generally uses the Born-Markov approximation; however, it is designed to handle regimes where conventional approximations fail.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the qutrit states and show spectral features (like modified Mollow triplets) resulting from different coupling regimes and driving strengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the IME framework as a generalization of standard master equations, applies it to a driven qutrit/cavity system, derives explicit forms for dissipation coefficients in various frames, and compares predictions using fluorescence spectra analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We employ a generalized approach to the master equation for driven open $N$-level ($N>2$) quantum systems using Lewis-Riesenfeld invariants, which avoids the driving-strength restrictions inherent to conventional approaches. We show that the invariant-based master equation provides a unifying generalized framework, which reduces to the frequently employed laboratory-frame master equations and the less frequently employed rotating-frame master equation framework under appropriate simplifications. Extending the prototypical two-level system, we show that the inclusion of another state coupled to the ground state via reservoir-induced dephasing gives rise to qualitatively new dissipative behaviors that are, in general, not captured by standard approximations. We also apply the invariant-based master equation framework to a driven quantum dot coupled to a leaky cavity, demonstrating the framework's ability to capture relevant dissipative dynamics without additional assumptions. Our work paves the way for quantum-control applications in the presence of dissipation.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors developed a novel spectroscopic technique using tip-height tuning within the boundary Renormalization Group framework to probe vortex cores in superconductors. They successfully used this method to distinguish between true Majorana zero modes and trivial resonances by observing that the characteristic flow of the ZBP contradicted MZM theory, proving the peak was a false positive.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful experimental methodology—using controlled environmental coupling (dissipative STM)—to perform dynamical quantum state verification, moving beyond simple spectral measurements to test fundamental topological protection in superconducting systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is distinguishing between true topological superconducting vortex states, which host Majorana zero modes (MZMs), and false positive zero-bias conductance peaks (ZBPs) arising from conventional vortex cores using STM.

**Main result.** By analyzing the dynamical evolution of the ZBP as the tip coupling is tuned via boundary renormalization group flow, the authors demonstrated that the observed peak was a non-topological feature whose flow contradicted MZM predictions.

**Method.** The study combines low-temperature scanning tunneling microscopy (STM) measurements with advanced theoretical modeling using boundary Renormalization Group (RG) theory and the Thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz (TBA) to analyze dissipative quantum circuits.

**Model / system.** The experiment is performed on $	ext{SrSn}_3$ thin films, treating the STM junction as a local probe coupled to an Ohmic environment. The physics is modeled using concepts from boundary RG flow applied to superconducting vortex cores and tunneling barriers.

**Key observables.** Zero-bias conductance peak (ZBP) evolution; dissipation strength ($r$); spectral weight fitting across single-channel, two-channel SC gap, and high-field regimes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipation strength $r < 1/2$; tip coupling parameter tuning the boundary RG flow; superconducting gap parameters ($\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the low-energy spectral evolution is governed by a universal dissipative environment response derived from TBA, allowing for the separation of trivial and topological contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup (STM junction in a dissipative environment) and show the critical diagnostic: how lowering the tip drives an initial ZBP into a zero-bias dip, which is contrasted with theoretical MZM flow predictions. Other figures detail global fits across various spectral components.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the ambiguity of static ZBPs, introduces the boundary RG/TBA framework to test dynamical protection, applies this method to $	ext{SrSn}_3$ vortex spectra, and concludes by showing that the observed flow pattern definitively rules out an MZM interpretation for the measured peak.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Clean, nonsplit vortex zero-bias peaks (ZBPs) can be misinterpreted as Majorana zero modes (MZMs), making static scanning tunneling microscopy intrinsically ambiguous. Here we use the STM tip coupling to drive a local boundary-renormalization-group (boundary RG) flow, turning dynamical Coulomb blockade into a falsification test for Majorana-like ZBPs. Experimentally, in a $\mathrm{SrSn}_3$ thin film, normal-state spectra establish an Ohmic dissipative environment, and a common boundary-RG/thermodynamic-Bethe-ansatz analysis of the superconducting-gap and vortex-center spectra yields consistent dissipation strengths within the $r < 1/2$ Majorana-filter regime. Lowering the tip nevertheless drives a clean, non-split vortex-center ZBP into a zero-bias dip, opposite to the protected flow of an isolated MZM, unmasking the peak as a Majorana false positive produced by a conventional vortex-core state. The same flow selectively suppresses the strongly tip-coupled channel, resolving the two-gap superconductivity. Dissipative STM thus tests dynamical protection rather than spectral appearance.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops an Effective Field Theory to explain operator scrambling using Out-of-Time-Ordered Correlators (OTOCs). By identifying an emergent strong U(1) symmetry in the doubled Hilbert space, the authors derive that the dynamics must follow a noisy FKPP equation. This provides a unified, symmetry-based understanding of how quantum information spreads and thermalizes in complex many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general theoretical tool—an EFT derived from symmetry principles—to describe non-equilibrium dynamics in strongly interacting quantum systems, which is highly relevant for understanding thermalization and chaos in many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to find a general symmetry principle that governs the effective dynamics of operator scrambling, which is diagnosed by Out-of-Time-Ordered Correlators (OTOCs). The challenge lies in developing an Effective Field Theory (EFT) for this process.

**Main result.** The OTOC dynamics are shown to be governed by a noisy FKPP equation, derived from an EFT built upon the spontaneous breaking of an emergent strong U(1) symmetry. This framework unifies descriptions of early-time exponential growth and late-time saturation in operator spreading.

**Method.** The authors develop a symmetry-based Effective Field Theory (EFT) by mapping the four-fold Keldysh contour representation of the OTOC onto a doubled Hilbert space, allowing for systematic analysis via slow modes and emergent symmetries.

**Model / system.** The theory is developed for Majorana systems (specifically Brownian or short-time-correlated large-N Majorana systems). A key test case used to verify the construction is the Brownian Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) chain.

**Key observables.** Out-of-Time-Ordered Correlators (OTOCs), operator size ($N[O(t)]$), and the resulting dynamics described by a noisy FKPP equation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis involves organizing terms based on strong-to-weak U(1) symmetry breaking, and key parameters include the Lyapunov exponent ($\lambda_L$) which is directly related to the noise strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on systematic expansions in powers of the slow mode field $\phi$, assuming consistency with Schwinger-Keldysh conditions, and imposing constraints like an emergent duality combining time reversal and contour permutation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a mathematical mapping from a four-fold Keldysh contour integral to a standard two-segment contour in a doubled Hilbert space, which is crucial for the EFT construction.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem using OTOCs and introduces the symmetry-based EFT framework. It then derives the effective action by exploiting emergent symmetries (U(1) breaking), leading to the noisy FKPP equation. Finally, it verifies this structure using a specific model like the Brownian SYK chain.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Operator scrambling is commonly diagnosed by the growth of out-of-time-ordered correlators (OTOCs), yet a general symmetry principle underlying their effective dynamics has remained elusive. For Brownian or short-time-correlated large-$N$ Majorana systems, we develop a symmetry-based effective field theory for operator scrambling, organized by a strong-to-weak U(1) symmetry breaking in operator space. The key observation is that, in the noninteracting fermion limit, the four-fold Keldysh contour representation of an OTOC admits an emergent strong U(1) symmetry in a doubled Hilbert-space description, even when the original system has no ordinary conserved quantity. The associated slow mode is the phase of the strong-charge creation operator, whose conjugate density is identified with the local operator size. Generic interactions explicitly break the strong symmetry and generate a mass term at lowest order for the would-be Goldstone mode, thereby converting diffusive operator spreading into chaotic growth. We further show that higher-order symmetry breaking terms are tightly constrained by an emergent duality that combines time reversal with contour permutation. This duality fixes the effective action up to quadratic order in the response field, relates the multiplicative noise strength directly to the Lyapunov exponent, and makes the positivity of the Lyapunov exponent a consequence of convergence of the real-time path integral. The resulting OTOC dynamics is governed by a noisy FKPP equation, which captures within a unified framework the early-time exponential growth, ballistic propagation, nonlinear saturation, and stochastic front broadening of operator scrambling. We verify this construction in a Brownian SYK chain, where a direct saddle-point expansion reproduces the symmetry-based effective action. Our results reveal a symmetry origin of operator-size hydrodynamics and scrambling.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates using bulk germanium as a superior material for spin-cQED qubits compared to strained heterostructures. By modeling the system's complex spin physics using the Luttinger–Kohn Hamiltonian, the authors show that hole spins in bulk Ge can achieve strong coupling ($g_s \gtrsim 100$ MHz) with microwave resonators. This makes bulk germanium a highly promising and scalable platform for quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses solid-state quantum computing hardware by demonstrating a high-performance qubit platform (Ge hole spins) capable of strong interaction with superconducting cavities, which is central to circuit QED architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is achieving strong spin-photon coupling ($g_s$) for scalable solid-state quantum computation using hole spins in semiconductor double quantum dots coupled to microwave resonators (spin-cQED).

**Main result.** Unstrained bulk germanium is established as a compelling platform, showing that hole spins can reach strong coupling regimes with superconducting resonators, achieving $g_s/2\pi \gtrsim 100$ MHz.

**Method.** The analysis involves systematic modeling using the Luttinger–Kohn Hamiltonian to derive an effective spin-photon Hamiltonian and calculate coupling strengths by diagonalizing these models.

**Model / system.** The system uses hole spins in double quantum dots (DQD) within bulk germanium, compared against strained heterostructures. The physics is governed by the LK Hamiltonian, which accounts for strong spin-orbit interactions mixing heavy-hole and light-hole states.

**Key observables.** Spin-photon coupling strength ($g_s/2\pi$), spin-flip tunnel matrix element ($t_{sf}/	ext{2}\pi$), and sensitivity to magnetic field orientation ($\mathbf{B}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Achievable $g_s \gtrsim 100$ MHz; the enhancement originates from SO interactions beyond the perturbative regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes operating at zero detuning ($\epsilon = 0$) and relies on deriving an effective Hamiltonian by treating strong spin-orbit coupling effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate device setups comparing strained QWs to unstrained bulk structures, showing the mechanism of spin-photon coupling via hybridization, and detailing $g_s$ dependence on $\mathbf{B}$ orientation for both platforms.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically models the system using the LK Hamiltonian, derives an effective spin-photon Hamiltonian, compares results between strained and bulk Ge structures, and concludes by establishing bulk Ge as a superior platform due to higher coupling strengths and reduced field dependence.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Unstrained bulk germanium is a particularly attractive material for circuit quantum electrodynamics with spins (spin-cQED). We show, through systematic modeling and comparison with state-of-the-art strained germanium heterostructures, that hole spins in bulk germanium double quantum dots readily reach the strong-coupling regime with superconducting microwave resonators, achieving spin-photon coupling strengths $g_s/2π\gtrsim100$\,MHz. This enhancement originates from large spin-orbit interactions beyond the perturbative regime. In addition, the coupling is much less sensitive to the orientation of the applied magnetic field, which shall ease operation and limit the impact of device-to-device variability. Our results establish bulk germanium as a compelling platform for scalable spin-cQED.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes that internal time in quantum mechanics arises from the accumulation of 'records' formed during actualization events. The duration assigned to an outcome is quantified by its surprisal ($-\ln p$), leading to a pathwise clock whose average value corresponds to the Shannon entropy. It offers a rigorous, information-theoretic foundation for understanding time without external parameters.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep theoretical link between fundamental information theory (entropy/surprisal) and the emergence of time in quantum mechanics, which is highly relevant to foundational studies in open systems and gravity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a record-based account of internal time in quantum mechanics, aiming to define temporal order and duration without relying on external time parameters.

**Main result.** Internal time is identified with the accumulated surprisal ($-\ln p$) associated with actualized outcomes, leading to an ensemble mean clock proportional to the Shannon entropy of the process.

**Method.** The framework uses conditioning on 'records' (actualized information) and derives the temporal measure by imposing consistency requirements on how duration composes additively under sequential quantum measurements.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum states undergoing sequential measurement contexts, where physical history is represented by an accumulating, nested set of stable records ($\Sigma_n$).

**Key observables.** Surprisal ($-\ln p$), Shannon Entropy ($H$), Rényi entropy spectrum, and the pathwise clock $	au(h_n)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The constant $\sigma$ (scaling factor between information gain and time units) and the distinguishability threshold $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include that duration depends only on conditional Born probability, that records persist to form a nested history, and that actualization is equivalent to conditioning on this record.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining stable records, deriving the pathwise clock proportional to surprisal, establishing the ensemble mean via Shannon entropy, and concluding with mathematical characterizations like Doob decomposition and additivity conditions for multiple clocks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a record-based account of internal time in quantum mechanics, where the formation of a stable record is represented as conditioning on actualized information, and along a history the accumulated record algebras are ordered by inclusion. If the duration of a realized outcome depends only on its conditional Born probability, composes additively under sequential conditioning, and is continuous and calibrated, then the actualization of each outcome contributes an internal duration equal to its surprisal, the negative logarithm of that probability, so that a certain outcome contributes no duration, whereas less likely outcomes contribute larger increments. The ensemble mean of the accumulated clock is the Shannon entropy of the record process, its moment-generating function is fixed by the Rényi entropy spectrum, and the realized clock admits a Doob decomposition into a predictable entropic compensator and a martingale of clock fluctuations, so that each increment is the information gain of the corresponding actualization. Records are characterized by graded criteria of distinguishability, decoherence, and stability. We also clarify the multiple-clock problem: in one fixed context, additivity of two surprisal clocks is equivalent to factorization of the Born distribution in that context, whereas for a pure bipartite state, additivity in every pair of local contexts is equivalent to rank-one factorization of the joint state and to the vanishing of all its $2\times2$ minors.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes how spatial variations in interactions within an XXZ spin chain lead to asymmetric information scrambling, quantified using OTOCs. By combining ETH theory with numerical calculations, the authors show that gradients suppress scrambling on strongly coupled sides and provide an analytical explanation for this asymmetry. This establishes deterministic inhomogeneity as a powerful tool for controlling quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, microscopic mechanism—deterministic gradients—to control quantum information dynamics (scrambling) in lattice models, which is highly relevant for understanding engineered quantum simulators.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how deterministic spatial inhomogeneity affects information scrambling dynamics and eigenstate thermalization within quantum many-body systems.

**Main result.** The interaction gradient induces a pronounced left–right asymmetry in information scrambling, causing operators on strongly interacting sides to exhibit suppressed scrambling compared to weakly interacting sides.

**Method.** The authors use Out-of-Time-Ordered Correlators (OTOCs) and analyze diagonal matrix elements within the Eigenstate Thermalization Hypothesis (ETH) framework.

**Model / system.** The primary model is an inhomogeneous XXZ spin chain, characterized by a spatially varying interaction profile $Jz(i; \delta)$. The analysis also touches upon the inhomogeneous transverse-field Ising model (ITFIM).

**Key observables.** Out-of-Time-Ordered Correlators (OTOCs), spectral statistics ($\langle r angle$), and diagonal matrix elements of local operators.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interaction gradient strength $\delta$ is the key parameter, controlling the crossover from integrable ($\delta=0$) to chaotic regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the infinite-temperature regime and utilizes the ETH framework to connect dynamics to energy eigenbasis properties.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of spectral statistics ($\langle r angle$ vs. $\delta$), showing crossovers between Poisson, GOE, and nonergodic regimes; they also show OTOC dynamics revealing left-right asymmetry in scrambling.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes how spatial inhomogeneity modifies information scrambling by calculating OTOCs and applying ETH diagnostics to determine the finite-size saturation value of these correlators.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Deterministic spatial inhomogeneity has become increasingly relevant in experimentally engineered quantum many-body systems, where interaction gradients can strongly influence nonequilibrium dynamics. Motivated by this, we investigate out-of-time-ordered correlators (OTOCs) and their connection to the eigenstate thermalization hypothesis (ETH) in inhomogeneous XXZ spin chains. Using a deterministic spatially varying interaction profile, we show that finite interaction gradients ($δ>0$) induce a pronounced left--right asymmetry in information scrambling, as quantified by OTOCs. This asymmetry persists even when the system exhibits spectral signatures of quantum chaos, with operators on the strongly interacting side exhibiting suppressed scrambling. To elucidate the origin of the asymmetric finite-size long-time saturation value of OTOCs, we employ two complementary approaches. First, we analyze the diagonal matrix elements of the OTOC observables in the energy eigenbasis within the ETH framework. Second, we derive an analytical expression for the finite-size saturation value based on the overlap between the Hamiltonian and the OTOC observables, which explicitly incorporates the spatial interaction profile. The analytical prediction is fully consistent with the numerical results and provides a microscopic explanation for how deterministic interaction gradients generate the observed asymmetry in the long-time saturation of OTOCs.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes that the power-law decay observed during structural glass relaxation is fundamentally due to an effective stiffening potential arising from particle confinement, rather than previously hypothesized mechanisms. By modeling this effect with a minimal cage model, the authors provide a complete physical picture of gradient descent dynamics in these disordered systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical glassy dynamics, the concept of energy landscape features (inflection points) governing slow relaxation is highly relevant to understanding quantum tunneling rates or quasi-adiabatic evolution in complex quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the poorly understood phenomenon of slow energy relaxation, characterized by power-law decay, observed in quenched structural glasses.

**Main result.** The power-law behavior is shown to emerge from a simple effective stiffening caging potential arising from many-body confinement, rendering complex mechanisms like saddle-point slowdown unnecessary.

**Method.** The authors use gradient descent simulations on various glass models and derive analytical predictions based on a phenomenological cage potential.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes structural glasses using models like the KALJ model and amorphous silica, focusing on dynamics governed by overdamped Gradient Descent (GD) in the energy landscape.

**Key observables.** Power-law decay exponents ($\alpha$ or $eta$) for energy/velocity relaxation ($E(t) \sim t^{-\alpha}$), cage size ($x_c$), and fluctuations around inflection points.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The exponent $eta = (\gamma + 1)/(\gamma + 2)$ derived from the stiffening potential; temperature regimes relative to $T_{	ext{onset}}$ or $T_{	ext{def}}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are dominated by descent within individual cages, allowing a minimal single-particle cage model to capture the essential physics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate GD trajectories showing energy landscape features like 'speed bumps' at inflection points; one figure compares mean-field spin glass predictions with structural glass behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper first reviews existing theories for slow relaxation, then simulates dynamics in structural glasses to rule out these mechanisms. It proposes the caging potential as the source of power-law decay and validates this using a minimal cage model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The slow energy relaxation in quenched glasses is a ubiquitous yet poorly understood phenomenon. Despite extensive study, the microscopic origin of the observed power-law decay remains debated, with proposed mechanisms ranging from saddle-point slowdown and marginal stability to coarsening of localized excitations and phonon dynamics. Here, by simulating gradient descent in archetypal structural glass formers, we show that none of these scenarios can account for our data. Instead, the power-law behavior emerges from a remarkably simple caging effect: each particle experiences an effective stiffening potential that arises from many-body confinement and diverges at a characteristic cage size. This mechanism analytically yields the observed power-law decay and is quantitatively reproduced by a minimal single-particle cage model with fixed neighbours, demonstrating that collective relaxation modes are not essential. The dynamics is punctuated by fluctuations as the system rolls through inflection points on the energy landscape, which act as `speed bumps' but do not affect the overall power-law behaviour. In contrast to mean-field spin glass theory, we find no characteristic temperature that separates distinct dynamical regimes; state following within a given glass basin occurs universally for all initial temperatures whenever the system is sufficiently close to the inherent structure. Our results establish a complete physical picture of gradient descent dynamics in typical structural glasses.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors show that inducing topological phases (QSH and QVH) in photonic crystals does not require breaking spatial symmetry globally. By introducing controlled, local random substitutional disorder into a photonic alloy, they successfully open topological gaps, enabling flexible, on-demand engineering of edge states using only doping tuning.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful paradigm shift, showing that engineered disorder can induce complex quantum phenomena like topological edge states without requiring perfect global structural control, which is highly relevant for designing robust photonic devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Conventional methods for inducing topological photonic phases like QSH/QVH require global symmetry breaking, which severely limits device reconfigurability.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that local and minimal symmetry breaking via random substitutional disorder is sufficient to open topological gaps and trigger robust helical edge states and valley kink states.

**Method.** Topological characterization is performed using the reflection-phase winding method, analyzing band structures derived from photonic crystal models.

**Model / system.** The system is a photonic alloy platform based on random substitutional disorder in a parallel-plate waveguide built on a honeycomb lattice structure.

**Key observables.** Topological phases (QSH/QVH), Spin Chern Number ($C_s$), reflection phase winding, and bulk transmission gaps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping concentration ($x$), system size (W), and the threshold doping concentration ($x_{th}$) which vanishes in the thermodynamic limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that local disorder can effectively mimic global symmetry breaking mechanisms necessary for topological transitions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of unit cells, band structures showing Dirac points, and phase plots ($\varphi$ vs. $\phi_y$) confirming full winding numbers corresponding to non-trivial topology.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the limitation of global symmetry breaking, then demonstrating QSH via local $z$-mirror symmetry breaking (doping), followed by extending the concept to QVH via in-plane inversion symmetry breaking, consistently using topological invariants for characterization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conventional approaches generate quantum spin Hall (QSH) and quantum valley Hall (QVH) effects by breaking spatial inversion symmetry across the entire photonic crystal. Consequently, modulating the bulk transmission gaps within these periodic frameworks typically requires global modification of structural parameters throughout the lattice, severely limiting device reconfigurability and post-fabrication adaptability. In this work, we demonstrate that global inversion symmetry breaking is not a prerequisite for inducing topological phases. Instead, local and minimal symmetry breaking is sufficient. Using a photonic alloy platform based on random substitutional disorder in a parallel-plate waveguide, we show that a disorder-driven local breaking of z-direction mirror symmetry and a local breaking of in-plane symmetry are capable of opening topological gaps to trigger QSH and QVH phase transitions, respectively, thereby supporting robust helical edge states and valley kink states. Rigorous topological characterization via the reflection-phase winding method reveals a striking size-dependent scaling behavior in both configurations, where the threshold doping concentration required to trigger the topological phase transition asymptotically vanishes in the thermodynamic limit. By enabling flexible, on-demand bulk gap engineering simply through random doping tuning rather than global structural reconfigurations, this photonic alloy platform not only deepens understanding of disorder induced topological physics but also offers a scalable, highly efficient design strategy for integrated topological optical communications.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a general auxiliary-state framework to numerically solve the Non-Markovian Quantum State Diffusion (NMQSD) equation. It analytically decomposes the dynamics into three fundamental structures and develops convergent first- and second-order time-stepping schemes. The resulting methods offer robust tools for simulating open quantum systems with arbitrary memory environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly general and mathematically rigorous computational tool for simulating complex open quantum systems where memory effects are crucial, overcoming limitations of previous specialized solvers.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a general numerical framework to solve the Non-Markovian Quantum State Diffusion (NMQSD) equation, which is complicated by functional derivatives with respect to the stochastic process.

**Main result.** The authors construct and analyze first- and second-order auxiliary-state schemes that provide rigorous error bounds ($\mathcal{O}(	au)$ or $\mathcal{O}(	au^2)$) for approximating the non-Markovian dynamics.

**Method.** They derive an analytical solution structure revealing three elementary dynamic components (propagation, insertion, pairing) and implement this using a time-discretization auxiliary-state approach.

**Model / system.** The system involves open quantum systems whose reduced density matrix evolution is modeled by the linear NMQSD equation. This formalism describes dynamics influenced by an environment with arbitrary bath correlation functions $\alpha(t, s)$.

**Key observables.** Error bounds for the physical state approximation ($\|\psi_{\emptyset}^n - \phi_{\emptyset}^n\|$), and expectation values like $\langle\sigma_xangle_t$, $\langle\sigma_yangle_t$, $\langle\sigma_zangle_t$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time step ($	au$) and total time ($T$); the structure depends on the bath correlation function $\alpha(t, s)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on pathwise boundedness of coefficients and uniform integrability of the memory kernel; the first-order scheme explicitly drops contributions from repeated time labels.

**Figures summary.** Tables summarize the correspondence between NMQSD forms and diagrammatic notations for Propagation, Pairing, and Insertion. Diagrams illustrate the three elementary transitions in the first-order scheme.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by deriving an analytical solution structure, decomposing it into three parts, constructing auxiliary-state schemes (first and second order), and finally providing rigorous error analysis proving convergence rates ($\mathcal{O}(	au)$ and $\mathcal{O}(	au^2)$) for these numerical methods.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The numerical simulation of non-Markovian open quantum systems based on the non-Markovian quantum state diffusion (NMQSD) equation is complicated by functional derivatives with respect to the stochastic process. A general numerical framework that directly treats these functional derivatives without relying on prescribed decompositions of the bath correlation function is still lacking. In this work, we derive an analytical solution of the linear NMQSD equation that reveals three elementary structures of the non-Markovian stochastic dynamics: stochastic propagation, functional-derivative insertion, and memory pairing. Based on this structure, we construct a general auxiliary-state framework for arbitrary bath correlation functions. The framework separates the numerical construction into time discretization, memory quadrature, and hierarchy truncation. We then construct first- and second-order schemes and provide diagrammatic transition rules for their explicit implementation. Numerical results verify the expected temporal accuracy and demonstrate the applicability of the proposed methods to different bath correlation functions and multi-level quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an agentic AI framework that allows autonomous execution of complex quantum sensing experiments using NV centers. The system uses LLMs to hypothesize next steps and interpret data from measurements like pODMR scans, successfully designing follow-up tests (like CPMG) based on initial weak signals. It establishes a clear division of labor where the AI handles scientific reasoning while deterministic code manages hardware safety.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a significant step toward closing the loop in quantum experiments by integrating advanced AI reasoning directly into the experimental control cycle, which is highly relevant for complex open quantum systems characterization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing autonomous experimental workflows for quantum sensing that can handle complex decision-making, data interpretation, and hypothesis generation beyond routine hardware control.

**Main result.** The agent successfully performed an end-to-end workflow—from initial measurement to autonomously designing a follow-up CPMG scan—and benchmarks show that the division of labor between LLM reasoning and deterministic code is crucial for success.

**Method.** An agentic AI workflow built around a Large Language Model (LLM) guides autonomous experiments, combining persistent state tracking with quantitative tools to interpret complex quantum measurement data.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) centers in diamond, used for sensing magnetic and electric fields via pulsed optically detected magnetic resonance (pODMR) and Ramsey measurements. The AI component uses LLMs (e.g., GPT variants) to reason over the experimental process.

**Key observables.** T2* decay time, resonance frequencies, signal depth in pODMR data, and detection of weak frequency components related to nearby ${}^{13}	ext{C}$ coupling.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Reasoning effort levels (Low to X-high), contrast depth thresholds ($\geq 0.132$), and specific microwave/Larmor frequencies (e.g., $3.87650 	ext{ GHz}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The agent's ability to form hypotheses is tested against deterministic hardware control, but the use of specific thresholds in retrospective analysis may not generalize.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the sequence of autonomous measurements (Ramsey $ightarrow$ CPMG) and benchmark comparisons showing model performance across varying reasoning efforts for both Ramsey checkpoint tasks and pODMR data evaluation.

**Paper structure.** The paper details an autonomous workflow on NV centers, presents case studies demonstrating hypothesis-driven measurement design (e.g., adding CPMG), and rigorously evaluates the LLM's reasoning capabilities using two distinct offline benchmarks (Ramsey checkpoint and pODMR).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We implement an agentic AI workflow built around a large language model (LLM) agent for autonomous experiments with nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in diamond. NV centers are a widely used platform for quantum sensing, and the ability to control many measurements from a computer makes NV experiments a natural setting for autonomous workflows. We make two main contributions. First, we demonstrate an autonomous NV experiment workflow that combines persistent project records, quantitative calculation and data analysis tools, and deterministic experiment control. In one autonomous experiment, the agent selected a single NV center, calibrated its resonant frequency, measured \(T_2^\ast\) with Ramsey measurements, and added a Carr--Purcell--Meiboom--Gill (CPMG) measurement to check a weak feature that could be related to nearby \(^{13}\mathrm{C}\). Second, we introduce two offline benchmarks that evaluate the agent's reasoning separately from laboratory execution. We evaluated both benchmarks with GPT-5.4, GPT-5.5, and GPT-5.6 Sol. In the Ramsey checkpoint benchmark, greater reasoning effort generally improved recognition of a residual resonance calibration offset. By contrast, in the pulsed optically detected magnetic resonance (pODMR) data evaluation benchmark, pulse sequence information alone produced more false positive resonance judgments at higher reasoning effort. Requiring an expected signal calculation kept false positive rates low across all three models and reasoning settings. The results suggest a clear division of labor for autonomous experiments. The agent forms scientific hypotheses and uses quantitative tools to evaluate data, while deterministic code controls the hardware and enforces safety constraints.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies concepts from statistical physics to model the Mafia game using an agent-based simulation. It analyzes how accumulated suspicion drives the system toward one of two absorbing states: mafia elimination or numerical parity. The findings reveal universal scaling laws when information is imperfect, but these laws break down significantly when perfect information (detectives) is introduced.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly quantum, the study of absorbing states, critical phenomena (data collapse), and non-equilibrium dynamics in complex interacting systems provides strong analogies to phase transitions studied in statistical mechanics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates collective decision-making under concealed affiliations, modeling this using the Mafia game to study history-dependent stochastic processes.

**Main result.** It finds that in the absence of perfect information (detectives), winning probabilities exhibit a universal data collapse characterized by a population scale $N_c$.

**Method.** The analysis relies on extensive Monte Carlo simulations and mean-field approximations to track absorption statistics over time.

**Model / system.** The system is an agent-based model of the Mafia game, where agents accumulate public suspicion scores based on pairwise interactions determined by their hidden roles (Mafia, Detective, Civilian).

**Key observables.** Mafia extinction times, winning probabilities for both factions, and the power-law exponent ($\alpha$) governing early-time survival statistics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Population size ($N$), initial mafia size ($N_m$), presence/number of detectives ($N_d$), and time ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model provides a minimal stochastic framework, simplifying complex social interactions into quantifiable suspicion updates; the early-time dynamics are approximated by mean-field theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate data collapse for $N_d=0$ when scaling by $N_c$, and show the failure of this collapse when detectives are present, indicating a change in critical behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from defining the game's conflict structure to detailing the agent-based rules for suspicion accumulation, culminating in simulations that test how history-dependence affects macroscopic absorption statistics compared to simpler models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Mafia game, also known as Werewolf, describes a competition between a coordinated minority whose identities are concealed and a larger good faction composed primarily of uninformed civilians attempting to identify and eliminate them. We introduce an agent-based formulation in which the daytime decision is not represented by uniform random voting. Instead, randomly paired agents modify player-specific public suspicion scores according to their roles, and one surviving player is subsequently eliminated with probability proportional to their accumulated suspicion. These scores persist throughout the game, producing a history-dependent stochastic process with two competing absorbing outcomes: elimination of the mafia or numerical parity between the mafia and the good faction. We investigate the effects of population size, initial mafia size, and the presence of perfectly informed detectives through extensive Monte Carlo simulations. In the absence of detectives, a random-execution approximation predicts that, in the dilute-mafia and early-time regime, the cumulative probability of mafia extinction behaves as $F(τ)\sim (τ/N)^{N_m}$, in agreement with simulations as $N_m/N$ decreases. The winning-probability curves also exhibit an empirical finite-size crossover characterized by a population scale $N_c$. Rescaling the population by $N_c$ approximately collapses the curves obtained for different initial mafia populations. Perfectly informed detectives substantially shorten mafia-survival times and introduce an additional dependence on population composition for which the same one-parameter rescaling is insufficient. The model provides a minimal connection between microscopic histories of accusation and the macroscopic absorption statistics of hidden-role games.

</details>

### [Efficient Lindbladian Learning from Constant-Time Pauli Responses](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25795v1)

**Authors:** Jiaxing Song, Yukun Zhang, Xiao Yuan, Yusen Wu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.25795v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `methods for driven-dissipative` **4/5** · `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `QC/QI experiment` **2/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

<sub>FIG. 1. Overview of the Lindbladian learning framework. (a) Randomized product-Pauli experiments estimate finite-time raw Pauli response functionals. (b) Response normalization removes the intrinsic mixing between coherent and dissipative generator terms. At first order in the evolution time, the response matrix G maps Lindbladian coefficients to the slopes of these raw responses at t = 0. Applying H = G−1 removes coherent–dissipative and common- extension mixing and produces normalized responses whose derivatives at the origin equal...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an advanced framework to learn the generator (Lindbladian) of complex open quantum systems using only short-time local Pauli response measurements. It resolves the inherent difficulty of separating coherent and dissipative dynamics by treating responses as a linear system. The authors propose two efficient, theoretically bounded algorithms—Chebyshev interpolation and single-time contraction—to extract all necessary coupling coefficients from experimental data.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly systematic and theoretically grounded methodology for extracting fundamental system parameters from limited, noisy experimental data in open quantum systems. The explicit handling of the coherent-dissipative ambiguity is crucial for interpreting measurements in realistic physical platforms like superconducting circuits or trapped ions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is efficiently learning the generator (Lindbladian) of an open many-body quantum system, which is complicated by dissipative contributions mixed into local Pauli response measurements.

**Main result.** The authors develop and analyze two efficient algorithms—Chebyshev–Lobatto interpolation and single-time projected contraction—to separate coherent and dissipative terms and estimate all Lindbladian coefficients from short-time data with provable complexity bounds.

**Method.** The approach involves constructing a linear system from raw Pauli responses, inverting it to isolate the contribution of each generator term, and then applying advanced numerical interpolation or iterative projection techniques to extract the time-dependent coefficients.

**Model / system.** The study concerns open quantum systems governed by Lindbladian dynamics ($\mathcal{L}^\dagger_	heta$). Learning relies on measuring local Pauli responses from randomized product-Pauli experiments, which provide data related to the system's evolution operator $e^{\mathcal{L}^\dagger t}$.

**Key observables.** Local Pauli response functionals $	ext{Tr}[ho P(t)]$, normalized finite-time responses $g_\alpha(t, 	heta)$, and the unknown Lindbladian coefficients $	heta = (	heta_1, \dots, 	heta_M)^T$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Target accuracy ($\epsilon$), number of candidate terms ($M$), evolution time window ($t$), and system locality scales ($\Lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dictionary of candidate Lindbladian terms must be known; the dissipative part must satisfy bounded locality properties (e.g., bounded dual-interaction-graph degree).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a three-stage process: (a) measuring raw responses via Pauli experiments, (b) normalizing these responses by inverting a response matrix ($H=G^{-1}$) to separate coherent/dissipative mixing, and (c) reconstructing the coefficients using interpolation or single-time inversion.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical problem of Lindbladian learning from local responses. It then details the mathematical framework involving response matrices ($G$ and $H$) to resolve ambiguities. Finally, it presents two distinct algorithmic approaches (Chebyshev interpolation and single-time contraction) alongside rigorous complexity analyses for both measurement shots and classical post-processing.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Learning the generator of an open many-body system is more challenging than Hamiltonian learning: local responses, which can directly reveal coherent interaction terms in closed-system dynamics, may also contain dissipative contributions in open-system dynamics. In this paper, we address this challenge by developing an efficient Lindbladian learning framework for a known local candidate generator dictionary with bounded dissipative support and either bounded dual-interaction-graph degree or bounded unweighted local strength. The framework resolves the coherent-dissipative ambiguity by treating local Pauli responses as a linear system over both types of generator terms. Inverting this response system separates their contributions and makes the individual Lindbladian coefficients accessible from local response data in a fixed short-time window. Within this framework, we develop two efficient learning algorithms: Chebyshev--Lobatto response interpolation, which uses logarithmically many short evolution times and has a post-mean cost linear in $M$, with the stated dependence on $ε$, and Single-time projected response contraction, which uses a single fixed evolution time and globally inverts a truncated response function. Both procedures estimate $M$ candidate coefficients to entrywise accuracy $ε$ using $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(M/ε^2)$ sample and classical post-processing complexity. Our theoretical results establish local response inversion as a scalable paradigm for learning, calibrating, and diagnosing complex quantum systems from experimentally accessible short-time data.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates how applying periodic external fields (Floquet driving) to a coupled qubit system can engineer a quantum thermal diode. By breaking the symmetry of coupling—specifically through single-side modulation—the authors achieve strong, controllable heat rectification. This establishes contact-selective Floquet dressing as a powerful design tool for managing energy flow in nanoscale quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium quantum thermodynamics by using time-periodic driving (Floquet engineering) to manipulate energy flow directionality in mesoscopic systems, which is highly relevant to open quantum system dynamics and thermal management.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central goal is controlling heat flow at the nanoscale by achieving strong thermal rectification without sacrificing the overall transmitted heat current, a known trade-off in static quantum diodes.

**Main result.** Contact-selective Floquet dressing provides a robust design principle to engineer controllable and tunable asymmetry in heat flow through minimal quantum thermal devices.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving and solving a Floquet–LGKS master equation from a microscopic system--bath model, comparing driven dynamics against static and undriven benchmarks.

**Model / system.** The system is a minimal quantum thermal diode composed of two longitudinally modulated Ising-coupled qubits, each coupled independently to its own thermal reservoir. The dynamics are governed by time-dependent Hamiltonians and resolved via Floquet theory.

**Key observables.** Steady-state heat current ($J_{ss}$), thermal rectification coefficient ($R$), and the blocking condition (suppressing one bias direction while maintaining transport in the other).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The modulation amplitude ($\alpha$) is crucial, as rectification scales quadratically with it in the weak-drive regime; single-side vs. dual-side driving configurations are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system–bath coupling is assumed to be weak, and approximations like Born, Markov, and Floquet–secular methods are employed for solving the master equation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the setup: two qubits with modulated frequencies coupled by an Ising interaction, connected to independent thermal reservoirs ($T_L, T_R$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model and Hamiltonian; it derives the Floquet-LGKS master equation; it analyzes single-side driving to achieve rectification and blocking; finally, it extends the analysis to dual-side driving, showing how pumping contributions must cancel for complete blockade.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Controlling heat flow in small quantum systems is a central goal of quantum thermodynamics and nanoscale transport. A key challenge is to achieve strong thermal rectification without suppressing the transmitted heat current, a tradeoff that often arises in static diode configurations. We establish a Floquet-control mechanism in a minimal quantum thermal diode formed by two longitudinally modulated Ising-coupled qubits, each coupled to an independent thermal reservoir. From a microscopic system--bath model, we derive a Floquet--LGKS master equation that resolves the drive-assisted transition channels. The resonant undriven device with left--right symmetric bath couplings serves as the reciprocal benchmark, while static detuning provides a rectifying reference with reduced heat current. Single-side driving breaks this reciprocal structure by creating a Floquet-dressed contact opposite a purely thermal contact. For this configuration, we obtain a compact steady-state current formula and an exact blocking condition for suppressing one bias direction while retaining finite transport in the opposite direction. In the weak sinusoidal-drive regime, rectification begins quadratically in the modulation amplitude. Dual-side driving adds a Floquet pumping contribution to the interaction-mediated current, so complete blocking requires cancellation of both contributions. These results establish contact-selective Floquet dressing as a design principle for controllable heat-flow asymmetry in minimal quantum thermal devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an advanced theoretical framework combining Cluster Correlation Expansion with stochastic methods to model the long-term magnetic noise from nuclear spins affecting electron spin qubits. The authors validate this approach against experimental data and show that actively compensating for the measured Overhauser field can dramatically extend qubit coherence times, offering a pathway toward more robust quantum computation in solid-state systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a fundamental decoherence bottleneck in solid-state quantum computing platforms by providing a quantitative method to mitigate slow environmental noise sources through dynamic control.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is accurately predicting the slow drift of magnetic noise originating from the nuclear spin bath, which severely limits the coherence time ($T_2^*$) of electron spin qubits in semiconductor quantum dots.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that by actively compensating for the measured Overhauser field rotation ($\Delta t$), the effective coherence time $	ilde{T}_2^*$ can be significantly enhanced—potentially 10 to 100 times larger than the ergodic $T_2^*$.

**Method.** They extend the Cluster Correlation Expansion (CCE) technique by incorporating stochastic treatments of cluster dynamics, combining quantum evolution with classical rate matrices to simulate long-time noise behavior.

**Model / system.** The system involves electron spin qubits coupled to a bath of nuclear spins within semiconductor quantum dots. The Hamiltonian includes Zeeman, Fermi contact hyperfine ($H_{hf}$), and dipolar interactions ($\mathbf{D}$).

**Key observables.** $T_2^*$ decay time (Ramsey experiments), autocorrelation functions of the Overhauser field, and Power Spectral Densities (PSDs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The simulation relies on modeling periodic electron occupation cycles ($T = t_{	ext{on}} + t_{	ext{off}}$) and quantifying noise drift over long timescales ($\mu$s regime).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key approximations include neglecting fast electronic dynamics relative to slow nuclear bath evolution, assuming low temperatures where phonon decay is negligible, and using the CCE formalism.

**Figures summary.** Figures validate the method by showing convergence tests for model dimensions (Fig. 12) and comparing numerical histograms of nearest-neighbor distributions against analytical results (Fig. 14).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical noise source to extending the CCE technique for long timescales, validating the methodology against multiple experimental diagnostics ($T_2^*$, autocorrelation), demonstrating back-action effects, and finally quantifying the benefits of active field compensation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The dynamics of a nuclear spin bath generates magnetic noise that is a key contributor to the decoherence of electron spin qubits in electrostatically-defined quantum dots. In this paper, we extend the cluster correlation expansion (CCE) technique, which has proven useful for predicting solid-state qubit coherence times across various settings but is limited to shorter time scales, to incorporate stochastic treatments of cluster dynamics in order to efficiently predict slow drifting Overhauser fields over longer time scales. This approach combines quantum evolution with classical rate matrices to enable simulation across a wide range of temporal regimes required to simulate, for example, the long-time convergence of the ergodic $T_2^*$ from Ramsey experiments. Our methodology is validated against experimental data from various silicon spin qubit systems, demonstrating a strong agreement between simulation and measurement of Ramsey experiments presented in the form of $T_2^*$ versus averaging time, autocorrelation functions, as well as power spectral densities. Furthermore, we demonstrate significant back-action effects through modeling and experiment; specifically, the dynamics of the nuclear spin bath depends upon the electron spin occupation schedule. Finally, our modeling quantitatively predicts the benefits from compensating for the slow drift of Overhauser fields in qubit operations. Our findings indicate that compensating for an Overhauser rotation measured $Δt$ in the past results in an effective $T_2^*$, which we denote $\tilde{T}_2^*(Δt)$ for clarity, under certain scenarios of interest, can be one or two orders of magnitude larger than the ergodic $T_2^*$ if the Overhauser rotation is re-characterized every 100 milliseconds; that is, $\tilde{T}_2^*(Δt = 100~{\rm ms})$ can be $10$ to $100$ times larger than $T_2^*$.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Krylov-space memory cores as a stationary framework to locate structured quantum memory within the dynamics of many-body systems. By analyzing residual fluctuations and deviations from equilibrium along the Krylov chain, it distinguishes true, state-selective memory regions from generic thermalization backgrounds. This offers a quantitative way to probe non-equilibrium structure in complex quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful diagnostic tool for identifying non-trivial, localized quantum correlations or 'memory' in complex, thermalizing systems without relying on full time evolution simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to characterize anomalous initial-state memory in nonintegrable quantum many-body systems that otherwise tend toward thermalization.

**Main result.** Anomalous states develop compact, low-depth 'memory cores' characterized by residual fluctuations and Gibbs mismatch, which are distinct from the broader stationary probability background.

**Method.** The analysis maps the system dynamics onto an effective one-dimensional hopping problem on the Krylov subspace generated by the initial state $|\psi_0angle$.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes closed quantum many-body systems governed by a time-independent Hamiltonian $H$, focusing specifically on the structure within the cyclic Krylov subspace.

**Key observables.** Stationary occupation profile ($\Pi_n$), residual fluctuation profile ($\chi_n$), Gibbs-deviation profile ($d_n$), and bond-resolved Krylov-current fluctuation activity ($\Gamma_n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The memory core depth $n_{mc}$ is defined by the spatial concentration of $\chi_n, d_n,$ and $\Gamma_n$, distinguishing it from the full occupation depth $n(q)_\Pi$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Memory is operationally defined as a state-selective residual structure resolved by fluctuations ($\chi_n$), mismatch ($d_n$), or current activity ($\Gamma_n$).

**Figures summary.** The notes detail comparisons between the active memory depth ($n_{mc}$) and the full stationary occupation depth, showing $n_{mc}$ is significantly smaller than the latter for tested states.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops diagnostics beyond simple probability profiles ($\Pi_n$) by introducing $\chi_n, d_n,$ and $\Gamma_n$. It then applies these tools to various models (PXP chain, Ising state) to quantify the size of the structured memory core ($n_{mc}$) relative to the system's overall dimension.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce Krylov-space memory cores as stationary, depth-resolved structures that reveal how anomalous initial-state memory is organized inside the Krylov space of otherwise thermalizing nonintegrable systems. The stationary occupation profile identifies where late-time probability is concentrated along the Krylov chain, while complementary diagnostics of residual equilibration fluctuations, deviation from the Gibbs reference, and long-time Krylov-current fluctuations determine the physical character of that region. Across weak thermalization, confinement-induced anomalous dynamics, and many-body scarring, anomalous initial states develop compact low-depth memory cores that carry appreciable residual fluctuations, Gibbs mismatch, and persistent current-fluctuation activity. These cores are often embedded within substantially broader stationary occupation halos. Generic reference states, by contrast, do not exhibit a comparable combination of signal strength and spatial compactness. An auxiliary integrable comparison further shows that compact Krylov memory is state selective rather than a generic consequence of integrability. Krylov-space memory cores therefore provide a stationary framework for identifying where structured quantum memory resides and how it remains dynamically encoded.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to search for CP violation in top-quark pair production by analyzing the spin polarization and correlations of the produced $tar{t}$ state. It uses advanced tools from quantum information theory, such as discord and concurrence, applied to the density matrix derived from SMEFT extensions. The work provides a clean, model-independent diagnostic tool for future collider experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on particle physics, the methodology—mapping fundamental symmetries (CP) onto quantifiable quantum state properties (discord, concurrence)—is directly transferable to analyzing coherence loss or symmetry breaking in complex many-body quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate how potential new CP-violating top-quark interactions are encoded within the quantum state of a produced $tar{t}$ pair and to identify suitable quantum observables sensitive to this violation.

**Main result.** The paper establishes a production-level framework using quantum information measures (like discord and concurrence) that directly probe CP-odd structures in the $tar{t}$ spin density matrix, independent of decay kinematics.

**Method.** Analytic derivation of the $tar{t}$ spin density matrix using SMEFT operators, followed by constructing observables based on quantum information theory applied to the state's polarization and correlations.

**Model / system.** The system is the production of a top-antitop pair ($tar{t}$) via various hard scattering processes ($	ext{e}^+	ext{e}^-, \gamma\gamma, qar{q}, gg$). The theoretical framework incorporates potential new physics through dimension-six SMEFT operators.

**Key observables.** Polarisation asymmetries, the difference between top and antitop polarisation vectors, the anti-symmetric component of the spin-correlation matrix, quantum information measures (discord, concurrence, magic, trace distance).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The new physics scale ($\Lambda$) and the Wilson coefficients ($C_i$) associated with CP-violating SMEFT operators.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses strictly on the production state density matrix; reconstruction from decay products is deferred to a companion paper. Tree-level calculations are used, neglecting absorptive phases.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the structure suggests comparisons across different collider channels and sensitivity plots for various observables.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces CP-violating SMEFT interactions, derives the production density matrix in a common spin basis, identifies characteristic CP-odd structures, constructs corresponding observables, and finally assesses their sensitivity using quantum information measures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how possible new CP-violating top-quark interactions are encoded in the quantum state of a produced $t\bar t$ pair. We derive analytic expressions for the production density matrix in several benchmark channels relevant to hadron, lepton and photon colliders. In a common spin basis, we identify two characteristic CP-odd structures in the Fano--Bloch decomposition: a difference between the top and antitop polarisation vectors and an antisymmetric component of the spin-correlation matrix. We construct observables that directly probe these structures and study how quantum information measures, including discord, concurrence, magic and trace distance, respond to CP-even and CP-odd SMEFT contributions. Finally, using current measurements and future collider projections, we assess the sensitivity of these observables to possible new sources of CP violation in top-quark production. This establishes the production-level framework whose experimental reconstruction is developed in a companion paper.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a method to deterministically load molecular arrays into optical tweezers using microwave-assisted collisions (MWAC). By utilizing repulsive van der Waals interactions in specific molecular states, the authors suppress lossy collisions and engineer controlled energy release to eject molecules sequentially. This approach paves the way for scalable quantum simulators with predicted filling fractions up to 96%.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical bottleneck in molecular quantum simulation platforms by providing an engineered mechanism (MWAC) to overcome inherent collisional losses, making scalable array preparation feasible for studying many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving deterministic, high-efficiency loading of molecular arrays trapped in optical tweezer lattices for quantum simulation.

**Main result.** By employing microwave-assisted collisions (MWAC) combined with repulsive van der Waals interactions, the authors demonstrate a pathway to achieve filling fractions up to 96%.

**Method.** The methodology involves suppressing lossy short-range molecular encounters by shelving molecules and then using controlled energy release from MWACs to deterministically eject one molecule per collision cycle.

**Model / system.** The system consists of laser-cooled polar molecules trapped in optical tweezer arrays. The process relies on controlling molecular interactions via microwave dressing of rotational states, enabling engineered collisions analogous to light-assisted atomic loading.

**Key observables.** Filling fraction ($\phi_n$), MWAC efficiency, and the final state populations of the loaded molecules.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Microwave detuning ($\Delta$), Rabi frequency ($\Omega$), initial molecular states (rotational/vibrational), and trap depth ($V_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The schemes rely on currently available laser-cooling techniques for polar molecules; the best performance is achieved using ro-vibrational repulsion, which is limited by the excited state lifetime.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the general MWAC process involving repulsive interactions and ejection. Specific figures show the dependence of collision efficiency on $\Omega$ and $\Delta$, and time evolution plots comparing filling fractions for rotational versus ro-vibrational loading schemes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the limitations of current molecular loading techniques, then proposing the three-step solution (shelving $	o$ MWAC $	o$ Ejection), detailing the underlying physics using coupled-channels calculations, and finally presenting quantitative results showing high filling fractions achievable with ro-vibrational states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Molecular tweezer arrays offer great prospects for quantum simulation, sensing, and computing, and would benefit from methods that enhance loading efficiency. Whereas light-assisted collisions underpin enhanced loading methods for atomic tweezer arrays, this approach cannot be directly extended to molecular arrays due to collisional loss. We show how this collisional loss can be suppressed by shelving molecules in rotationally or vibrationally excited states, so that a shelved molecule interacts with a newly loaded molecule through a repulsive van der Waals interaction. By introducing microwave assisted collisions, we show how to control the final states and the energy released in a collision between a pair of molecules. Following this controlled collision, one of the two molecules can be ejected, and we explore several strategies for ensuring deterministic ejection. Our schemes rely on currently available techniques for laser-coolable molecules, and we predict achievable filling fractions up to 96%, paving the way for scalable molecular arrays.

</details>

**Summary.** This review surveys the experimental progress in harnessing ultracold polar molecules as a platform for quantum science. It details advanced techniques to achieve full quantum control, focusing specifically on managing long-range dipole-dipole interactions and minimizing loss during molecular collisions. The work highlights how these controlled systems can be used for complex quantum simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work sits at the intersection of AMO physics and quantum simulation. The detailed discussion on controlling dipole-dipole interactions via magic wavelengths/angles is crucial for realizing coherent many-body dynamics in molecular systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Reviewing experimental advances required to achieve full quantum control over ultracold polar molecules for applications like quantum simulation.

**Main result.** The review details successful techniques for controlling molecular collisions, engineering long-range dipolar interactions, and achieving precise internal state manipulation in these systems.

**Method.** The paper reviews various experimental methods, including magnetoassociation, STIRAP transfer, and using optical lattices/tweezers to control interactions.

**Model / system.** The system is ultracold polar diatomic molecules (e.g., bialkali species) possessing rich internal structure and long-range dipole-dipole interactions ($H_{dd}$). Control relies on manipulating rotational and hyperfine states via microwave or optical fields.

**Key observables.** Relative transition strengths, differential light shifts, collision loss rates, and the ability to prepare molecules in specific quantum superpositions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dipolar interaction strength ($V_{dd}$), magnetic field strength, laser detuning/wavelength (for magic angles/wavelengths), and temperature regime (ultracold).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The review assumes the applicability of models like the rigid-rotor approximation and relies on external fields to induce or control dipole moments.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Zeeman structures for bialkali molecules showing relative transition strengths versus magnetic field, and potentially show energy level diagrams related to molecular structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured as a review, focusing sequentially on three key areas: 1) Control of ultracold molecular collisions, 2) Engineering dipolar interactions using external fields/lattices, and 3) General internal state control mechanisms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ultracold polar molecules have emerged as an exciting platform for experiments in quantum science, with many proposed applications seeking to leverage the existence of long-range dipolar interactions and the rich internal structure of long-lived states. However, the complexity of molecules also presents many experimental challenges. In this chapter, we review the experimental advances over the past two decades that have brought ultracold molecules under full quantum control. We focus on three key topics: control of ultracold molecular collisions, engineering dipolar interactions and nascent quantum simulations in optical lattices.

</details>

### [Exponential de Finetti Theorems for Fermionic Gaussian States](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25779v1)

**Authors:** Jędrzej Burkat, Michał Studziński, Sergii Strelchuk
