**Summary.** This theoretical paper proves an exponential de Finetti theorem tailored for fermionic Gaussian states, significantly improving upon previous bounds. It extends the scope from highly symmetric states to all Gaussian-invariant states by utilizing advanced techniques involving replica symmetry and algebraic identities. The result provides a quantitative measure of how close complex quantum correlations are to simple product states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful tools for characterizing entanglement structure in many-body quantum systems by quantifying how close a complex correlated state is to a simple product (i.i.d.) state, which is crucial for understanding thermalization and local measurements in condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove an exponential variant of the de Finetti theorem for free-fermionic Gaussian states, extending previous results from Gaussian-symmetric to the broader class of Gaussian-invariant states.

**Main result.** They establish that reduced states of Gaussian-invariant fermionic Gaussian states can be approximated by convex combinations of almost-i.i.d. states with an error bound decaying exponentially or super-exponentially in system parameters, achieving polylogarithmic dimensional penalties.

**Method.** The proof combines advanced techniques from quantum information theory, including using Chernoff bounds on overlap variables and establishing algebraic equivalences between different mathematical formulations of the de Finetti theorem via commutation relations involving bridge operators.

**Model / system.** The system under study is fermionic Gaussian states defined on a lattice or set of qubits (Hilbert space $H \cong (\mathbb{C}^2)^{\otimes n}$), analyzed through replicas ($k$) and partial traces ($	ext{Tr}_m$). The theory focuses on symmetries like Gaussian-symmetric and Gaussian-invariant states.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the error bound $\delta$ quantifying how well a reduced state approximates an i.i.d. mixture; other key elements include overlap variables ($Q$) and bridge operators ($\Lambda_{ab}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the number of replicas ($k, m$), the system size ($n$), and the approximation parameter $r$. The analysis highlights different scaling regimes based on the relative sizes of $k$ and $m$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are derived for free-fermionic Gaussian states and rely heavily on specific algebraic properties involving Majorana operators and parity/pair-parity operators.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 numerically compares upper bounds for the error $\delta$ using different mathematical forms (Full Expression, Rising Factorial, Ratio Form) across various regimes of system parameters ($k>m$, $k=m$, $k<m$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the exponential bound for Gaussian-symmetric states (Theorem 1), then extending this to the broader class of Gaussian-invariant states via purification theorems, and finally proving the equivalence between different mathematical formulations of the de Finetti theorem using algebraic identities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove an exponential variant of the Gaussian de Finetti theorem: the subsystems of permutation-invariant, free-fermionic Gaussian states are well-approximated by convex combinations of almost-i.i.d. states that are Gaussian on subsets of their parts. Our result provides an error bound between the original state and its approximants that decays exponentially in the number of unconstrained parts, becoming super-exponential when the subsystem under consideration is small. The dimensional penalty of our bound is polylogarithmic in the local Hilbert space dimension, an exponential improvement over the standard de Finetti theorem of [Nat. Phys. 3, 645-649]. In the fully i.i.d. limit, our bound recovers the Gaussian de Finetti theorem of [arXiv:2603.12392]. Previous works considered Gaussian-symmetric states, which are supported on the trivial irrep of the tensor matchgate representation. We extend these to a broader class of Gaussian-invariant states containing, for example, i.i.d. copies of single-replica mixed Gaussian states. We show that Gaussian-invariant states are precisely the partial traces of Gaussian-symmetric states on locally enlarged replicas, and always admit a purification into a larger Gaussian-symmetric state. This extends de Finetti theorems to the full set of Gaussian-invariant states, with only a polynomial overhead in the dimensional penalty of the error bound.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate a method to engineer strong, controllable dipolar interactions between polar molecules by utilizing microwave dressing on their vibrational states. This technique achieves this tunability while simultaneously suppressing inelastic collisional losses across all relevant collision channels. The result opens a robust platform for simulating complex quantum magnetic and correlated electron models in the ultracold regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly controlled mechanism for generating tunable, long-range anisotropic interactions in ultracold quantum gases, directly enabling simulations of complex models like extended Hubbard and $t$-$J_z$ models using molecular platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To realize a unified platform for quantum simulation that provides strong, directly tunable state-dependent interactions while maintaining collisional stability in ultracold polar molecules.

**Main result.** The proposed microwave dressing scheme successfully generates highly tunable dipolar Ising ($J_z$), density-density ($V$), and spin-density ($W$) interactions while suppressing two-body losses below $10^{-13} 	ext{ cm}^3/	ext{s}$.

**Method.** Theoretical modeling using coupled-channel quantum scattering calculations to determine loss rates, combined with microwave dressing theory to engineer state-dependent long-range dipolar couplings.

**Model / system.** The system involves a mixture of polar molecules whose pseudo-spin is encoded in the vibrational ground ($v=0$) and first excited ($v=1$) states. Microwave fields are used to induce controlled, anisotropic interactions between these molecular states.

**Key observables.** Tunable interaction strengths ($J_z, V, W$), two-body collisional loss rates ($\max(k_{vv'})$), and scattering lengths ($\alpha_{vv'}^s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Microwave detuning ratios ($\Delta/\Omega$) and Rabi frequency ratios ($\Omega_\sigma/\Omega_\pi$); the interaction length scales relative to interparticle spacing ($d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial analysis focuses on the $v=0$ and $v=1$ subspace, though extension to higher vibrational manifolds is suggested.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy level diagrams (Fig. 1), map out stable regions in loss rate vs. detuning ratios (Fig. 3), show accessible parameter space for interactions ($J_z, V, W$) under stability constraints (Fig. 4), and trace tunable paths along these parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical platform using microwave dressing to engineer state-dependent dipolar interactions. It then rigorously calculates collision loss rates to ensure stability across various tuning paths, culminating in mapping out the parameter space where strong, controllable interactions can be achieved without significant inelastic decay.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose encoding a pseudo-spin-$1/2$ system in the ground ($v=0$) and first excited ($v=1$) vibrational states of polar molecules. Double microwave shielding simultaneously shields molecules in both states, suppressing two-body losses by orders of magnitude while strictly avoiding three-body recombination. The microwave dressing is state-dependent and results in highly tunable, long-range dipolar Ising exchange ($J_z$), density-density ($V$), and spin-density ($W$) interactions. These interaction length scales readily exceed the typical interparticle spacing, pushing the molecules deep into the strongly interacting regime. In bulk gases, this enables the exploration of itinerant quantum magnetism and quantum droplets with novel anisotropic spin textures; in optical lattices, it naturally realizes extended Hubbard and $t$-$J_z$ models, opening new directions in quantum simulation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates quantum teleportation over a metropolitan network built using Hollow-Core Fibre (HCF). By showing that HCF drastically reduces noise compared to standard fibers, the authors establish a robust framework for coexisting quantum and classical data streams. This paves a practical path toward realizing scalable, field-deployable quantum internet infrastructure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial experimental blueprint for scaling quantum networks by solving the critical engineering problem of integrating fragile quantum signals with high-power classical communication channels using novel fiber platforms like HCF.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving high-fidelity quantum teleportation over real-world, deployed fiber optic networks while simultaneously coexisting with intense classical data traffic.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated robust quantum teleportation using a field-deployed Hollow-Core Fibre (HCF) network, showing that HCF significantly suppresses noise compared to solid-core fibers.

**Method.** They implemented the quantum teleportation protocol using time-bin qubits and Bell-State Measurement (BSM), characterizing performance under high classical power levels up to 160 mW.

**Model / system.** The experiment utilizes a metropolitan HCF network connecting three nodes. The system involves generating entangled pairs, performing BSM at an intermediate node, and transmitting quantum states over the deployed fiber infrastructure.

**Key observables.** Three-fold coincidence rates, teleportation fidelities ($\langle F^{(1)}angle$), Raman scattering coefficients (HCF vs. SCF), and polarization/HOM interference stability over time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Classical launch powers up to 160 mW; noise suppression factor of approximately three orders of magnitude compared to standard fibers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The success relies on the HCF's ability to maintain long-term indistinguishability and exhibit significantly lower Raman noise, enabling a classical-data-compatible framework.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the deployed network topology in Chengdu, experimental setups detailing qubit generation and BSM integration with DWDM classical channels, and characterization plots comparing Raman scattering coefficients between HCF and SCF.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical setup (HCF deployment), the quantum protocol implementation (teleportation via BSM), rigorous performance testing under noise/classical load, and comparison of results against theoretical limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

When a photon and one member of an entangled photon pair are jointly projected onto a Bell-state measurement (BSM), the quantum state of the photon can be transferred to the distant partner of the pair without physically transmitting this information carrier. In real-world deployment, however, teleportation performance is fundamentally bottlenecked by quantum channel impairments, such as loss, noise, and fluctuations, which induce severe decoherence and degrade fidelity. This vulnerability is further exacerbated in scenarios with intense classical data traffic or background light. Realizing scalable quantum networks, therefore, hinges on developing advanced channel architectures capable of supporting both high-fidelity quantum operations and high-capacity classical communications within a shared infrastructure. Towards this end, hollow core fibre (HCF) offers a promising quantum channel resource by combining free-space-like weak light-matter interaction with the stability of fibre-based systems. Here, utilizing a field-deployed metropolitan HCF network spanning three spatially separated nodes in Chengdu, we achieve quantum teleportation with an intermediate BSM under co-propagating classical traffic. Crucially, the HCF links preserve the long-term indistinguishability of photonic qubits without active stabilization, and exhibit a Raman noise approximately three orders of magnitude lower than that of standard solid-core counterparts. This noise suppression enables robust quantum teleportation even alongside classical launch powers up to 160 mW. Our findings establish a classical-data-compatible framework for quantum networking over deployed fibre infrastructure and offer a wavelength-agnostic, plug-and-play, and free-running pathway toward the quantum internet.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors calculate the entanglement asymmetry in the gapped XYZ spin chain by linking it to the static susceptibility via a charged-moment identity. They combine theoretical results from sine-Gordon form factors with numerical iDMRG simulations to derive a universal master formula for this asymmetry. This work provides a rigorous quantitative measure of symmetry breaking encoded in quantum entanglement.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep insights into how entanglement measures quantify symmetry breaking in quantum many-body systems, connecting concepts from critical phenomena (CFT/sine-Gordon) with modern tools like entanglement entropy calculations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to measure entanglement asymmetry ($\Delta S_A$) and determine its universal amplitude in a gapped phase of the XYZ spin-$rac12$ chain where U(1) symmetry is broken.

**Main result.** They derive a master formula for $\Delta S_A^{(n)}(\ell)$ involving logarithmic growth, providing a lower bound on the universal amplitude $c$ via two-kink and one-breather contributions.

**Method.** The calculation combines a charged-moment identity with a non-conservation sum rule derived from sine-Gordon form factors, which are then validated using infinite-system iDMRG simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is the XYZ spin-$rac12$ chain Hamiltonian in its gapped, U(1)-breaking phase. The analysis utilizes concepts from conformal field theory (sine-Gordon model) and quantum entanglement measures.

**Key observables.** Entanglement Asymmetry ($\Delta S_A$), Static Susceptibility ($\chi_{zz}$), Kink mass ($M$), Universal amplitude $c(eta^2)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Anisotropy parameter $\gamma$, replica index $n$, interval length $\ell$, and the coupling constant related to the sine-Gordon model ($\xi_{sG}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculation relies on a charged-moment identity, treating the system in the continuum limit via the sine-Gordon model, and assuming the leading asymptotic behavior of the entanglement entropy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between iDMRG results and theoretical bounds for $c(eta^2)$, show the convergence of $\Delta S_A^{(n)}(\ell)$ to a master formula as interval length increases, and plot the rise of $\Delta S_A^{(n)}(\ell)$ with increasing anisotropy $\gamma$.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the charged-moment identity linking asymmetry to susceptibility. It then evaluates this susceptibility using form factors (sine-Gordon model) to derive a lower bound on the amplitude, which is finally confirmed and refined through iDMRG simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The entanglement asymmetry measures how strongly a symmetry is broken inside a subsystem. Analytic results at equilibrium have so far covered free theories and, perturbatively, the critical XXZ chain. We compute the Rényi entanglement asymmetries of a large interval in the gapped, $U(1)$-breaking phase of the interacting XYZ chain. The calculation combines three ingredients. A charged-moment identity, which we prove for fermionic Gaussian and for injective matrix-product ground states, ties the asymmetry to the static susceptibility of the broken charge. A non-conservation sum rule then evaluates the susceptibility from sine-Gordon form factors, its two-kink and one-breather channels providing a lower bound on the universal amplitude. The Baxter--Johnson--Krinsky--McCoy solution supplies the kink mass for different couplings. Infinite-system density-matrix renormalization group simulations built on these masses reproduce the master formula.

</details>

The scattering length is expressed in dipolar length units, and shown as a universal function of the dimensionless parameter αe/Ed. Markers indicate the results of full coupled-channels calculations for NaK and KAg, and the agreement with the minimal model verifies the universality.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a theoretical framework to calculate the ro-vibrational van der Waals interaction in ultracold polar molecules. It finds this specific interaction is unusually strong, allowing for novel applications such as evaporative cooling and controlled state manipulation without external field shielding. This opens new avenues for quantum simulation using molecular gases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses controllable, state-dependent long-range interactions in molecular systems, which is crucial for building quantum simulators or studying fundamental many-body physics in a highly tunable platform.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on theoretically describing and harnessing the ro-vibrational van der Waals (vdW) interaction between ultracold polar molecules.

**Main result.** This novel ro-vibrational vdW interaction is predicted to be significantly stronger than other molecular interactions, enabling advanced quantum control techniques like evaporative cooling without external shielding.

**Method.** The analysis employs second-order perturbation theory applied to the dipole-dipole interaction Hamiltonian, utilizing the Dunham expansion for energy levels.

**Model / system.** The system involves ultracold polar molecules in different ro-vibrational states $(v, j)$. The interactions are governed by the anisotropic dipole-dipole coupling and anharmonicity effects.

**Key observables.** Interaction strength ($C_6$), collision dynamics (elastic vs. inelastic loss), and state-dependent interaction coefficients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Molecular constants like rotational/vibrational frequencies ($\omega_e, B_e$) and the ro-vibrational coupling constant ($\alpha_e$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the anharmonicity of the potential leads to a non-resonant dipolar interaction (ro-vibrational vdW) that dominates over purely rotational or hyperfine contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates molecular energy levels showing coupling between states due to anharmonicity, which drives the ro-vibrational vdW mechanism.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical system and the need for strong interactions. It then develops the theoretical framework by calculating the ro-vibrational coupling constant ($\alpha_e$) using various potential models. Finally, it details the resulting strong vdW interaction and its diverse applications in quantum simulation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We describe the ro-vibrational van der Waals interaction between ultracold polar molecules. This interaction is strong, leading to fast elastic collisions and orders of magnitude suppression of collisional loss. This enables evaporative cooling of Fermi mixtures of molecules in different ro-vibrational states, without active shielding by applying external fields. The scheme is compatible with microwave shielding, where it enables controlled state dependent interactions, opening up new opportunities for quantum simulation and impurity physics. The interaction can also be used to stabilize fermionic molecules in optical lattices, to control interactions in synthetic dimensions, for enhanced tweezer loading, and direct infrared shielding.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates how partially fault-tolerant quantum encoding can significantly boost the accuracy of quantum simulations on current noisy hardware. By using the [[4, 2, 2]] code and an advanced filtering technique called ORP, the authors show substantial improvements in estimating local observables for the Ising model compared to unencoded runs. This work provides a practical roadmap for scaling complex quantum simulations beyond immediate NISQ limitations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the critical gap between NISQ devices and fault-tolerance by proposing a resource-efficient, intermediate error correction strategy applicable to simulating condensed matter models like the Ising model.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to improve quantum simulation performance on noisy, near-term hardware by implementing error suppression techniques.

**Main result.** Partially fault-tolerant encoding significantly outperforms unencoded simulations, showing accuracy improvements of 2-6% in 1+1D and over 200% in 2+1D at late times studied.

**Method.** The authors use a partially fault-tolerant scheme combining syndrome extraction with non-fault-tolerant logical operations, alongside Observable-Ranked Postselection (ORP) for data filtering.

**Model / system.** Simulations focus on the Ising model in 1+1D and 2+1D lattices. The physical implementation uses IBM's superconducting quantum computer (`ibm boston`) with a heavy-hex processor, employing the [[4, 2, 2]] Iceberg code.

**Key observables.** Local observables are estimated; key diagnostics include the logical error rate and the improvement factor in local-observable accuracy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison is made between unencoded vs. partially fault-tolerant regimes, showing performance gains that scale with circuit depth (especially in 2+1D).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach balances error rate improvements from FT against overhead by using a pFT scheme; ORP assumes the optimal cutoff level balances bias reduction against statistical uncertainty inflation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1a details the structure and stabilizers of the [[4, 2, 2]] code block. Figure 1b shows the placement of these blocks on the ibm boston processor. Figure 1c illustrates the circuit operations involving syndrome extraction and non-FT logical gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the need for error suppression in quantum simulations, details the use of the [[4, 2, 2]] code block structure, explains the partially fault-tolerant protocol combining FT syndrome measurement with nFT logic, and finally presents results showing quantitative improvements over unencoded baselines using ORP.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error-detecting codes offer a near-term path for improving the performance of quantum simulations on noisy hardware. Using IBM's superconducting quantum computer ibm_boston, we show that partially fault-tolerant encoded quantum simulations of the Ising model in 1+1D and 2+1D outperform their unencoded counterparts in estimating local observables. To represent 42 logical qubits on the heavy-hex quantum processor, 21 blocks of the [[4, 2, 2]] Iceberg code and up to 136 physical qubits are used. By pairing fault-tolerant syndrome extraction with non-fault-tolerant logical operations, this scheme preserves many of the benefits of error detection while avoiding the overhead typically required for a fully fault-tolerant logical gate set. The encoding's square logical connectivity, together with the freedom to place logical qubits within each block, enables simulations of a 2D spatial lattice with lower circuit depth than the unencoded implementation requires. We introduce Observable-Ranked Postselection, a selective-filtering technique based on syndrome correlations that recovers reliable results without the prohibitive shot loss of full syndrome postselection. Under the cumulative effect of device errors, this encoding improves local-observable accuracy over the unencoded baseline by 2-6% at intermediate times in 1+1D simulations, growing with circuit depth to over 200% in 2+1D at the latest times studied.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper generalizes the study of contextuality by replacing probability measures with distributions valued in effect algebras. It develops a unified framework that distinguishes between inherent structural contextuality and observable contextuality after measurement outcomes are realized. The methodology uses advanced tools from sheaf theory and convex geometry to derive generalized mathematical criteria for quantum non-contextuality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep mathematical underpinning for understanding measurement limitations in quantum mechanics, connecting abstract algebra (effect algebras) directly to fundamental physical concepts like non-contextuality and resource theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a unified mathematical framework to analyze quantum contextuality by generalizing standard probability-based models to those valued in convex effect algebras.

**Main result.** It establishes a clear distinction between internal contextuality (structural) and observable contextuality (state-dependent), characterizing non-contextuality via generalized cone programs.

**Method.** The authors employ sheaf theory, ordered-vector-space representations of effect modules, and cone duality to generalize linear programming formulations used in classical probability theory.

**Model / system.** The model is the 'effect-valued measurement model,' which replaces standard probability distributions with distributions valued in an effect algebra A. This encompasses probabilistic, deterministic, and general quantum measurements (POVMs).

**Key observables.** Contextuality witnesses (generalized Bell inequalities), observable contextuality, internal contextuality, and the feasibility of associated cone programs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure is governed by the effect algebra A, which abstracts unsharp measurement effects.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes that general measurements can be modeled using convex effect algebras, allowing for a unified algebraic treatment beyond standard quantum probability measures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by generalizing the sheaf-theoretic approach from semirings to effect algebras. It then analyzes contextuality via cone programs and introduces generalized Bell witnesses derived from duality theory, concluding with structural analyses of dilation theorems and monadic constructions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We generalize the Abramsky--Brandenburger sheaf-theoretic treatment of contextuality by replacing probability distributions with distributions valued in a convex effect algebra \(A\). This yields a notion of \(A\)-valued measurement model encompassing probabilistic, deterministic, and quantum measurement structures within a single framework.   States \(σ:A\to[0,1]\) induce ordinary empirical models, allowing observable behaviour to be viewed as arising from effect-valued measurement data. This leads to a distinction between internal contextuality of \(A\)-models and observable contextuality after state evaluation. We analyze the relationship between these notions and identify coherence conditions under which observable classical explanations assemble into internal ones.   Using the ordered-vector-space representation of effect modules, we show that non-contextuality is characterized by feasibility of an associated cone program, generalizing the linear-programming formulation of contextuality in the probabilistic case. We also introduce an effect-valued contextual fraction and study its relation to observable contextuality witnesses. We show how cone duality leads to a notion of Bell witnesses for contextuality as a direct generalization of Bell inequalities.   Finally, we analyze sharp realizability and uniform dilation for measurement models, clarifying the relationship between general POVMs and projective measurements, and show how resource-indexed effect structures induce graded monads generalizing the quantum monad.

</details>

Various doping counts (5, 75, 468) and types (T or S gates) are chosen that permit efficient measurement of fidelity (DFE) or fidelity proxy (XEB). XEB is known to overestimate fidelity due to readout errors, so we also show a rescaled quantity that controls for this effect. All experiments show that our estimated lower bound accurately captures the maximum loss in fidelity due to doping. Error bars show 95% confidence intervals. b. Syndrome probability distributions show high consistency, regardless of doping strategy. This is consistent with a similar noise environment affecting all experiments, and doping gates being noiseless.</sub>

**Summary.** The paper presents a method called Doped Clifford Sampling (DCS) to demonstrate quantum computational hardness while simultaneously addressing hardware noise and verification. By encoding hard circuits into structured quantum codes, the authors achieve high fidelity at deep depths on superconducting qubits. This provides a systematic way to certify quantum advantage claims with quantifiable error bounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the gap between theoretical quantum advantage claims (hardness) and experimental reality (noise/verification), providing a concrete framework for certifying computation in NISQ-era devices relevant to AMO platforms like trapped ions or superconducting circuits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is combining complexity-theoretic hardness (for quantum advantage) with the practical requirements of scalable quantum computation: error suppression and verifiable fidelity.

**Main result.** The authors propose a structured circuit approach that allows for high-fidelity sampling from hard quantum states while providing a device-dependent, quantifiable certificate of experimental fidelity.

**Method.** They introduce 'structured circuits' encoded in quantum codes (spacetime codes) to simultaneously achieve hardness and error correction. Fidelity is certified by measuring code syndromes after syndrome post-selection.

**Model / system.** The work uses a 70-qubit, depth-70 Clifford circuit doped with T gates, implemented on a superconducting quantum processor using 97 physical qubits for encoding in spacetime codes.

**Key observables.** Lower bound on the fidelity (e.g., $\ge 0.284$ at 95% confidence), syndrome measurement distributions, and the required number of physical qubits vs. logical qubits.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth (70), number of T gates (468), encoding overhead (97 physical qubits for 70 data qubits), and fidelity lower bound ($\ge 0.284$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The resulting certificate is device dependent, and the fidelity lower bound relies on assuming a general Pauli noise model attainable by twirling gates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the workflow of doped Clifford sampling (DCS), comparing measured vs. expected fidelities across doping stages; other figures detail qubit layouts and validation of the fidelity lower bound using DFE.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the DCS protocol, demonstrates its application to a large-scale circuit on superconducting hardware, quantifies the resulting fidelity lower bound through syndrome measurements, and compares this approach favorably against existing fidelity benchmarks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Sampling-based proposals are prominent candidates for demonstrating quantum computations beyond the reach of classical supercomputers. However, it has been difficult to combine their complexity-theoretic hardness with two capabilities needed for scalable quantum computing more generally: suppressing hardware errors, and verifying the quantum computation itself. Here we address both issues by introducing structured circuits, which, in addition to provable hardness guarantees, admit an encoding in a quantum code. This allows us to simultaneously reach high fidelities at high circuit depths, and to certify an experimental fidelity via the circuit structure and measurement of code syndromes. The resulting certificate is device dependent, but requires substantially weaker noise assumptions than existing fidelity proxy benchmarks. We demonstrate our proposal with a $70$-qubit, depth-$70$ Clifford circuit doped with $468$ $T$ gates. We use a total of $97$ physical qubits to encode this computation in spacetime codes, effectively suppressing gate error rates by $10\times$ after syndrome post-selection, and yielding a state with a fidelity lower bound of $0.284$ with $95\%$ confidence. Our construction is a systematic method for promoting a stabilizer state to a magic state while keeping an error-detected fidelity certificate.

</details>

**Summary.** The research uses Quantum Monte Carlo simulations to model Bose-Bose liquid mixtures confined towards two dimensions. It rigorously tests theoretical functionals against these calculations, finding that while 2D models offer computational savings, they break down rapidly as confinement increases beyond a narrow regime. This work is vital for accurately predicting the behavior of quantum droplets and vortices in highly constrained atomic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial benchmarks for modeling strongly interacting ultracold atomic mixtures in quasi-low dimensional geometries, highlighting the limitations of mean-field approximations (like LHY) when full correlation effects are important.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates ultradilute Bose-Bose liquid mixtures confined towards the quasi-two-dimensional (2D) limit to determine the confinement regime where their equations of state exhibit universality.

**Main result.** QMC calculations show that universal descriptions based on two scattering parameters ($a$ and $r_{	ext{eff}}$) are only valid for moderate confinement, suggesting a very narrow range where 2D Lee-Huang-Yang (LHY) functionals are applicable.

**Method.** The study employs Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) methods at zero temperature to calculate equations of state and develop density functionals, comparing these results against established LHY predictions.

**Model / system.** The system is a mixture of $^{39}	ext{K}$ bosons confined in an external harmonic potential, allowing for the study of crossover from 3D bulk behavior towards ideal 2D physics.

**Key observables.** Equations of state, density profiles (with and without vortices), saturation densities, and droplet energies per particle ($E/N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Scattering length ($a$), effective range ($r_{	ext{eff}}$), and the confinement strength quantified by the harmonic oscillator length ($a_z$) or squeezing factor ($f$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The applicability of 2D LHY functionals is severely limited to a narrow range of confinement, and two scattering parameters are insufficient for describing interactions below a certain confinement threshold.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare energy per particle vs. density showing universality limits (Fig. 1); they also contrast droplet density profiles between QMC and LHY methods across varying confinements (Fig. 2), and show the departure of DMC results from LHY predictions as confinement increases (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theoretical frameworks using QMC to calculate equations of state for various confinements, compares these rigorous results against simpler models like the 2D LHY functional, and applies this analysis to predict properties of self-bound quantum droplets.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study ultradilute $^{39}$K Bose-Bose bulk mixtures and droplets in an external harmonic potential that confines them in one spatial direction towards the two-dimensional (2D) limit. Equations of state for several confinements are obtained with quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) at $T=0$, using interaction potentials that include information on the $s$-wave scattering length $a$ and the effective range $r_{\rm eff}$. Performing the calculations using two different interaction potential models we have determined the range of confinements for which equations of state are universal in terms of $a$ and $r_{\rm eff}$. Based on the QMC equation of state, we develop a 2D QMC density functional for each confinement strength and use it together with the local density approximation to determine properties of the self-bound drops. For moderate squeezing, energies and droplet profiles obtained using the 2D QMC functional agree well with those obtained using 3D functionals, while offering a substantial reduction in computational cost, and a consistent approach in crossover to 2D. Noticeably, our results approach 2D mean-field (MF) + Lee-Huang-Yang (LHY) predictions only for the most strongly confined systems for which universality in terms of $a$ and $r_{\rm eff}$ is observed. This implies a very narrow range of confinements for which 2D LHY functionals are applicable, which has important consequences for the study of vortices.

</details>

**Authors:** Wojciech Bruzda, Zahra Raissi  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.25954v1>

Squares on the solid line represent theoretical predictions, while dots with error bars (corresponding to the standard error) show the sample means over 256 independent Haar-random unitaries.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an 'equi-entropic map' ($\Xi$) designed to enforce that three specific bipartitions of a four-party quantum state share equal linear entropy, even if the state is not maximally entangled. Using group theory and moment calculations, the authors prove this equi-entropic property holds for the map's output states. The work provides a theoretical framework for quantifying structured entanglement uniformity in multipartite systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of enforcing entropic uniformity through a structured linear map provides a powerful theoretical tool for characterizing entanglement properties beyond simple maximal entanglement conditions. This has implications for designing robust quantum protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors investigate a weaker form of entanglement uniformity for four-party quantum systems, requiring three specific bipartitions to share equal linear entropy.

**Main result.** They introduce an 'equi-entropic map' ($\Xi$) whose image states are guaranteed to have equal entropies across the three relevant rearrangements. For large dimensions, this common entropy approaches its maximum value as $O(d^{-2})$.

**Method.** The analysis involves constructing a linear map $\Xi$ using group averaging over reshuffling and partial transposition operators acting on the state tensor. Convergence is analyzed through iterative procedures and moment calculations.

**Model / system.** The system under study consists of four-party quantum states in a composite Hilbert space $H = \mathcal{C}^d \otimes 4$. The analysis relates these states to concepts like Absolutely Maximally Entangled (AME) states and two-unitary matrices.

**Key observables.** Normalized Linear Entropy ($S$), the entropy deficit, and the deviation from maximal mixedness in the Hilbert–Schmidt norm ($\delta_{	ext{HS}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Local dimension $d$, which is analyzed in the asymptotic limit ($d 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on leading-moment approximations for large dimensions and assumes the existence of specific group structures related to permutations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 (mentioned conceptually) shows that for Haar-random unitary inputs, the expected entropy $E[S(\Xi(X))]$ saturates towards 1 as the dimension $d$ increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining highly entangled states (AME) to introducing the map $\Xi$ via group averaging. It then proves the equi-entropic property of the map's image, analyzes its asymptotic behavior for random inputs, and finally relates entropy deficit to distance measures like the HS defect.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Absolutely maximally entangled states represent a highly constrained form of multipartite entanglement and play an important role in quantum information theory. We investigate a weaker form of uniformity of entanglement for four-party systems of local dimension $d>2$ that requires the three balanced bipartitions to have equal but not necessarily maximal linear entropy. We introduce a linear map $Ξ$ that enforces exact equality of entropies under reshuffling and partial transposition. The transformation arises as the asymptotic limit of an iterative averaging procedure and admits a group-theoretic description in terms of permutations of tensor indices. For Haar-random unitary inputs, a leading-moment analysis supported by numerical simulations predicts highly entangled outputs whose common entropy approaches the maximal value as the local dimension grows. We characterize the algebraic structure, fixed points, and asymptotic behavior of this map and its relation to two-unitary matrices and orthogonal Latin squares.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses quantum Monte Carlo simulations to study the attractive Hubbard model on the kagome lattice. The primary finding is the existence of a superfluid phase transition characterized by chiral-XY criticality that occurs without any accompanying charge density wave order. This challenges mean-field predictions and highlights the role of geometric frustration in stabilizing pure superconducting behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that superfluidity can be robustly established without charge order in a geometrically frustrated lattice like kagome is highly relevant for understanding unconventional superconductivity mechanisms beyond simple mean-field treatments, which often predict coexisting CDW and SC states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the emergence of superfluidity in the attractive Hubbard model on the kagome lattice while simultaneously demonstrating that this pairing transition occurs without any accompanying charge order.

**Main result.** A superfluid transition is found at a critical interaction $U_c/t = -4.58(3)$ belonging to the chiral-XY universality class, and this superconductivity persists even for large interactions where mean-field theory might predict charge ordering.

**Method.** The research employs auxiliary-field quantum Monte Carlo simulations and finite-size scaling analysis to probe the ground state properties across different electronic fillings.

**Model / system.** The system is the attractive Hubbard model defined on the geometrically frustrated kagome lattice. The Hamiltonian includes hopping ($t$) and an attractive on-site interaction ($U$).

**Key observables.** Superfluid order parameter ($	ext{Ps}$), single-particle gap ($\Delta_{sp}$), density correlation functions ($C_n(r)$, $S_n(\mathbf{q})$), and local derivatives of kinetic energy and double occupancy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical attractive interaction ratio ($U_c/t$), electronic filling ($ho=2/3$ for Dirac point, $ho=1/2$, $ho=5/6$ near van Hove singularities).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the applicability of QMC simulations and comparisons with theoretical expectations from mean-field theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the noninteracting band structure showing relevant fillings, and subsequent figures show finite-size scaling collapses of the superfluid order parameter ($\Delta_s$) near the critical point, alongside plots tracking local correlation changes across the transition.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system by first establishing the model and methods (QMC/scaling), then focusing on specific fillings like $ho=2/3$ to find the chiral-XY transition. It contrasts these findings with mean-field predictions and uses local observables ($\partial D/\partial|U|/t$) as secondary confirmation of the phase boundary.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using auxiliary-field quantum Monte Carlo simulations, we study the zero-temperature attractive Hubbard model on the kagome lattice at various relevant electronic fillings. The low-energy physics at $2/3$-density is influenced by the Dirac point at the Fermi energy, wherein we unveil a superfluid transition at a critical attractive interaction $U_c/t=-4.58(3)$, which belongs to the chiral-XY universality class. This U(1) symmetry-breaking is not accompanied by charge order, even for substantially large interaction strengths, in a regime where a description in terms of hardcore bosons becomes increasingly suitable. An investigation of the latter shows that charge ordering at $1/3$-filling only occurs at interaction strengths much larger than those corresponding to the mapping to the original fermionic model. Additionally, for densities exhibiting van Hove singularities in the non-interacting density of states, our results show that any attractive interaction gives rise to superfluidity, yet again without charge ordering, contrary to recent studies employing mean-field theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores how PT-symmetry influences the decoherence and memory effects of a central spin interacting with an environment. By using pseudo-Hermitian techniques, the authors show that tuning the symmetry parameter ($\gamma$) can effectively shield the system from noise, leading to predictable changes in coherence decay and non-Markovian behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical mechanism (PT-symmetry) to explain phenomena like environment-induced coherence freezing and information backflow saturation, which are crucial concepts in open quantum systems theory relevant to AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how introducing PT-symmetry into a central spin model affects fundamental open quantum system dynamics, specifically decoherence, environment-induced selection (einselection), and non-Markovianity.

**Main result.** Increasing the PT-symmetry parameter ($\gamma$) slows down decoherence until it ceases at $\gamma=1$, leading to einselection towards self-Hamiltonian eigenstates. The analysis reveals characteristic turning points in information backflow, indicating environmental shielding by the PT-symmetric structure.

**Method.** The authors employ the pseudo-Hermitian formalism and apply a Dyson map to transform the non-Hermitian Hamiltonian into an equivalent Hermitian representation for analysis.

**Model / system.** The system is a central spin model coupled to a bath (environment) via a PT-symmetric interaction Hamiltonian. The dynamics are analyzed by examining the time evolution of coherence, purity, and information flow measures.

**Key observables.** Decoherence factor ($r(t)$), steady-state purity, Breuer-Laine-Piilo (BLP) measure for non-Markovianity, and information backflow.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The PT-symmetry parameter $\gamma$, the self-Hamiltonian strength ($\omega_0$), and environmental coupling scales ($s_N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on pseudo-Hermitian formalism techniques and approximations like the Gaussian approximation for the bath's characteristic function in certain regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the decoherence factor $|r(t)|$ approaching 1 at $\gamma=1$, and show non-Markovianity measures (NBLP) exhibiting turning point behavior as a function of $\gamma$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system by first establishing the pseudo-Hermitian framework, then calculating decoherence dynamics in different environmental regimes, followed by analyzing einselection effects and finally quantifying non-Markovianity via the BLP measure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We have introduced PT-symmetry to a central spin model by adding a PT-symmetric interaction with a tunable hermiticity parameter $γ$. Using the pseudo-Hermitian formalism, we applied a Dyson map to transform the non-Hermitian Hamiltonian to its Hermitian representation. We have found that the decoherence slows down as $γ$ increases, eventually ceasing at $γ=1$. We define a pseudo-Hermitian observable that commutes with the metric operator to add as the self-Hamiltonian. Einselection gradually forced the system to select the eigenstates of the self-Hamiltonian as $γ\rightarrow1$. The steady-state purity of the central spin states in the strong environment regime exhibits a paradoxical decrease as $γ$ increases. However, a turning point (minimum) corresponding to a maximum information dissipation to the spin bath is found. Finally, the Breuer-Laine-Piilo (BLP) measure, which is used to quantify the non-Markovianity of a quantum system, was evaluated for a finite time. The BLP measure in the strong environment regime exhibited similar turning point behavior, which means that the information backflow reaches a saturation point before declining. This decline signifies the point where PT-symmetry starts shielding the central spin from the environment.

</details>

**Authors:** Vasco Cavina, Frank Ernesto Quintela Rodríguez, Donato Farina

**Summary.** This paper establishes a thermodynamic framework for non-Hermitian quantum evolution by defining an entropy production quantity based on post-selected fluctuation theorems. The authors derive a generalized second law ($\langle\Delta Sangle + \Xi \ge 0$) and show that the coherent part of this production exhibits unique signatures, particularly near exceptional points in the system's parameter space.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous thermodynamic interpretation for non-Hermitian quantum dynamics, linking concepts like entropy production and fluctuation theorems directly to spectral singularities (EPs), which is crucial for understanding open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors develop a first-principles thermodynamic framework for non-Hermitian dynamics by identifying an entropy production quantity derived from a post-selected fluctuation theorem.

**Main result.** They establish a second law analogue, $\langle\Delta Sangle + \Xi \ge 0$, and show that the coherent contribution to entropy production ($\Xi_c$) exhibits distinctive signatures near exceptional points (EPs).

**Method.** The framework utilizes post-selection on fluctuation theorems, relating entropy production ($\Sigma$) to information-theoretic measures like Petz–Rényi divergences ($D_2$).

**Model / system.** The analysis is primarily conducted using a paradigmatic two-level system (qubit) governed by an effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonian $H_{	ext{eff}}(t)$.

**Key observables.** Entropy Production ($\Sigma$), Non-Hermitian Correction ($\Xi$), Average Entropy Variation ($\langle\Delta Sangle$), and the Coherent Contribution ($\Xi_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the behavior near Exceptional Points (EPs), where non-normality of the effective Hamiltonian becomes apparent.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework relies fundamentally on post-selection, conditioning the dynamics on no jumps occurring during evolution.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the time evolution of $\Sigma$, $\Xi$, and $-\langle\Delta Sangle$ for a qubit model at an EP. Figure 2 specifically plots the coherent contribution $\Xi_c$ near the EP, showing a strongly negative signature centered exactly at the EP location.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from deriving the formal definition of entropy production ($\Sigma$) using post-selected FTs, establishing its connection to relative entropies ($D_2$), and finally decomposing $\Sigma$ into incoherent and coherent parts to analyze EPs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a first-principles thermodynamic framework for non-Hermitian dynamics based on a post-selected version of the fluctuation theorem. This allows us to identify a quantity that remains positive throughout the non-Hermitian evolution and can be interpreted as the entropy production of the post-selected dynamics. We relate this quantity to previously proposed notions of non-Hermitian entropy and derive an associated second law. Furthermore, we establish a connection with information-theoretic quantities, in particular the Petz-Rényi divergences, and leverage this connection to derive upper and lower bounds. Finally, we decompose the entropy production into incoherent and coherent contributions, identifying distinctive features of the coherent term in the vicinity of exceptional points. We illustrate our results using a paradigmatic model of non-Hermitian evolution based on a two-level system.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes anomalous generalized bunching in multiphoton interference within lossy linear optical circuits. It demonstrates that conditioning the measurement on photon survival fundamentally changes the expected correlation hierarchy compared to standard models. The key finding is that this anomaly can persist even after accounting for output-mode attenuation, pointing to an intrinsic nonunitary interference structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems because it provides a detailed theoretical understanding of how environmental coupling (loss) modifies fundamental quantum correlations, specifically generalized bunching, in ways that are not captured by simple unconditioned measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates anomalous generalized bunching phenomena occurring during multiphoton interference when internal loss mechanisms and survival conditioning are present in passive linear optical circuits.

**Main result.** Survival conditioning fundamentally alters the minimal hierarchy of generalized bunching compared to unconditioned probabilities, leading to anomalies like nonmonotonic behavior maximized at partially distinguishable photon states.

**Method.** The analysis employs a theoretical framework using conditional probability ratios ($B(T)_K(S)$) derived from transfer matrices describing lossy optical circuits. Mathematical proofs establish necessary and sufficient conditions for the anomaly's existence.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by passive linear optical circuits where $N$ photons interact via a unitary operator $\hat{U}$, followed by coupling to unobserved environmental (loss) modes, resulting in an accessible transfer matrix $T$.

**Key observables.** Conditional bunching probability ($B(T)_K(S)$), which measures the target-region probability given that all photons survive.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of photons ($N=2$ or $N=3$), degree of distinguishability ($x \in [0, 1]$), and parameters characterizing loss/interference ($\Delta_2, \Delta_3$, etc.).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the unitary transformation acts only on spatial modes without changing internal states; it distinguishes between external linear loss and mode-dependent output loss.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate generalized bunching in both standard (unitary) and lossy settings, comparing results for $N=2$ and $N=3$. Specific figures compare anomalous vs. monotonic behavior under different circuit conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the formalism of conditional bunching probability in lossy circuits to analyzing specific cases ($N=2$, then $N=3$). It systematically proves that mode-dependent output loss can induce anomalies, and finally derives necessary mathematical conditions ($\Delta_2 > 0$ and $q(1) < 0$) for this anomaly to persist even after rigorous row normalization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that internal loss and survival conditioning can activate anomalous generalized bunching in passive linear optical circuits. We introduce a conditional bunching probability that all photons occupy a target region of accessible output modes, given that all photons survive. For two-photon inputs, we prove that this probability is always monotonic for any circuit size and loss configuration, although a multimode target region can reverse the monotonic direction. For three-photon inputs in a minimal three-mode lossy interferometer, we find a nonmonotonic anomaly in which the conditional bunching probability is maximized for partially distinguishable photons. This behavior is forbidden for the corresponding unconditioned target-region probability, demonstrating that survival-conditioned loss changes the minimal hierarchy of generalized bunching.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reveals that symmetry-breaking long-range order can emerge kinetically when excitations are initially constrained by symmetries. By coupling the system to an order parameter field, these constraints are lifted, allowing excitations to gain kinetic energy that stabilizes the ordered phase. The mechanism is successfully applied to both spin chains and itinerant magnetism in the Kitaev-Kondo model.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of order being driven by kinetic energy gain rather than purely minimizing potential energy is a powerful general mechanism applicable to many quantum systems, including those studied in AMO physics (e.g., superfluid transitions or Bose-Einstein condensation).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how kinetic energy gain from excitations, particularly those initially constrained by symmetries, can drive a symmetry-breaking phase transition into a state with long-range order.

**Main result.** This mechanism is demonstrated in both spin chains (dimerization instability) and the Kitaev-Kondo model, showing that finite density of mobile excitations can stabilize ordered phases, even counterintuitively promoting order at higher temperatures.

**Method.** The analysis employs mean-field theory, perturbation theory in small coupling constants, and free energy functional minimization to track the stability boundaries driven by kinetic energy contributions.

**Model / system.** The work analyzes two primary models: (i) a spin chain with dipole conservation coupled to lattice distortions, and (ii) the Kitaev-Kondo model involving itinerant electrons interacting with local moments forming a spin liquid. The Hamiltonian includes terms for hopping, magnetic coupling, and interaction.

**Key observables.** Dimerization order parameter ($\langle u angle$), spontaneous magnetization ($\vec{m}$), and the critical coupling strength ($g_c$ or $J_{c,0}$) required to induce ordering.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling constants ($g$, $J$), temperature ($T$), excitation gap ($\Delta v$), and density of excitations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mean-field approximations for the order parameter fields (e.g., lattice distortion or local magnetization) and perturbative expansions in small coupling strengths.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how dimerization activates mobility in a spin chain, show vison dispersion upon coupling to a magnetized band, and present phase diagrams mapping kinetic energy gain against order parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the general mechanism first, applying it sequentially to two distinct physical realizations: the 1D spin chain instability, followed by the more complex 2D Kitaev-Kondo model analysis involving vison excitations and free energy calculations at finite temperature.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A fascinating feature of strongly correlated systems is that excitations may carry nontrivial quantum numbers under emergent symmetries which, in some cases, may strongly constrain their motion. We show that such constrained excitations can play a central role in driving symmetry-breaking long-range order through a kinetic mechanism: coupling to a local order-parameter field activates the mobility of otherwise constrained excitations, and condensation of the order parameter then enables a large gain in kinetic energy, thereby stabilizing a phase with long-range order. We illustrate this mechanism in two settings: (i) a dimerization instability in a spin chain with a magnetization dipole conservation law, and (ii) itinerant magnetism of electrons in the Kitaev-Kondo model, where charge carriers couple to local moments that form a spin liquid. This mechanism becomes operative whenever a finite density of mobility-constrained excitations is present, either through non-equilibrium preparation or thermal activation. In the latter case, we argue that increasing temperature can, counterintuitively, promote order by enhancing the kinetic-energy gain of thermally activated excitations.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes using the largest eigenvalue ($\lambda_{	ext{max}}$) of empirical correlation matrices as a universal, model-independent order parameter for detecting phase transitions. By analyzing how $\lambda_{	ext{max}}$ and its derivatives deviate from random matrix predictions (Wishart ensemble), the authors successfully identify critical points in both equilibrium spin systems and chaotic dynamical maps.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the methodology—using spectral properties of correlation matrices to detect critical points—is highly relevant to characterizing quantum phase transitions where entanglement or correlations drive the transition. The concept of an 'order parameter' derived from matrix spectra is a powerful general tool.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates using the largest eigenvalue of correlation matrices as an effective, model-independent order parameter to detect phase transitions in complex physical systems.

**Main result.** The behavior of $\lambda_{	ext{max}}$—specifically inflection points or maxima in its derivatives—successfully tracks known phase transitions (e.g., Ising model) and dynamical bifurcations (logistic map).

**Method.** The analysis relies on Random Matrix Theory, specifically analyzing the largest eigenvalue ($\lambda_{	ext{max}}$) of matrices drawn from Wishart ensembles.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to both equilibrium systems like the 2D Ising model and non-equilibrium dynamical systems like the logistic map. The core mathematical tool involves constructing empirical covariance matrices $C = rac{1}{T} X X^T$.

**Key observables.** Largest eigenvalue ($\lambda_{	ext{max}}$), its mean $\langle\lambda_{	ext{max}}angle$, and its derivatives with respect to control parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($k_B T/J$) for the Ising model, or the bifurcation parameter ($r$) for the logistic map.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The connection between $rac{d\lambda_{	ext{max}}}{d(	ext{parameter})}$ and thermodynamic response functions is noted as being an empirical analogy, not a guaranteed theoretical link to critical divergence.

**Figures summary.** Figures show how $\langle\lambda_{	ext{max}}angle$ exhibits sharp variations or inflection points near critical parameters (e.g., $T_c \approx 2.27$ for Ising), accompanied by maxima in variance and derivatives.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using Wishart ensembles, applies this diagnostic tool to specific physical models (Ising/logistic map), analyzes spectral statistics against RMT predictions (Marchenko-Pastur), and concludes with empirical evidence of its utility as a universal indicator for structural changes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the properties of the largest eigenvalue of correlation matrices within the framework of Wishart ensembles. In this work, we propose the largest eigenvalue as an effective empirical order parameter for detecting phase transitions in chaotic and spin systems, drawing an analogy between its derivatives and thermodynamic response functions derived from the free energy, however not necessarily linked to a critical divergence originally observed in the context of phase transitions theory.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper addresses the difficulty of dynamically realizing state transfers defined by exceptional-point braiding using only native experimental controls. The authors propose a novel constrained shortcut-to-adiabaticity principle that relaxes strict instantaneous cancellation requirements. This method successfully guides the system to the correct target endpoint state transfer while remaining robust against realistic experimental imperfections.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, physically realizable recipe for controlling non-Hermitian quantum dynamics near EPs by modifying standard adiabatic theorems to account for limited control access, which is highly relevant for superconducting circuit implementations of topological quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is realizing dynamic state transfers dictated by exceptional-point (EP) braiding using only experimentally accessible, native controls.

**Main result.** The authors propose a constrained shortcut-to-adiabaticity principle that successfully reproduces the target endpoint state transfer while remaining robust to experimental imperfections like calibration errors and bandwidth limitations.

**Method.** They introduce a protocol involving partial local cancellation of coupling channels combined with a full-cycle constraint on the residual interaction-picture flow, rather than enforcing complete instantaneous adiabatic following.

**Model / system.** The method is demonstrated using a minimal two-mode non-Hermitian model equivalent to the effective Hamiltonian used in dissipative-transmon exceptional-point experiments. The system dynamics are governed by time-dependent detuning ($\Delta(t)$) and drive amplitude ($\Omega(t)$), with loss imbalance fixing the non-Hermitian scale.

**Key observables.** State-transfer error ($\epsilon_{i,j}(t)$), transition probabilities ($P_{i,j}(t)$), and the residual interaction-picture flow ($\hat{\Phi}_I(t_0)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detuning $\Delta(t)$, drive amplitude $\Omega(t)$, relative loss imbalance ($\Gamma_0$), and the duration of the braiding loop ($t_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The available laboratory controls only span a partial set of the full dressed-frame coupling channels, necessitating the partial cancellation strategy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between ideal/uncontrolled vs. constrained dynamics (showing reduced state-transfer error), and show representative waveforms for dressing angles, native control fields ($g_x(t), g_z(t)$) that vanish at boundaries, and trajectories in the $(\Delta, \Omega)$ plane.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of standard adiabatic methods near EPs. It then introduces the constrained STA principle by analyzing the dressed-frame Hamiltonian, deriving necessary conditions for local cancellation and full-cycle flow constraints. Finally, it demonstrates the protocol's success via numerical simulation and robustness testing.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exceptional points define branch-exchange state transfers through holomorphic continuation of non-Hermitian eigenmodes, but realizing these transfers dynamically remains difficult. Slow encircling does not generally transport the full set of instantaneous eigenstates, while shortcuts to adiabaticity can require controls outside the native experimental manifold. Here, we introduce a constrained shortcut-to-adiabaticity principle for exceptional-point braiding using native controls. In a dressed instantaneous-eigenstate frame, the available controls cancel the accessible transition channels locally, while the residual channels remain active during the evolution but are constrained to have no net accumulated effect over the closed loop. The protocol therefore targets the endpoint state transfer selected by ideal adiabatic branch exchange, rather than enforcing complete local cancellation or adiabatic following throughout the trajectory. We demonstrate the construction in a minimal two-mode non-Hermitian model equivalent, up to a trace shift and a basis convention, to the effective Hamiltonian used in dissipative-transmon exceptional-point experiments, where detuning and drive amplitude provide real controls and the relative loss imbalance fixes the non-Hermitian scale. Smooth real waveforms reshape only these controls, reproduce the branch-exchange transfer, and remain accurate under calibration errors, exceptional-point uncertainty and finite-bandwidth filtering with modest overhead.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the complex magnetoresistance hysteresis in granular $	ext{Cr}_{1-x}	ext{Fe}_x	ext{O}_2$ at low temperatures. By measuring how the MR loops change with the rate of magnetic field sweeping, the authors extracted a slow relaxation time scale ($	au_{MT} \approx 3$ s). This suggests that the unusual electrical response is governed not by fast spin dynamics, but by the sluggish reconfiguration of local magnetic moments at critical intergranular contacts.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of transport-weighted dynamics, where slow magnetic relaxation dictates measurable electrical properties in disordered granular media, provides an experimental realization of non-equilibrium physics relevant to open quantum systems and glassy dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the origin of complex, nonmonotonic magnetoresistance (MR) hysteresis observed at low temperatures in granular magnetic oxides.

**Main result.** The unusual hysteretic behavior is attributed to a slow relaxation process ($	au_{MT} \approx 3$ s) governing local moment and domain configurations within transport-critical intergranular contacts.

**Method.** The authors reanalyzed MR data, focusing on the dependence of resistance extrema positions on the magnetic field sweep rate ($
u$) to extract an effective relaxation time scale.

**Model / system.** The system is granular $	ext{Cr}_{1-x}	ext{Fe}_x	ext{O}_2$ powder where electrical transport occurs through a sparse network of tunneling junctions between magnetic grains. The physics involves coupling slow, history-dependent local magnetic relaxation to the measured macroscopic resistance.

**Key observables.** Nonmonotonic MR loop shape, field shift of intermediate-field minima ($\mu_0H_{\min}(R)$), and the effective magnetotransport relaxation time ($	au_{MT}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures (e.g., 4.25 K), magnetic field sweep rate ($
u$), and the derived relaxation time scale ($	au_{MT} \approx 3$ s).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The measured resistance is strongly weighted by a small subset of 'transport-active' contacts, allowing the slow dynamics to manifest macroscopically. The MR can be modeled as a sum of fast and slowly relaxing contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show low-field MR hysteresis loops at varying temperatures and field sweep rates; one figure compares undoped vs. Fe-containing samples, highlighting enhanced nonmonotonicity with Fe doping.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from observing complex MR features to quantifying the rate dependence of these features ($\mu_0H_{\min}(R)$ shift $\propto 
u$), leading to the extraction of a characteristic relaxation time scale ($	au_{MT}$), and finally modeling this behavior using kinetic equations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Low-temperature magnetoresistance loops of compacted CrO2 powders become strongly nonmonotonic when electrical transport is dominated by a small number of spin-dependent intergranular tunnelling paths. We reanalyse previously reported data for undoped CrO2 and Fe-containing Cr1-xFexO2, focusing on additional branch crossings beyond the conventional low-field hysteresis. In the Fe-containing specimen, the resistance decreases, passes through a minimum, rises over an extended field interval, and then decreases again at higher field. This nonmonotonic hysteretic feature is strongly reduced when the magnetic-field sweep is slowed. Digitization of the three fastest sweeps shows that the field at which the resistance starts to recover shifts approximately linearly with sweep rate, corresponding to an effective magnetotransport relaxation time of about 3 s. This scale is attributed not to microscopic spin flips but to the slow evolution of local moment and domain configurations at the intergranular contacts dominating the current. The observations are consistent with conventional negative tunnelling magnetoresistance superimposed on a slower resistance-increasing contribution associated with transient local magnetic disorder. Fe increases coercivity and interfacial magnetic heterogeneity while reducing the overall negative magnetoresistance. The results support a transport-weighted magnetic-reconfiguration mechanism for the unusual hysteresis shape.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models the internal workings of large language models as interacting spin systems undergoing dynamics across finite layers. It derives an analytical criterion showing that undesirable content 'tipping' is governed by crossing specific energy barriers (basins). The findings recast unpredictable AI failure into a quantifiable, engineering-related risk based on underlying physical principles.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mapping of LLM failure modes onto concepts like dynamical first passage processes, disorder control, and phase transitions provides a powerful physical analogy for understanding emergent AI behavior in complex, high-dimensional systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates why large language models (LLMs) unexpectedly generate undesirable content ('tipping') even under deterministic decoding, despite architectural variations.

**Main result.** Tipping is shown to emerge from many-body interactions between tokens modeled as interacting spins crossing a finite-layer system boundary. This suggests AI failure modes are 'foreseeable engineering risks' rather than inherent unpredictability.

**Method.** The authors model the LLM computation using concepts from condensed matter physics, treating token interactions as spin dynamics governed by a dynamical first passage process between competing output basins.

**Model / system.** The system is an idealized, finite-layer transformer architecture where attention weights define pairwise 'spin' couplings. The dynamics are analyzed via layer propagators and residual state evolution equations.

**Key observables.** Tipping occurs when the projected residual state crosses a defined 'output-basin wall,' quantified by a gap function $x = r \cdot (D - B)$. Column entropy ($ar{S}(t)$) measures attention disorder.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical threshold is determined by the finite number of layers ($\ell$), and dynamics are controlled by Attention disorder, which dictates transport across basin boundaries.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include suppressing positional encoding, setting LayerNorm to identity, and isolating the pure-Attention mechanism for deriving explicit propagators.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate autoregressive iteration showing attention-induced tipping (Fig. 1) and schematically map out token switching points in a multi-spin system across layers (Fig. 2). Disorder entropy ($ar{S}(t)$) is shown to saturate at low disorder levels (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the core problem (tipping) to modeling it as spin dynamics, deriving a closed-form propagator for pure attention, establishing an analytical tipping criterion based on basin competition, and finally testing this theory across multiple LLM families.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Why do ChatGPT-like AIs, despite major architectural and training differences, unexpectedly tip to undesirable content (e.g. harmful, misleading, repetitive) even under deterministic greedy decoding? We show that a broad class of such tippings is caused by the many-body interactions between tokens (spins) as they cross the finite-layer system. Tipping emerges as a dynamical first passage process between competing output basins. Attention disorder controls the transport toward, away from, or along the basins' boundary. A few-basin reduction yields a closed finite-layer threshold, whose coarse-grained predictions show good agreement across ChatGPT-like families. These results suggest that a broad class of AI failures represents 'foreseeable engineering risk' rather than inherently unpredictable behavior, with important implications for legal and societal assessments of AI harm.

</details>
