**Summary.** This work develops a powerful theoretical framework linking the Operator Product Expansion (OPE), which governs microscopic CFT behavior, to Quasinormal Modes (QNMs), which describe late-time relaxation. By showing these two descriptions overlap in the complex time plane, the authors propose a 'thermal bootstrap' program that allows one set of data to constrain and predict results from the other.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on CFTs/gravity, the methodology of connecting short-distance (UV) physics constraints to long-time (IR) dynamics via spectral methods is highly relevant for open quantum systems where memory effects or thermalization rates are key.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to unify two descriptions of linear response in large-N CFTs at finite temperature: the microscopic Operator Product Expansion (OPE) and the macroscopic Quasinormal Mode (QNM) spectrum.

**Main result.** A formal map is established showing that OPE data constrains QNM asymptotics, leading to a 'thermal bootstrap programme' where both descriptions mutually constrain each other.

**Method.** The authors employ analytic continuation techniques across the complex time plane and utilize Mellin transforms to derive sum rules connecting spectral zeta functions (from QNMs) to OPE coefficients.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on large-N Conformal Field Theories (CFTs) at finite temperature, using retarded correlators as the primary diagnostic tool. The Schwarzschild-AdS$_5$ black brane serves as a key physical example.

**Key observables.** Retarded correlator $G_R(t, \vec{k})$, QNM frequencies ($\omega_n$), and OPE coefficients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite temperature ($T=1/eta$), large-$N$ limit, complex time plane structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The relationship relies on the existence of an overlapping region of convergence between the UV (OPE) and IR (QNM) descriptions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the conceptual overlap between OPE and QNM representations in the complex time plane, showing how different regimes define the correlator.

**Paper structure.** The structure moves from defining the problem via two separate expansions (OPE vs. QNM), establishing the mathematical connection through analytic continuation and Mellin transforms, deriving specific sum rules, and finally applying this framework to calculate physical quantities like fundamental mode QNMs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

At microscopic scales the linear response of thermal states of large-$N$ CFTs is governed by a thermal operator product expansion (OPE), while at large scales response is governed by collective excitations known as quasinormal modes (QNM). We show that the OPE and QNM representations of the retarded correlator in mixed time and spatial momentum coordinates have an overlapping region of convergence in the complex time plane, giving a map between OPE and QNM data. We show that large-overtone QNM asymptotics are related to OPE singularities, while low-overtone QNM data appear in analytic continuation from short to large times. Using this approach we obtain new analytic results for QNM asymptotics, and numerically obtain low-overtone QNMs from OPE data for the Schwarzschild-AdS$_5$ black brane. We further show that the OPE spectrum is intimately related to QNM data through a set of sum rules which we derive in Mellin space. Finally, using the lightcone OPE, we argue that stress tensor correlators at large spatial momentum thermalise slower as the conformal collider bounds approach saturation. This work points to a new thermal bootstrap programme where OPE and QNM data constrain each other.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models mechanical safety risks in extremely dense crowds trapped without escape routes. By coupling elastic collision dynamics with social forces, the authors quantify trade-offs between overall wall pressure and peak collision loads. The findings provide mechanistic insights into how social behaviors like cohesion and buffering dictate crowd risk profiles.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical physics, the analysis of emergent collective behavior, phase transitions (susceptibility discontinuities), and non-equilibrium steady states in a highly interacting many-body system shares conceptual overlap with critical phenomena studied in statistical mechanics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates direct mechanical hazards in dense, confined crowds that lack any means of egress, moving beyond typical evacuation safety metrics.

**Main result.** The paper establishes a critical wall-pressure/contact-load trade-off and identifies distinct phase boundaries (independent vs. grouped agents) emerging only from the coupled dynamics of elastic reorientation and social forces.

**Method.** The authors employ multi-scale modeling, combining an Elastic Reorientation Model (ERM) with a Social Force Model (SFM) to simulate agent interactions under high density.

**Model / system.** The system models N agents as rigid disks in a 2D confined domain. Interactions are governed by collision-triggered dynamics (conserving kinetic energy) and persistent social forces, incorporating parameters for group cohesion ($\gamma_g$) and wall buffering ($\gamma_w$).

**Key observables.** Macroscopic wall line pressure ($P_{	ext{wall}}$), microscopic maximum per-agent collision impulse ($\delta p_{\max}$), and susceptibility measures used to locate phase boundaries.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cohesion strength ($\gamma_g$), wall buffering strength ($\gamma_w$), and the relative influence of SFM forces (pushing/sliding) versus purely elastic collisions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The rigid disk approximation is used, neglecting body deformation; safety criteria are defined by comparing $\delta p_{\max}$ to a critical impulse $p^*$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the system domain and show how different initial condition distributions yield similar final state dynamics for the baseline elastic collision model.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the mechanical hazards, then detailing the coupled ERM+SFM framework, analyzing the resulting trade-offs ($P$-$p$), and finally using finite-size scaling to map out distinct phase boundaries associated with agent grouping and boundary effects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Crowd safety in confined venues is usually evaluated through evacuation performance or pre-collision avoidance, while direct mechanical hazards in dense gatherings without egress remain poorly understood. We study an Elastic Reorientation Model (ERM), a Social Force Model (SFM), and their coupled dynamics. Post-collision behavior is represented by social-group cohesion ($γ_g$) and wall buffering ($γ_w$), while risk is quantified by the macroscopic wall line pressure ($P_{\text{wall}}$) and the microscopic maximum per-agent collision impulse ($δp_{\text{max}}$). In the ERM, cohesion and wall buffering generally reduce $P_{\text{wall}}$ by retaining agents in the bulk, but large groups exhibit a high-$δp_{\text{max}}$ hazard window at intermediate cohesion. As $γ_g\rightarrow1$, local pairing suppresses cluster growth and shifts kinetic energy from relative to center-of-mass motion, reducing $δp_{\text{max}}$. SFM pushing and sliding amplify $δp_{\text{max}}$, especially when agent-agent and agent-wall interactions coexist, while active driving raises $P_{\text{wall}}$ through near-wall accumulation. The coupled dynamics produces a wall-pressure/contact-load ($P$-$p$) trade-off. Finite-size scaling reveals an independent-agent-induced phase boundary at $γ_w=0.5$, characterized by a susceptibility discontinuity, and a grouped-agent-induced continuous phase boundary along a finite segment of $(1-γ_w)(1-γ_g)=0.5$, characterized by divergent susceptibility and terminating at a critical point. Both disappear in the social-force-free ERM, showing that they emerge from the coupled ERM+SFM dynamics. These results provide mechanistic guidance for crowd-risk mitigation and safety planning in high-density venues without egress.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a sophisticated quantum-tomographic method to search for CP violation in $tar{t}$ production. It systematically separates potential new physics sources by analyzing two distinct classes of angular observables: polar angles (for production) and azimuthal angles involving decay planes (for decay). The framework is powerful because it provides a comprehensive diagnostic tool for future collider experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on particle physics, the methodology—using quantum state tomography to disentangle sources of non-Hermitian or CP-violating information encoded in correlations—is highly relevant to open quantum systems theory and characterizing decoherence channels.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to develop a quantum-tomographic framework to definitively determine whether observed CP-odd effects in $tar{t}$ events originate from the production mechanism, the decay vertex, or both.

**Main result.** By combining measurements of polar-angle distributions (probing production) and azimuthal observables involving $b$-lepton decay planes (probing decay), a systematic strategy is established to separate these sources of CP violation.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum state tomography applied to angular distributions derived from the factorized process $I 	o tar{t} 	o b\ell^+
u_\ell ar{b}\ell^-ar{
u}_\ell$. This involves extending standard tomography to include anomalous couplings in the $Wtb$ vertex.

**Model / system.** The system is top-antitop quark pair production ($tar{t}$) followed by leptonic decay. The framework utilizes the narrow-width approximation, factorizing the process into a production density matrix and individual top/antitop decay density matrices, which are analyzed using Effective Field Theory (SMEFT) corrections to the $Wtb$ vertex.

**Key observables.** Polar-angle distributions (reconstruct $ho_I$) and azimuthal observables involving $b-\ell$ decay planes (show sine modulations for decay CPV).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is sensitive to anomalous couplings in the $Wtb$ vertex, tested at various collider energies ($\sqrt{s}=365 	ext{ GeV}$ mentioned for $e^+e^-$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The narrow-width approximation is employed throughout the calculation. Leptons are assumed massless.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the diagrammatic representation of the full process $I ightarrow tar{t} ightarrow b \ell^+
u_\ell ar{b} \ell^- ar{
u}_\ell$.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the factorized spin-density-matrix description, then deriving decay matrices incorporating anomalous couplings, followed by deriving the angular distributions necessary for tomography, and concluding with a discussion of the combined measurement strategy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a quantum-tomographic framework for determining whether possible CP-odd effects in $t\bar t$ events originate in production, in decay, or in both. In the narrow-width approximation, the process $I\to t\bar t\to b\ell^+ν\,\bar b\ell^-\barν$ factorises into a production density matrix and top and antitop decay density matrices. We extend the standard tomography procedure to a general anomalous $Wtb$ vertex and derive the corresponding angular distributions. Polar-angle distributions retain their usual tomographic form, up to modifications of the spin-analysing powers, and can therefore be used to reconstruct the production density matrix and test its CP properties. By contrast, dedicated azimuthal observables involving the $b$--lepton decay planes contain characteristic sine modulations that provide linear probes of possible new CP-violating interactions in the decay vertex. Combining the two classes of observables gives a systematic strategy for separating sources of CP violation in production and in decay. We illustrate the resulting angular signatures for representative $t\bar t$ production scenarios at hadron and lepton colliders.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a theory for the universal cubic-in-time phase accumulated by wave packets in a constant force field, applicable from cold atoms to optics. By measuring this relative phase through interference, researchers can precisely probe the underlying force landscape and confirm theoretical predictions with high accuracy.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly sensitive, non-linear diagnostic tool—the cubic phase—that is robustly predicted across diverse physical systems (optics to atoms). It demonstrates how fundamental symmetries dictate observable quantities in quantum dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the universal cubic-in-time phase accumulated by quantum wave packets propagating under a constant external force (linear potential). This relative phase serves as a designable signal accessible through interference.

**Main result.** The theory predicts an analytically closed form for the relative cubic coefficient, which is confirmed experimentally via heterodyne demodulation of simulated interference fringes with high accuracy. The eigenforce acts as an effective antagonist to the applied force in this phase structure.

**Method.** Theoretical derivation using Feynman propagators and Airy eigenstates, complemented by numerical simulations modeling wave packet collisions (interference) and subsequent fitting of the time-dependent relative phase.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum wave packet evolving under a linear potential ($V \propto x$), which is shown to be mathematically analogous across platforms including ultracold atoms, paraxial optics, and surface-gravity water waves. The core physics is governed by Schrödinger-type equations.

**Key observables.** The relative cubic coefficient of the phase ($\Delta\varphi \propto t^3$) extracted from the interference pattern density $ho$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied force ($F_{	ext{phys}}$), intrinsic eigenforce ($F_{	ext{eigen}}$), and time ($t$). The analysis highlights specific zero lines in the force landscape.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a quasi-one-dimensional description along the propagation axis, and the measurement of the phase is limited to the region around the first interference fringe.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Airy wave packet propagation and interference patterns showing the imprinted relative phase $\Delta\varphi \propto t^3$. Specific panels demonstrate how the fitted cubic coefficient vanishes in regimes of symmetry cancellation or specific force alignments.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the universality of the cubic phase across multiple physical platforms, derives the general form of the relative cubic coefficient based on eigenforce and applied force, and then presents both theoretical analysis (identifying zero lines) and experimental/simulation results confirming the measurable signal via interference.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A quantum wave packet in a linear potential, i.e., under a constant force such as gravity, accumulates a cubic-in-time phase that is universal across Schrodinger-type platforms and naturally realized by Airy eigenstates. Because the classical action is quadratic in the force, this phase comprises exactly three contributions: intrinsic, force-induced, and a cross term. The force-induced contribution alone is shape-independent, whereas the Airy eigenstate renders the shape-dependent contributions non-dispersing. An eigenstate-based nondimensionalization identifies the eigenforce, namely the intrinsic force underlying the packet's acceleration in the absence of an applied force, as a natural parameter. As a function of both forces, the cubic coefficient takes an analytically closed and physically interpretable form that factors along two zero lines: the static Airy eigenstate and a nontrivial zero at which the phase cancels without stationarity. This exposes the eigenforce as an effective antagonist to the applied force, not only in the caustic's self-acceleration but also within the phase, while leaving the centroid unaffected in accordance with Ehrenfest's theorem. Spatially uniform within each packet, the phase cannot be measured directly and is accessible only through the relative phase of two colliding packets, each evolving in its own potential. The general relative cubic coefficient, forbidden by symmetry for identical packets and activated by preparation asymmetry, therefore provides a designable signal. Extracted through heterodyne demodulation of the simulated interference between two Airy packets, its central value agrees with the prediction to sub-percent accuracy within the fitting uncertainty. The analysis spans ultracold-atom condensates, paraxial optics, and surface-gravity water waves.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a general theoretical framework connecting bond-current susceptibility anisotropy to the resulting current topology in correlated electron materials. By analyzing three different lattices, the authors show that fluctuations near specific filling levels favor distinct real-space current patterns, such as closed loop currents or source–sink textures. This provides a powerful tool for interpreting experimental signatures of charge order and broken symmetries.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter charge order, the rigorous mathematical framework linking momentum-space susceptibilities to real-space topological currents is highly relevant to understanding emergent collective excitations in quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a direct link between bond-current form factors, susceptibility anisotropy, and the resulting real-space current topology in correlated electron systems.

**Main result.** The anisotropy of the bond-current susceptibility systematically favors ordering wave vectors that support closed loop-current states, providing a general framework for identifying such orders.

**Method.** Derivation of symmetry-allowed bond-current form factors and calculation of the Random-Phase-Approximation (RPA) susceptibility on various lattices.

**Model / system.** Correlated electron systems modeled using nearest-neighbor bond-charge interactions, studied on square, triangular, and kagome lattices.

**Key observables.** Bond-current form factors ($\gamma_k$), susceptibility anisotropy $\chi(Q)$, and real-space current topology (e.g., closed loop currents vs. source–sink textures).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling level ($\mu/t$), lattice geometry (square, triangular, kagome), and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The physical current is identified with the imaginary part of the reconstructed order parameter; analysis often focuses on the sine-form-factor representation despite acknowledging limitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate lattice structures (triangular/kagome) and show momentum-space bond-current susceptibilities for the square lattice, alongside real-space current configurations demonstrating both closed loops and source–sink patterns.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives form factors for different lattices, calculates susceptibility maxima to predict ordering vectors, and then reconstructs the corresponding real-space current topology based on these fluctuations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive all symmetry-allowed bond-current form factors generated by nearest-neighbor bond-charge interactions on square, triangular, and kagome lattices. We find that the anisotropy of the bond-current susceptibility systematically favors ordering wave vectors that support closed loop-current states, whereas symmetry-related wave vectors with weaker susceptibility generate noncirculating current textures. Near van Hove filling, this correspondence is robust across all lattice geometries considered, producing staggered flux phases on the square lattice, diamond-shaped current patterns on the triangular lattice and both chiral and nonchiral loop-current states on the kagome lattice. Our results establish a direct link between bond-current form factors, susceptibility anisotropy, and real-space current topology, providing a general framework for identifying loop-current orders and interpreting signatures of time-reversal symmetry breaking in correlated electron materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work develops a field theory for crystalline membranes coupling flexural phonons with Dirac fermions. Using Renormalization Group techniques, the authors determine that while the system can remain stable in the flat limit, instabilities at finite wavelengths lead to distinct critical points. The most complex case involves a hybrid transition characterized by the chiral-XY Gross-Neveu-Yukawa universality class.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of critical phenomena concepts (RG theory) to coupled electronic/structural degrees of freedom is highly relevant for understanding quantum phase transitions in low-dimensional materials that bridge solid-state physics and statistical mechanics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the low-energy field theory describing the coupled dynamics between nonrelativistic flexural phonons and relativistic Dirac quasiparticles in crystalline membranes.

**Main result.** It distinguishes two critical scenarios: a stable flat phase, or genuine hybrid electronic-structural critical points governed by the chiral-XY Gross-Neveu-Yukawa universality class when symmetry permits coupling.

**Method.** The analysis relies heavily on Renormalization Group (RG) flow techniques and one-loop calculations to determine the stability of different physical regimes and characterize critical exponents.

**Model / system.** The system is a crystalline membrane hosting Dirac fermions, modeled by coupled effective actions for flexural phonons and massless Dirac quasiparticles. The coupling strength and renormalization are key focus areas.

**Key observables.** Critical exponents (e.g., related to Wilson-Fisher or chiral-XY classes), renormalized elastic constants, and the flow of dimensionless coupling strengths ($ar{g}, y$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio between flexural and Dirac energy scales ($s(\ell)$), charge neutrality conditions, and symmetry constraints (e.g., horizontal reflection parity).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis separates the infrared behavior (flat phase) from potential instabilities at finite momentum ($\pm Q$), and assumes specific symmetries dictate which universality class applies.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the structure implies showing RG flow diagrams or critical exponents for different coupling regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the coupled low-energy field theory, analyzes the stability of the long-wavelength flat phase via RG flow, and then examines two distinct instability scenarios: one governed by bosonic Wilson-Fisher fixed points, and a second, hybrid case leading to chiral-XY GNY universality.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Crystalline membranes hosting Dirac fermions, with graphene as the paradigmatic example, combine two low-energy sectors with sharply different dynamics: nonrelativistic flexural phonons and relativistic-like Dirac quasiparticles. We develop the low-energy field theory of this coupled system at charge neutrality and determine how this dynamical mismatch controls the coupling between the two sectors. In the long-wavelength flat phase, rotational symmetry ties the renormalization of the leading local scalar strain--density coupling to the scale-dependent bending rigidity, causing its dimensionless strength to decrease logarithmically. At the same time, flexural modes become parametrically slower than the Dirac fermions, so the resulting fermionic feedback vanishes as a power law.The flat phase is therefore stable against this perturbation. The physics changes when elastic interactions or electronic softening destabilize the membrane at a finite wavelength, selecting a ripple pattern formed by modes at $\pm\mathbf{Q}$. For an isolated pair of ordering wavevectors, provided that commensurability-induced phase pinning is irrelevant, the transition is governed by the bosonic Wilson--Fisher fixed point, while the Dirac fermions remain spectators. A genuinely hybrid electronic--structural critical point arises instead when symmetry permits a mass-type Dirac bilinear to share the ripple's momentum and quantum numbers, including horizontal-reflection parity. The transition is then described by the chiral-XY Gross--Neveu--Yukawa universality class. Using the known one-loop critical exponents, we characterize this transition, determine the induced secondary elastic distortion, and show that the fermionic and bosonic velocities lock in the isotropic continuum limit.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a detailed stochastic model based on a multiplicative event clock to explain logarithmic aging diffusion observed in various disordered materials. By analyzing the joint consistency of multiple observables—not just one scaling curve—the authors propose a mechanism that rigorously separates this specific type of slow dynamics from simpler theoretical approximations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical framework to distinguish between different physical origins of slow dynamics in disordered systems, which is crucial for interpreting experimental measurements in glassy materials or amorphous solids.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of uniquely identifying the underlying stochastic mechanism responsible for logarithmic aging diffusion, as simple relaxation laws are insufficient.

**Main result.** A specific multiplicative event clock model is proposed that generates unique signatures—such as distinct scaling behaviors in mean-square displacement and non-converging ensemble averages—that distinguish it from other slow dynamics models.

**Method.** The authors analyze the process using an exact event-level construction based on iterating age-conditioned forward-recurrence laws, comparing this to diffusion-limit representations like Fokker-Planck equations.

**Model / system.** The model is a 'Multiplicative Event Clock' governing logarithmic aging diffusion. It applies conceptually to physical systems exhibiting slow dynamics, such as electron glasses or disordered magnetic materials.

**Key observables.** Logarithmic drift and spreading, Einstein relation under local detailed balance, ultraslow transit/target-survival laws, relative scatter of mean-square displacement ($\propto 1/\ln(T/t_0)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of event times ($U_n = \ln(T_{n+1}/T_n)$) being i.i.d., and the time scale separation between trajectory reproducibility and ensemble averages.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes the exact event-level construction from its diffusion limit, acknowledging that generalized Langevin closures might match some responses but fail to reproduce core event statistics.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the difference between one-time logarithmic relaxation (e.g., conductance) and protocol-dependent aging (e.g., TRM).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the inadequacy of simple scaling laws, introduces the multiplicative event clock via its recurrence law, derives resulting statistics for count/variance, compares this exact construction against continuum limits (Fokker-Planck), and concludes by demanding joint consistency across multiple observable types.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Logarithmic time dependences occur in many aging materials, but neither a $\ln t$ relaxation law nor a $1/t$ event rate uniquely identifies the underlying stochastic mechanism. We examine a specific log-aging process defined by iterating the age-conditioned forward-recurrence law after every event. This rule makes the event times multiplicative: the logarithmic ratios $U_n=\ln(T_{n+1}/T_n)$ are independent and identically distributed with an explicit non-exponential density. Consequently, both the mean and the variance of the event count grow linearly with $\ln(t/t_0)$, while the density of the $n$th event time has a log-normal central sector and a fixed-$n$ algebraic far tail. These clock statistics generate logarithmic drift and spreading, an Einstein relation under local detailed balance, and ultraslow transit and target-survival laws. They also separate trajectory reproducibility from ensemble--time equivalence: the relative scatter of the time-averaged mean-square displacement decays as $1/\ln(T/t_0)$, although its mean does not converge to the ensemble lag MSD. We distinguish the exact event-level construction from its diffusion-limit generalized Fokker--Planck and random-clock subordination representations, and from a generalized-Langevin closure that can match selected responses and covariances but need not reproduce event counts or rare-duration statistics. The proposed clock is therefore tested not by a single logarithmic curve, but by the joint, no-refitting consistency of multiplier, count, transport, first-passage, and finite-window observables.

</details>

The mixture in the presence of the gradient fills the space also outside the plates. The surface of the plate which is in contact with the liquid within the slab is subject to the non- equilibrium pressure pNE(L), while the other surface is subject to the pressure pNE(L →∞) (yellow arrows). Accordingly, the net pressure acting on each plate is pCas(L) = pNE(L) − pNE(L →∞).</sub>

</details>

The black solid line corresponds to Ψ(fs)(X) calculated numerically (starting from X ≃0.3), while its asymptotic behaviors given by Eq. (21) for X ≫1 and by Eq. (22) for X ≪1 are plotted with dashed green and red lines, respectively. The asymptotic expressions provide accurate approximation of the actual curve, except for 2 ≲X ≲4.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically derives and analyzes the non-equilibrium Casimir pressure arising from concentration gradients and gravity in a binary liquid mixture. Using fluctuating hydrodynamics, it shows that this excess pressure decays algebraically with separation $L$ and can be either attractive or repulsive depending on the fluid properties. The work proposes both advanced mathematical techniques for calculation and concrete experimental setups for measurement.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluid dynamics, the use of fluctuating hydrodynamics to calculate long-range correlations (Casimir forces) connects statistical mechanics principles to measurable macroscopic forces, which is relevant to understanding fluctuations in complex quantum or many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the non-equilibrium Casimir pressure generated by thermal hydrodynamic fluctuations in a binary liquid mixture subjected to both an imposed concentration gradient and gravity.

**Main result.** The excess Casimir pressure decays algebraically as $1/(k_{\mathrm{RO}}L)$ at large separations, and its sign (attractive or repulsive) depends on the thermodynamic properties of the mixture.

**Method.** Fluctuating hydrodynamics equations are employed to calculate the excess pressure using fluctuation-dissipation relations and subsequent asymptotic analysis techniques like Poisson resummation and Mellin transforms.

**Model / system.** The system is a three-dimensional slab containing an isothermal binary liquid mixture, modeled far from its consolute critical point. The non-equilibrium state is driven by a constant concentration gradient ($
abla c$) parallel to gravity ($g$).

**Key observables.** Non-equilibrium Casimir pressure $p_{Cas}(L)$, the scaling function $\Psi(k_{\mathrm{RO}}L)$, and the force magnitude.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The roll-off wavevector ($k_{\mathrm{RO}}$), the ratio of imposed to equilibrium gradients ($
abla c / 
abla c_{gr}$), and the slab thickness $L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system is assumed to be far from convective instability, and various approximations are made regarding the relative speeds of momentum versus mass diffusion.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 numerically illustrates the scaling function $\Psi_{fs}(X)$ for different asymptotic regimes ($X \gg 1$ and $X \ll 1$). Figure S1 shows experimental data relating scattered light intensity to the compressibility factor $Z(\phi)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory using fluctuating hydrodynamics, derives the scaling function $\Psi(X)$ for the Casimir pressure, analyzes its asymptotic behavior ($L^{-1}$ decay), and concludes with proposals for both theoretical analysis (Poisson resummation) and experimental measurement setups.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the non-equilibrium Casimir pressure in an isothermal binary liquid mixture maintained in a spatially constant and stationary concentration gradient parallel to gravity and confined within a three-dimensional slab of thickness $L$, bounded by two infinite plates parallel to both the gravitational field and the imposed gradient. We assume that the liquid mixture, under the same non-equilibrium conditions, occupies both the interior and the exterior of the slab. Using fluctuating hydrodynamics, we show that the resulting finite-size excess pressure on the plates is described by a scaling function of the dimensionless variable $k_{\mathrm{RO}}L$, where $k_{\mathrm{RO}}$ is the gravity-induced roll-off wavevector. At large separations, this Casimir pressure decays as $1/(k_{\mathrm{RO}}L)$. Depending on the thermodynamic properties of the mixture, the corresponding force can be either attractive or repulsive, while it vanishes for ideal solutions. Since the mixture is assumed to be far from its consolute critical point, the Casimir pressure investigated here is entirely of non-equilibrium origin and it vanishes in the absence of the imposed concentration gradient. Finally, we propose an experimental setup where this force might be measured, consisting of two optically trapped colloidal particles immersed in a dense aqueous colloidal suspension diffusing into an overlying layer of pure water, estimating the expected magnitude of the resulting force.

</details>

### [Universal Optimization and Tighter Fidelity Bounds for Approximate Quantum Error Correction](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24968v1)

**Authors:** Jing Wu, Michele Grossi, Doga Kurkcuoglu, Silvia Zorzetti

**Summary.** This paper introduces an efficient theoretical framework to calculate entanglement fidelity bounds for Approximate Quantum Error Correction. By exploiting a duality relationship and employing Principal Component Analysis, the authors overcome the exponential computational bottleneck associated with large quantum codes. This allows for high-precision analysis of error resilience in systems previously considered too complex to model accurately.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete algorithmic improvements for quantifying hardware limitations (decoherence) in complex quantum systems, directly impacting the feasibility of building fault-tolerant quantum computers.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing computationally tractable methods for Approximate Quantum Error Correction (AQEC) because standard optimization techniques scale exponentially with system size.

**Main result.** The authors establish an efficient framework using a dual representation and Principal Component Analysis (PCA) that yields a tighter analytical lower bound on entanglement fidelity, enabling high-precision optimization for previously intractable codes.

**Method.** They leverage the duality between recovery maps and environment decoupling, reformulating the maximization problem to utilize PCA dimensionality reduction techniques.

**Model / system.** The work applies to AQEC in both discrete-variable (DV) and continuous-variable (CV) quantum processors. Specific examples include analyzing GKP codes under thermal loss and Shor nine-qubit codes under amplitude damping.

**Key observables.** Entanglement fidelity ($F_e$), In-phase lower bound ($F_{	ext{IP}}$), and the computational speedup achieved by the new framework.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise parameters like thermal loss rates ($\eta$) and damping rates ($\gamma$); system size (number of qubits/modes) dictates complexity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The in-phase lower bound assumes a block-diagonal structure for certain density matrix components, and the PCA framework relies on spectral concentration to justify dimensionality reduction.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare different entanglement infidelity lower bounds (e.g., In-phase vs. near-optimal) showing superior performance for the new method; other figures demonstrate the exponential suppression of truncation error as more principal components are kept.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the theoretical framework using duality and Uhlmann's theorem, deriving the tighter in-phase lower bound, and finally implementing a PCA-based computational scheme to apply this theory to large, realistic quantum codes like GKP and Shor codes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Approximate quantum error correction (AQEC) not only dictates the performance of discrete- and continuous-variable quantum error correction codes but also serves as a unifying framework across various physical disciplines. Identifying the optimal recovery channel to maximize the entanglement fidelity via standard semidefinite programming is computationally bottlenecked by the exponentially growing number of Kraus operators with system size, rendering large-scale optimization prohibitive. While analytical near-optimal maps exist, they typically work only when the Knill-Laflamme conditions are nearly satisfied. In this Letter, we establish an efficient framework by leveraging the duality between recovery and environment decoupling. This framework yields a tighter analytical lower bound on entanglement fidelity than the conventional limit set by the transpose channel. Furthermore, by exploiting the decayed weights of noise Kraus operators, we introduce a framework based on principal component analysis to reduce the dimension. In thermal loss channels where the weights decay exponentially, our approach achieves a 33-fold computational speedup while maintaining rigorous accuracy. Our approach enables high-precision optimization for AQEC codes that were previously intractable due to the curse of dimensionality.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Delta-VQE, a variational quantum circuit technique designed to locate quantum phase transitions by measuring the energy difference between two distinct reference states. While resource-efficient, its accuracy hinges on selecting ansätze that physically represent the competing phases across the critical boundary, as general choices can lead to false positives at symmetry points.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a quantum algorithm approach to characterizing phase transitions in strongly correlated systems, which is highly relevant for understanding emergent phenomena in condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Detecting quantum phase transitions (QPTs) is computationally demanding using classical simulations, even for small systems.

**Main result.** The proposed Delta-VQE method can resource-efficiently probe QPTs, but its success critically depends on designing physically representative variational ansätze that correctly capture the competing phases across the transition boundary.

**Method.** Delta-VQE determines critical points by calculating and minimizing the absolute energy difference ($\Delta E$) between two variational quantum circuits optimized from reference states corresponding to distinct phases. This contrasts with standard methods like fidelity susceptibility.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the one-dimensional transverse-field Ising model with a three-spin cluster interaction, exhibiting various phases including Paramagnetic (PM), Symmetry-Protected Topological (SPT) cluster, Ferromagnetic (FM), and Antiferromagnetic (AFM).

**Key observables.** The primary diagnostic is $\Delta E = |E_1 - E_2|$, the absolute difference in variational energies. Other observables include order parameters and fidelity susceptibility ($\chi_F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transverse field strength ($h$) and coupling constants ($J$), which define the phase diagram and critical points ($h_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the minimum of $\Delta E$ signals a quantum critical point. A major limitation is that using general dual ansätze can incorrectly identify self-dual points rather than true QCPs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams, showing Ising transition lines and self-dual lines. They compare $\Delta E(h)$ results against fidelity susceptibility ($\chi_F$), demonstrating that $\Delta$-VQE shows less finite-size dependence when correctly implemented.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the resource challenge of QPT detection, details the Delta-VQE protocol using two variational ansätze for a cluster-Ising model, and then presents comparative results showing its efficacy versus fidelity susceptibility analysis under varying coupling strengths ($J$).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Mapping quantum phase diagrams through classical simulation is notoriously resource-intensive, as even small systems far from the thermodynamic limit demand prohibitive computational effort. The variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) offers a compelling alternative, exploiting approximate ground states to distinguish phases. An appealing proposal, dubbed as Delta-VQE, determines critical points by contrasting variational energies optimized from reference states of distinct phases. Intriguingly, the diagnostic sharpens as circuit depth decreases, highlighting its promise as a resource-conscious probe of quantum criticality. To probe the broader applicability and underlying mechanisms of this approach, we investigate the one-dimensional transverse-field Ising model with a three-spin cluster interaction, a setting in which the Ising transitions are generally situated beyond the self-dual line. We demonstrate that, whenever dual ansätze are employed, Delta-VQE invariably detects the self-dual points rather than the true criticality. In contrast, when ansätze are carefully tailored to embody the competing phases across the boundary, the genuine Ising critical point can be successfully identified with only minor finite-size effects. Our results establish that, while Delta-VQE provides a resource-efficient probe of quantum criticality without requiring precise ground-state preparation, its diagnostic power is fundamentally contingent upon the judicious selection of physically representative ansätze.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper generalizes Kirchhoff's law of thermal radiation to time-varying (Floquet) media where standard frequency relations fail. It derives a new relationship showing that emissivity is determined by a weighted sum of harmonic-resolved absorptivities. This has practical implications for designing advanced, non-equilibrium thermal emitters.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends fundamental electromagnetic theorems into the realm of non-equilibrium quantum optics, providing a powerful tool for designing novel thermal emitters whose behavior cannot be predicted by static laws.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The standard Kirchhoff's law relating thermal emission ($\epsilon$) to absorption ($\alpha$) breaks down in time-varying (Floquet) media because temporal modulation violates time-translation invariance.

**Main result.** A generalized Kirchhoff's law is derived, stating that emissivity equals a weighted sum of harmonic-resolved absorptivities of the adjoint system. This allows for calculating $\epsilon$ from $\alpha$ in these complex systems.

**Method.** The derivation uses fluctuation-dissipation theorems and Green's function techniques adapted for periodically time-varying media (Floquet theory), analyzing partial contributions across frequency harmonics.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on linear, time-varying Floquet media, including examples like modulated resonators and core-shell particle structures. The system is characterized by temporal modulation at a frequency $\Omega$.

**Key observables.** Emissivity ($\epsilon$), Absorptivity ($\alpha$), Partial harmonic contributions ($\epsilon_m, \alpha_m$), and the thermal occupation factor $\Theta(\omega, T)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Modulation frequency ($\Omega$), modulation strength (related to coupling rates), and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes linear response theory and relies on extensions of the Fluctuation-Dissipation Theorem to periodically driven systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the breakdown of standard Kirchhoff constraints in time-varying media, showing how $\epsilon$ and $\alpha$ can be significantly different for nonreciprocal or modulated structures. They also detail harmonic decomposition.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical necessity for a generalized law due to modulation, derives the relationship linking partial emissivities to adjoint absorptivities across harmonics, and then demonstrates this law's implications using specific physical models (e.g., nonreciprocal resonators).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Kirchhoff's law fundamentally relates thermal emission to absorption. For linear, static, reciprocal media, it equates the emissivity and absorptivity for each direction and frequency, while in nonreciprocal systems emission and absorption are equal when the bias is time-reversed. In time-varying media, however, temporal modulation breaks time-translation invariance, converts frequencies, and enables energy exchange with the modulation drive. As a result, a same-frequency relation between absorptivity and emissivity can no longer be expected. Here, we derive a generalized Kirchhoff's law for linear time-varying Floquet media. We show that the emissivity at a given frequency equals a weighted sum of harmonic-resolved absorptivities of the adjoint system, with weights accounting for thermal occupation and photon-flux conversion. This relation has both practical and fundamental consequences. In practical terms, it allows emissivity to be calculated from absorption, simplifying the design of time-varying thermal emitters. More fundamentally, it reveals thermal radiation regimes inaccessible in static media. In particular, we identify time-varying structures that exhibit strong emission with negligible absorption at the same frequency for all directions, yielding a near-maximal violation of the conventional form of Kirchhoff's law.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that Hollow-Core Fibers (HCFs) are superior to traditional silica fibers for coexisting quantum key distribution and classical data transmission due to their ultra-low nonlinearity. Through experiments over 18 km, they achieved a stable secret key rate while maintaining high theoretical capacity estimates. This work validates HCFs as a scalable platform for future quantum internet infrastructure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems because it addresses the critical engineering challenge of integrating fragile quantum signals with high-power classical channels in a practical fiber infrastructure, which requires detailed noise characterization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is enabling quantum key distribution (QKD) and classical data transmission to coexist reliably within a single optical fiber for future quantum networks.

**Main result.** The study successfully demonstrated entanglement-based HD-QKD coexisting with data over an 18-km Hollow-Core Fiber (HCF) link, achieving an average Secret Key Rate of 10.56 kbps and predicting rates above 135 kbps over 200 km using state-of-the-art HCFs.

**Method.** The authors employed time-encoded High-Dimensional Quantum Key Distribution (HD-QKD) protocols, characterizing noise sources like Raman scattering by comparing performance in HCF versus conventional Single-Mode Fiber (SMF).

**Model / system.** The physical platform is the Hollow-Core Fiber (HCF), which guides light predominantly in air, offering intrinsically ultra-low nonlinearity compared to Pure Silica Core Fibers (PSCFs) that suffer from noise like Raman scattering and four-wave mixing.

**Key observables.** Secret Key Rate (SKR), Coincidence-to-Accidental Ratio (CAR), Quantum Bit Error Rates (QBERs), and theoretical data capacity (up to 2.3 Tbps).

**Important parameters / regimes.** 18-km link length, $0 	ext{ dBm}$ bidirectional received power, time-encoded dimension ($d=8$), and predicted SKR $> 135 	ext{ kbps}$ over $200+	ext{ km}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical analysis relies on the availability of state-of-the-art low-loss HCFs to maintain high performance over long distances.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare SMF and HCF performance, illustrate the experimental setup for simultaneous quantum/classical transmission, and show noise power spectral density and coincidence counts vs. classical channel wavelength.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the coexistence problem in standard fibers to proposing HCFs as a solution; it details experimental measurements of noise suppression and QKD performance over 18 km; finally, it provides theoretical scaling analysis predicting long-distance capabilities using low-loss models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The coexistence of quantum information and classical signals in a single fiber is essential for future quantum networks that leverage the well-established optical fiber infrastructure. Although multiplexing technologies can separate quantum and classical signals, pure silica core fibers (PSCFs) remain fundamentally limited by the high nonlinearity, which generates substantial Raman scattering and four-wave mixing noise. Hollow-core fibers (HCFs), guiding light predominantly in air, offer an attractive solution with intrinsically ultra-low nonlinearity and strongly suppressed nonlinear noise. In this work, we demonstrate the entanglement-based key coexisting with data over an 18-km HCF link. We achieve time-encoded high-dimensional quantum key distribution (HD-QKD) carrying 0 dBm of bidirectional received power, corresponding to a theoretical data capacity of up to 2.3 Tbps. During 24 hours of continuous operation, an average secret key rate (SKR) of 10.56 kbps is obtained. Theoretical analysis further predicts SKRs above 135 kbps over transmission distances exceeding 200 km using state-of-the-art low-loss HCFs. These results show significantly improved performance compared with PSCF-based systems and highlight the potential of HCFs for scalable quantum-classical coexistence compatible with the architectures of established fiber-optic networks.

</details>

### [Maximal complementarity in the n-qubit Pauli group](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24988v1)

**Authors:** Markus Frembs, Giovanni Natale, Christopher S. P. Wever, Philipp A. Hoehn  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.24988v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **3/5** · `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.24988_figures/2607.24988_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Sketch of a bi-regular cut of ̃P○ n with N1 = 3, N2 = 4 and ∣CS(Q ∈S) −1∣= 2. Vertices connected by straight lines commute with each other. (Not all vertices and edges included).</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the concept of maximal complementarity for observables generated by Abelian subgroups within the n-qubit Pauli group. It develops algebraic criteria to characterize when these degenerate observables are mutually complementary. The main achievement is proving that such maximal sets enforce strong, quantifiable entropic uncertainty relations on quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools to quantify fundamental quantum limitations (like complementarity and uncertainty) in highly structured systems like multi-qubit Pauli measurements, which is crucial for understanding quantum error correction and measurement theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates maximal complementarity for observables associated with the n-qubit Pauli group, extending this concept from non-degenerate to degenerate (coarse-grained) observables.

**Main result.** It establishes criteria for two such observables to be complementary and proves that these maximal complementary sets yield strong entropic uncertainty relations.

**Method.** The analysis uses algebraic methods based on the structure of Abelian subgroups within the Pauli group, relating complementarity to commutant conditions and purity invariants.

**Model / system.** The system is an n-qubit quantum system whose observables are derived from the Pauli group ($	ext{P}_n$). The focus is on Abelian subgroups ($	ilde{A}, 	ilde{B}$) of this group, which govern the degenerate observables studied.

**Key observables.** Complementary observables $\{O_{\mathcal{A}_k}\}$, Purity Invariants ($I_\psi(S)$), and Entropic Uncertainty Bounds ($H_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n$), the dimension ($m$) of the Abelian subgroups, and the cardinality of maximal complementary sets ($\approx 2^n+1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on the known structure of the Pauli group and its Abelian subgroups, particularly when relating complementarity to incidence structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining strong complementarity for observables associated with Abelian subgroups. It then derives necessary and sufficient conditions (Theorem 1) for two such subgroups to be complementary. Subsequent sections relate these sets to purity invariants and finally derive the resulting entropic uncertainty relations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Observables in quantum mechanics are generally complementary, that is, they reveal mutually incompatible pieces of information about a given system. This property is not only a fundamental tenet of the quantum formalism, but also a key component in quantum cryptographic protocols. As such it has fuelled much research into finding sets of highly complementary observables. In its strongest form -- the one we consider in this work -- the information between complementary observables is not merely incompatible but mutually exclusive: maximal information about one observable implies no information about the other, and vice versa. Maximal sets of non-degenerate complementary observables are known to exist in systems of prime power dimension such as n-qubit systems. Here, we study complementarity of degenerate observables, specifically we prove that observables associated with the n-qubit Pauli group also exhibit complementarity under this restriction: first, we obtain a criterion for two such observables to be complementary and, second, we relate maximal sets of complementary observables with informational pure state complementarity equalities, further studied in two companion papers. Equivalently, these results can be formulated in terms of maximal complementary sets of (not necessarily maximal) Abelian subgroups of the Pauli group linking with (possibly coarse-grained) mutually unbiased bases. Finally, we prove that these complementarity sets entail strong entropic uncertainty relations.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper numerically analyzes quasiparticle-induced dissipation mechanisms in Fluxonium superconducting qubits. It challenges prior assumptions that certain operating points are immune to energy loss, demonstrating sensitivity even when considering small numerical factors. The findings guide future qubit design by highlighting the critical role of gap asymmetry and specific environmental radiation energies for achieving robust quantum coherence.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses decoherence in solid-state quantum hardware by providing quantitative insights into how material properties (like gap asymmetry) dictate the vulnerability of qubits to environmental noise sources like QPs.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quasiparticle (QP)-induced dissipation limits the performance of superconducting quantum processors, challenging existing theoretical assumptions about qubit protection.

**Main result.** The authors show that fluxonium qubits are sensitive to QP-induced errors at bias points previously thought to be protected, and $T_1$ protection from photon-assisted tunneling only occurs for a specific radiation energy.

**Method.** Numerical simulation using Fermi's golden rule to calculate energy relaxation rates ($\Gamma$) arising from both direct QP tunneling and Photon-Assisted Tunneling (PAT) processes.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Fluxonium qubits, modeled by a Hamiltonian involving Josephson Junctions. The dissipation mechanisms are analyzed via coupling terms derived from the QP tunneling Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}_t$) and PAT interaction ($\hat{H}_{	ext{PAT}}$).

**Key observables.** QP-induced energy relaxation rate ($\Gamma$), coherence time ($T_1$), and spectral functions $S_{QP,J}^{\pm}$ and $S_{	ext{PAT}}^{\pm}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting gap energies ($\Delta_L, \Delta_R$) and their asymmetry ($\delta\Delta$), external magnetic flux bias ($\Phi_{ext}$), and QP energy distributions (hot vs. cold).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on Fermi's golden rule and makes approximations regarding the relative magnitudes of QP energies compared to the superconducting gap, though it challenges some initial assumptions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate QP density of states, tunneling processes (electron vs. hole), coherence factors ($C_\pm$) dependence on bias/asymmetry, and matrix elements versus applied flux bias.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of QP dissipation in superconducting qubits. It then details the theoretical framework using Fermi's golden rule for calculating rates from both direct tunneling and PAT processes, culminating in specific predictions regarding protection mechanisms based on gap asymmetry and radiation energy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonequilibrium quasiparticles (QPs) generated by stray infrared and ionizing radiation can limit the performance of superconducting quantum processors and present challenges for quantum error correction schemes. Models of QP-induced energy relaxation commonly assume that the characteristic energy of the QPs and the qubit transition energy are both small relative to the superconducting gap. Under these assumptions, certain qubits such as the fluxonium would exhibit protection against QP-induced dissipation at specific bias points. Here, we show that this is not necessarily the case, numerically analyzing the predicted rate of QP-induced dissipation in fluxonium qubits for different QP energy distributions and for QPs created via photon-assisted tunneling processes. We find that accounting for small numerical factors, existing theoretical models predict sensitivity to QP-induced errors at bias points previously thought to be protected. We find that inclusion of asymmetry in the superconducting gap energy across the junction can reintroduce suppression of QP-induced relaxation, as expected. Additionally, for QPs created by photon-assisted tunneling, we predict that $T_1$ protection will only occur for a specific energy of pair-breaking radiation. This understanding of fluxonium sensitivity to QP-induced dissipation informs the development of fluxonium-based processors and future QP-mitigation strategies.

</details>

**Summary.** This review comprehensively surveys chiral magnons, which exhibit non-reciprocal spin wave propagation due to broken momentum-inversion symmetry. It unifies the understanding of their origins—from Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions to altermagnetism—and details advanced theoretical tools like non-Hermitian physics. The work guides materials science toward realizing next-generation, low-power magnonic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between symmetry breaking mechanisms (like altermagnetism) and emergent directional quantum phenomena provides a rich platform for studying topological aspects in magnetic systems, bridging condensed matter theory with spintronics engineering.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses chiral magnons, which are collective spin excitations characterized by a broken momentum-inversion symmetry ($\omega(\mathbf{k}) 
eq \omega(-\mathbf{k})$), leading to non-reciprocal spin-wave propagation.

**Main result.** Chiral magnon physics is unified across mechanisms—including SOC-DMI and altermagnetism—providing pathways for low-dissipation, non-reciprocal devices distinct from topological magnonics.

**Method.** The review synthesizes theoretical analysis using symmetry group theory, model Hamiltonians, and spin-wave theory with experimental data from INS, PNS, and transport measurements like SNE/SSE.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on magnetic ordered systems (e.g., $\alpha$-MnTe, CrSb) where chirality arises either from broken spatial inversion symmetry or through altermagnetism in the weak SOC limit. The framework extends to non-Hermitian dynamics involving gain and loss.

**Key observables.** Momentum-inversion asymmetry ($\omega(\mathbf{k}) 
eq \omega(-\mathbf{k})$), non-reciprocal propagation (NRP), chiral splitting ($\Delta\omega$), and lifetime asymmetry ($\Gamma(\mathbf{k}) 
eq \Gamma(-\mathbf{k})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature stability ($T_N > 300 	ext{ K}$), energy scales (meV range), and damping parameters ($\alpha < 0.01$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on symmetry analysis, spin-wave theory approximations, and the assumption that chiral effects can be engineered or observed in various material classes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate theoretical origins (e.g., Rashba-Dresselhaus analogue for magnons) and show magnon dispersion relations exhibiting directional splitting ($	ext{Fig. 2}$), alongside diagrams detailing multiphysics couplings like magnon-photon interaction ($	ext{Fig. 9}$).

**Paper structure.** The review is structured by first defining the problem (NRP via chiral magnons), then systematically covering theoretical origins (DMI vs. Altermagnetism/SSG framework), followed by detailed discussions of experimental diagnostics and advanced topics like non-Hermitian dynamics, concluding with future device applications.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chiral magnons are distinctive collective spin excitations in magnetic ordered systems, whose dispersion relations break momentum-inversion symmetry, $ω(\boldsymbol{k}) \neq ω(-\boldsymbol{k})$, resulting in essential non-reciprocal spin-wave propagation. This built-in directionality provides new opportunities for spin information transfer, thermal-spin interconversion, and low-dissipation non-reciprocal microwave devices, which complement but differ from topological magnonics. In recent years, the proposal and rapid development of altermagnetism have broadened the physical origin and research framework of chiral magnons, making them a research frontier in condensed matter physics. This review presents a unified framework for chiral magnons, covering symmetry-breaking mechanisms, material implementation, experimental characterization, transport response, and many-body non-Hermitian dynamics, and evaluates routes toward room-temperature and device-related platforms. The discussion is based on symmetry analysis, model Hamiltonians, and spin-wave theory, combined with first-principles calculations as well as recent spectroscopic (e.g., inelastic and polarized neutron scattering, Brillouin light scattering) and transport measurements. This review further summarizes bulk-gap and Berry-curvature induced chiral magnon edge states, the enhancement of non-reciprocity via chiral spin pumping and cavity-magnon hybrids, as well as non-Hermitian features arising from multiparticle damping and gain-loss competition. This review provides a comprehensive reference for elucidating the underlying mechanisms of chiral magnons, advancing the synthesis and experimental characterization of novel materials, and also guiding the design of next-generation non-reciprocal magnonic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously analyzes the limits of Interaction-Free Measurement (IFM) by comparing its performance to direct probing using estimation theory. It finds that while IFM offers no advantage when estimating a continuous parameter like transparency, it provides a significant, quantifiable advantage when determining if an object is present at all. The key takeaway is that the benefit lies in discrimination against null hypotheses, not in precise measurement of loss.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous theoretical boundaries on how much information can be extracted from non-destructive measurements, which is crucial for designing next-generation quantum sensors in optics and fundamental physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper quantifies the advantage of Interaction-Free Measurement (IFM) compared to direct probing for quantum sensing tasks, specifically distinguishing between parameter estimation and hypothesis testing.

**Main result.** For estimating transparency ($T$), IFM offers no advantage over direct probing; however, a significant advantage exists when discriminating between an object's presence versus its complete absence.

**Method.** The analysis uses estimation-theoretic tools like Fisher information (for parameter estimation) and Chernoff information (for hypothesis testing), comparing results for multi-pass interferometers against single-pass probes.

**Model / system.** The primary system is the Elitzur-Vaidman interferometer or general fixed multi-pass arrangements where a single photon interacts sequentially with an absorbing object characterized by transmissivity $T$.

**Key observables.** Fisher Information ($F$), Chernoff information ($C$), and the ratio of quantum Fisher information ($\mathcal{F}_Q$) to purified state information ($\mathcal{F}_{	ext{pur}}^Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transmissivity $T$, number of Zeno cycles ($N$), and parasitic loss per cycle ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the object is memoryless, non-dispersive, and that the comparison holds for fixed multi-pass arrangements (though an advantage exists for null detection).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare Fisher information ratios between MZI/Zeno chains vs. direct probing; one figure contrasts asymptotic growth rates for conclusive interaction-free answers versus continuous parameter estimation.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares the quantum advantage of IFM across different tasks: first showing no gain for estimating $T$, then demonstrating a superior, growing advantage ($N \ln N$) when testing against object absence, and finally establishing general bounds on this advantage.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interaction-free measurement infers the presence of an absorbing object from a photon that, in the counterfactual sense, never interacted with it, and is widely described as a route to minimally invasive sensing. We ask what it actually buys, in estimation-theoretic terms. Written as a channel-estimation problem, the Elitzur-Vaidman interferometer carries exactly half the Fisher information about the object's transmissivity that direct transmission probing does, and the two schemes deliver identical Fisher information per absorbed photon. For measuring how transparent something is, the interferometer buys nothing. The advantage lies in discrimination, and we show that what it requires is not that the object be opaque but that the competing hypothesis be the object's absence. Against empty space the Chernoff information per absorbed photon grows almost linearly, as the number of Zeno cycles times its logarithm, even for a weakly absorbing object; between two partial transparencies it does not grow at all. Parasitic loss in the cycle caps the advantage. The number of conclusive interrogations per absorbed photon reaches a maximum inversely proportional to the loss per cycle, at an optimal cycle number that is likewise inversely proportional to it, which identifies the loss per cycle as the figure of merit governing how far interaction-free sensing can be pushed. Finally, the negative result is not special to the interferometer. For a single photon meeting a memoryless, non-dispersive object any number of times through arbitrary fixed optics, the accessible quantum Fisher information never exceeds that of the same incident flux spent on independent single-pass probes, and is generically far below it. This recovers the bound of Massar, Mitchison and Pironio for this class, by a short argument that also identifies when it is tight.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel basis-resolved covariance framework to statistically characterize low-energy wavefunctions in the Kitaev–Heisenberg model. By analyzing how local spin, bond correlations, and plaquette fluxes co-vary across the phase diagram using PCA, the authors map out the quantum state's statistical geometry. This reveals that the complexity of the wavefunction structure changes fundamentally when moving into exotic Kitaev spin liquid regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the use of advanced statistical tools like covariance geometry provides a generalizable method for characterizing complex quantum states beyond simple symmetry breaking, which is relevant across many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to characterize the organization of low-energy many-body wavefunctions in strongly correlated quantum systems, particularly where conventional order parameters fail.

**Main result.** The covariance geometry varies significantly across the phase diagram: ordered phases are 1D, Kitaev regimes show intrinsic multidimensionality, and this geometry depends on the physical observable used for probing.

**Method.** A basis-resolved covariance framework is developed using Principal Component Analysis (PCA) applied to feature vectors derived from local-spin, bond-correlation, and plaquette-flux operators.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the spin-$1/2$ Kitaev–Heisenberg model on a honeycomb lattice. The Hamiltonian interpolates between Heisenberg and Kitaev limits via coupling parameters J and K.

**Key observables.** Covariance geometry (quantified by Shannon entropy and Participation Ratio), principal covariance modes, local-spin expectation values, bond correlations, and plaquette fluxes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interaction parameter $\phi$, which tunes the system across various quantum phases including magnetically ordered states and Kitaev spin liquid regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that constructing feature vectors from specific physical operators (local-spin, bond, flux) provides a statistically meaningful representation of the wavefunction's structure for PCA.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the honeycomb lattice structure and show covariance spectra/principal components across different phases; Tables summarize Shannon entropy and Participation Ratio values for various representations.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the framework by defining basis-resolved feature spaces using multiple physical operators, calculates the ensemble covariance matrix over the phase diagram, and analyzes its statistical geometry using PCA, entropy, and participation ratio to map out phase transitions statistically.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a basis-resolved covariance framework for investigating the organization of low-energy many-body wavefunctions in the spin-$1/2$ Kitaev--Heisenberg model. By constructing covariance matrices from local-spin, bond-correlation, and plaquette-flux representations of the low-energy states, Principal Component Analysis (PCA) is employed to identify the dominant collective covariance modes. We find a systematic evolution of the covariance geometry across the phase diagram: magnetically ordered phases are described by an essentially one-dimensional covariance manifold, conventional magnetic phase boundaries exhibit competition between leading covariance modes, whereas the Kitaev regimes develop intrinsically multidimensional covariance geometry. Furthermore, the same many-body wavefunction produces distinct covariance geometries in different operator representations, demonstrating that covariance geometry is determined jointly by the quantum state and the physical observables used to probe it. Shannon entropy and the participation ratio provide quantitative measures of this evolution. The present framework establishes basis-resolved covariance geometry as a complementary statistical perspective for characterizing frustrated quantum many-body systems beyond conventional order parameters.

</details>

**Summary.** This review details how quantum teleportation is crucial for building a global quantum internet by enabling long-distance transfer of quantum states using entanglement. It analyzes the necessary components, such as entanglement swapping and quantum repeaters, required to overcome physical limitations like photon loss in optical fibers. The work surveys current experimental progress across diverse hardware platforms toward scalable quantum networking.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a comprehensive overview of the physical implementations required for scaling quantum information protocols, touching upon hardware integration across different domains like photonics, solid-state qubits, and atomic systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is realizing a global-scale quantum internet, which requires robust methods for distributing quantum resources over long distances.

**Main result.** Quantum teleportation, enabled by entanglement swapping and quantum repeaters, provides the essential mechanism for state transfer necessary to build such a network across various physical platforms.

**Method.** The review synthesizes protocols involving quantum teleportation (using Bell State Measurements) and quantum repeater chains to overcome photon loss over long-haul links.

**Model / system.** The discussion covers multiple architectures, including single-link vs. multi-link setups, utilizing platforms ranging from optical fibers and atomic memories to satellite/balloon relays for global connectivity.

**Key observables.** Teleportation fidelity, entanglement distribution range, and transmission loss scaling are key performance indicators.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The achievable distance is fundamentally limited by photon loss; quantum repeaters aim to change this from exponential to polynomial scaling.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes the feasibility of high-fidelity entanglement generation and measurement across diverse physical media (e.g., light $\leftrightarrow$ matter).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the basic schemes for quantum teleportation, entanglement swapping, and six fundamental network architectures required for building quantum communication links.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining quantum teleportation as essential to discussing entanglement swapping, then detailing various experimental architectures (six types), and finally focusing on quantum repeaters and future scalability challenges.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum networks play a pivotal role in quantum information science, which not only provide a secure communication platform for remote access to quantum computers but also serve as the strategic core for achieving large-scale quantum information processing, forming the foundational infrastructure for the future global-scale quantum internet. Quantum teleportation, which enables the transmission of unknown quantum states over long distances by employing quantum entanglement together with classical communication, is essential for the distribution of quantum resources in the construction of the global-scale quantum internet. To realize a global-scale quantum internet, quantum repeater protocols represent one of the most promising approaches for enabling quantum communication between any nodes. This concise review presents representative experimental demonstrations of quantum teleportation for constructing quantum networks across different physical platforms. Along this trajectory, the review discusses current challenges, open issues, and future perspectives toward scalable and practical quantum internet.

</details>

**Authors:** Yi-Ting Tu, Yang-Zhi Chou, Yi Huang, Sankar Das Sarma
