**Summary.** This paper theoretically explores how stripe potentials interact with nontrivial quantum geometry to dictate the superconducting pairing symmetry in 2D metals. By solving the gap equation, the authors predict a competition between $p_x$-wave (nodal) and $p_y$-wave (fully gapped) superconductivity. The results offer a pathway to control exotic superconducting states using engineered lattice potentials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical framework for engineering superconducting pairing symmetry using engineered periodic potentials (stripes) in systems with intrinsic geometric properties (Berry curvature). This concept of 'controlling' superconductivity via geometry is highly relevant to topological materials and advanced quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the combination of nontrivial quantum geometry (Berry curvature) and an applied periodic stripe potential influences the superconducting pairing symmetry in a 2D single-flavor metal.

**Main result.** Two distinct pairing states, longitudinal $p_y$-wave and exotic transverse $p_x$-wave, can be stabilized, with their competition controlled by electron density and Berry curvature effects. The $p_x$ state exhibits a nodal line at $k_x=0$, while the $p_y$ state is fully gapped.

**Method.** The analysis involves solving the linearized gap equation (LGE) in the quasi-one-dimensional limit, deriving effective interactions via projection onto the lowest subband, and comparing eigenvalues for different pairing symmetries.

**Model / system.** The model is a 2D single-flavor electron system subjected to both a constant Berry curvature ($B>0$) and a real-space stripe potential $U(x, y) = -2U_0 \cos gx$. The physics is analyzed in the quasi-1D limit by restricting calculations to the Fermi surface.

**Key observables.** Pairing symmetry ($	heta$), superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), and gap structure (nodal lines vs. full gaps).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Stripe strength parameters ($A g^2, B g^2$) and the ratio of Fermi momentum to stripe period ($k_F/g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the weak coupling limit (small $T_c$), the strong-stripe approximation (projecting onto the lowest subband), and initially assumes real symmetry for pairing channels.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing the preferred pairing form ($	heta$) in parameter space $(A g^2, B g^2, k_F /g)$ and show $T_c$ dependence on interaction strength for different pairing symmetries.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the physical model (Hamiltonian with stripe potential), deriving an effective low-energy interaction ($V_q$), solving the linearized gap equation in the quasi-1D limit to find candidate pairing channels ($\lambda_{p_x}, \lambda_{p_y}$), and finally mapping out the phase diagram controlling the transition between these symmetries.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study how the interplay between nontrivial quantum geometry and an applied stripe potential affects superconductivity in a two-dimensional single-flavor metal. Assuming a weak contact attractive interaction and focusing on the lowest subband in the presence of a strong stripe potential, we analytically derive two possible pairing states in the quasi-one-dimensional limit. In addition to the conventional longitudinal $p_y$-wave order (with the stripes along the $y$ direction), we find that an exotic transverse $p_x$-wave order can be stabilized. The competition between these two orders is controlled by the electron density of each stripe and the Berry-curvature-dressed interaction. Notably, the transverse $p_x$ wave order develops a nodal line at $k_x=0$, while the longitudinal $p_y$ order is fully gapped. We discuss the possible experimental probes distinguishing these orders. Our results establish a way of controlling the pairing symmetry through a stripe potential, predicting superconductivity with nontrivial quantum geometry.

</details>

### [Charge-6e superconductivity from doping SU(3) spin liquids](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25909v1)

**Authors:** Yan-Qi Wang, Boran Zhou, Hui Yang, Zhi-Qiang Gao  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.25909v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `exotic spin models in light-matter` **3/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.25909_figures/2607.25909_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>

Colors label the lowest energy ansatz: trimer (black), Φ = 0 (green), |Φ△| = π/3 CSL with C = ±1 (blue), |Φ△| = 2π/3 CSL with C = ±2 (orange), and Φ = π (red). White markers in (a) denote the φ = 0 boundaries K3/J = 0.417 and 5.955 of Ref. [52]. Calculations use a 24 × 24 unit-cell lattice.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper explores how doping SU(3) symmetric quantum spin liquids can realize exotic charge-6e superconductivity. Using advanced techniques like parton constructions on a Hubbard model, the authors demonstrate that symmetry breaking and fractionalization cooperate to stabilize these higher-charge superconducting states. It offers a powerful conceptual link between topological order in frustrated magnets and unconventional pairing mechanisms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly detailed, theoretically rigorous framework connecting higher-rank symmetries (SU(3)) and fractionalization mechanisms to predict unconventional superconductivity, which is crucial for understanding high-temperature superconductors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes doping SU(3)-symmetric spin liquids as a novel theoretical route to achieve exotic charge-6e superconductivity, generalizing previous work on charge-4e SC.

**Main result.** Doping mechanisms in SU(3) quantum spin liquids can lead to various superconducting states, including chiral and non-Abelian charge-6e superconductors by pairing fractionalized fermionic trions.

**Method.** The study employs complementary parton constructions, mean-field ansatz calculations, and analysis of topological field theories (like Chern-Simons terms) applied to doped Hubbard models.

**Model / system.** The primary model is a bilayer triangular-lattice Hubbard model possessing SU(3) spin symmetry. The theory analyzes the low-energy physics resulting from doping this system across different quantum spin liquid phases ($\mathbb{Z}_3$, Abelian/non-Abelian CSLs).

**Key observables.** Superconducting charge quantization (e.g., 6e), Hall conductance ($\sigma_H$), chiral central charge ($c_-$), and the nature of topological order.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping concentration ($x$), interlayer exchange coupling ($J_\perp^T$), and various symmetry-breaking parameters defining the spin liquid type (e.g., $	ext{SU}(3)_1, 	ext{SU}(3)_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on parton constructions and mean-field approximations to map out complex ground states and superconducting order parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 (from Chunk 4 notes) illustrates the phase diagram of an effective SU(3) spin model, showing transitions between trimer, U(1) spin liquid, and various chiral spin liquid phases as a function of temperature and coupling ratios ($K_3/J$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the physical model (Hubbard Hamiltonian with SU(3) symmetry), then using parton constructions to analyze doping effects in specific quantum spin liquids ($\mathbb{Z}_3$, CSLs), culminating in the identification of charge-6e superconducting phases.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose doping $SU(3)$-symmetric spin liquids as a route toward charge-$6e$ superconductivity. This generalizes the idea of constructing charge-$4e$ superconductivity from doped $SU(4)$-symmetric phases. As a concrete platform, we study a bilayer triangular-lattice Hubbard model with $SU(3)$ spin symmetry and interlayer antiferromagnetic exchange. Using complementary parton constructions, we analyze doped $\mathbb{Z}_3$ quantum spin liquid and $SU(3)$-related chiral spin liquids. Doping a $\mathbb{Z}_3$ quantum spin liquid can produce an orthogonal metal with a gauge invariant fermi surface of charge-$3e$ fermionic trions. Pairing these trions gives a time-reversal-symmetric charge-$6e$ superconductor. Doping Abelian $SU(3)_1$ and $SU(6)_1$ chiral spin liquids yields chiral charge-$6e$ superconductors with and without residual Abelian topological order, respectively. Doping a non-Abelian $SU(3)_2$ chiral spin liquid leads to a non-Abelian chiral charge-$6e$ superconductor intertwined with $SO(3)_{-3}$ topological order and supporting non-Abelian $h/(6e)$ superconducting vortices. We also identify several other phases, including $\mathbb{Z}_3$ orthogonal metal, quantum anomalous Hall (crystal) phases enriched by $\mathbb{Z}_3$ or $\mathbb{Z}_2$ topological order, $SU(3)$-breaking charge-$2e$ superconductors, composite fermi liquid coupled to non-Abelian gauge field, and descendant chiral spin liquids. Our results identify doped $SU(3)$ spin liquids as a natural setting where symmetry, fractionalization, and topology cooperate to produce charge-$6e$ superconductivity.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel, efficient method to calculate real-time correlation functions in interacting quantum spin systems by combining Pauli Propagation with low-rank time extension. This overcomes the severe limitation of operator basis explosion inherent to direct simulation methods. The technique allows for reliable probing of complex dynamics and spectral features in 1D and 2D quantum magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work presents an advanced computational toolkit that tackles the fundamental bottleneck of real-time quantum simulation—the exponential growth of operators—by merging established methods (PP) with modern data analysis techniques (low-rank extrapolation).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is the efficient computation of real-time two-point time-ordered correlators, which are essential for linking microscopic quantum theory to measurable macroscopic observables.

**Main result.** By combining short-time Pauli propagation with low-rank extension methods based on positivity conditions, the authors extend correlation function calculations far beyond the direct computational time window while maintaining spectral accuracy.

**Method.** The core method involves using Pauli Propagation (PP) to evolve operators in the Heisenberg picture, which is then augmented by time extension techniques that exploit the low-rank nature of the dynamics to overcome the exponential growth of Pauli strings. Ground states are approximated using Clifford-Augmented Matrix Product States (CAMPS).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on one- and two-dimensional quantum spin systems, specifically utilizing the Heisenberg model Hamiltonian ($H = J \sum \mathbf{X}_i \cdot \mathbf{X}_{i+1}$). Analyses cover both ferromagnetic and anti-ferromagnetic regimes.

**Key observables.** Time-dependent correlation functions $C(t)$, dynamical structure factor $S(\mathbf{k}, \omega)$, and the static staggered moment $m_s$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pauli string truncation parameters ($\epsilon$, $w$), MPS bond dimensions ($D$), and system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the correlation functions exhibit a low-rank structure, allowing extrapolation from short-time data; it also relies on truncating the Pauli operator basis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the computational strategy combining PP with CAMPS and low-rank fitting. They show finite-size scaling of excitation spectra ($\omega(\mathbf{k})$) for 2D AFM systems, demonstrating convergence to theoretical limits.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a combined numerical framework: first establishing ground state approximations via CAMPS, then using Pauli Propagation for short-time dynamics, and finally applying low-rank fitting/extension techniques to extrapolate results to longer times and higher resolutions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pauli propagation has shown promise for classically simulating quantum dynamics by evolving observables directly in the Heisenberg picture. In this work, we investigate its use for computing real-time two-point time-ordered correlators in one- and two-dimensional quantum systems. One major limitation of Pauli propagation is the rapid growth in the number of Pauli strings beyond short times. We overcome this limitation by combining accurate short-time Pauli-propagation data with time extension methods based on a positivity condition and the observation that the dynamics are often dominated by a small number of characteristic frequencies. This combined approach extends correlation functions far beyond the directly accessible time window while avoiding the exponential proliferation of Pauli operators. We demonstrate that the resulting correlators retain the relevant dynamical and spectral information. Our results broaden the regime in which classical methods can reliably probe the real-time dynamics of interacting quantum many-body systems.

</details>

Here MF corresponds to the MC data from Meijer and Frenkel [35], and x = DRY, RT, SMSA, and Var approxima- tions, as discussed in the text.</sub>

**Summary.** This work applies a variational approach to determine the thermodynamics of Yukawa fluids, which model plasmas and colloidal suspensions. By using hard-sphere fluid properties as a reference, the authors accurately calculate excess energy, entropy, and pressure even in strongly coupled regimes. The method is particularly useful for predicting phase boundaries, such as the melting curve, where experimental or simulation data are lacking.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical plasma physics, the use of advanced statistical mechanical techniques (variational bounds) to predict phase transitions in strongly coupled systems is relevant for understanding quantum many-body ground states and critical phenomena.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is calculating the excess energy, entropy, and pressure of a strongly coupled Yukawa fluid.

**Main result.** The variational approach provides accurate estimates for these thermodynamic properties, successfully extending predictions into regimes where numerical data are scarce or unavailable.

**Method.** A variational approach based on the Bogoliubov inequality is employed, using the hard-sphere fluid as a reference system to bound and estimate the excess free energy.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Yukawa fluid, characterized by screened Coulomb repulsive interactions $\phi(r) = \Gamma/r \exp(-\kappa r/a)$. This model is relevant for dusty plasmas and colloidal suspensions.

**Key observables.** Excess free energy ($f_{ex}$), excess entropy ($s_{ex}$), excess pressure ($p_{ex}$), and the melting curve location ($\Gamma_m(\kappa)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling parameter ($\Gamma$), screening parameter ($\kappa$), and the variational parameter ($\eta$). The strong coupling regime is a key focus.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on approximations for the radial distribution function (e.g., Percus-Yevick theory) and assumes that the effective packing fraction at freezing approaches the hard-sphere limit as screening increases.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare calculated thermal energy, compressibility factor ($Z$), and effective packing fraction ($\eta_{fr}$) against Molecular Dynamics simulations and theoretical scaling laws, showing convergence towards known asymptotes.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the variational formalism for excess thermodynamic quantities, applies it to calculate specific observables like $u_{th}$ and $s_{ex}$, and then uses these results to extrapolate the melting curve into highly screened regimes ($\kappa$ large).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The excess energy, entropy, and pressure of a strongly coupled Yukawa fluid are calculated from the variational approach using the fluid of hard spheres as a reference system. As in the case of the one-component plasma, the Percus-Yevick virial entropy is appropriate for such calculations and delivers remarkable agreement with available results from molecular dynamics simulations. The agreement with the molecular-dynamics results is particularly impressive in the strongly coupled regime, making this approach a useful predictive tool when numerical data are scarce or not yet available. As an application of the variational approach, we estimate the location of the melting curve in the regime of sufficiently strong screening.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously benchmarks optical receivers—comparing continuous-variable, photon-counting, and hybrid detectors—to determine their impact on certified randomness extraction. Using SDPs, the authors show that receiver choice critically affects security bounds, demonstrating that hybrid schemes offer advantages at low energy while CV methods excel at moderate energy.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous, information-theoretic bounds on achievable randomness in practical quantum communication settings by focusing on the measurement apparatus's limitations, which is crucial for designing robust quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper benchmarks how different optical receivers (CV, photon-counting, hybrid) affect the security analysis of quantum protocols, particularly for randomness certification.

**Main result.** Hybrid receivers perform optimally at low energy when retaining beacon labels, while continuous-variable receivers yield the highest certified entropy at moderate energies compared to other methods.

**Method.** The authors use observed receiver statistics $P(b|x)$ within a prepare-and-measure framework to derive an exact bound on the min-entropy $H_{\min}(B|X, \Lambda)$ using Semidefinite Programming (SDP).

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum states prepared by a source characterized by its Gram matrix and measured by uncharacterized optical receivers. The analysis is framed within semi-device-independent security proofs.

**Key observables.** $H_{\min}(B|X, \Lambda)$ (certified min-entropy), $P(b|x)$ (observed conditional probabilities), and the adversary's guessing probability ($P_{	ext{guess}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mean photon number bound ($\mu$), energy constraint ($	ext{Tr}[\hat{ho}_x \hat{n}] \le \mu$), and phase drift ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The security analysis assumes the side information $\Lambda$ is classical, and the source model is fixed by its Gram matrix or constrained by an energy bound.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the prepare-and-measure protocol with energy bounding. Figure 14 compares distinguishability indices for different receivers over varying mean photon number ($\mu$) and phase drift ($	heta$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general framework using SDPs to bound min-entropy, analyzes specific receiver types (photon counting vs. CV/hybrid), derives quantitative results comparing their performance across energy regimes, and discusses implications for various quantum protocols.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The choice of optical receiver determines which properties of the transmitted states remain visible in the observed data and therefore affects the performance of different quantum protocols. We compare continuous-variable, photon-counting and hybrid receivers within the same prepare-and-measure framework, using semi-device-independent randomness generation as the main case study. The measurement device is left uncharacterised, while the source is described by the Gram matrix of its pure signal states using an energy-derived overlap constraint, a magnitude-Gram benchmark or the full complex Gram matrix of a certified coherent phase-shift-keyed constellation. Within this framework, the observed receiver statistics are used to bound $H_{\min}(B|X,Λ)$ against classical side information correlated with the measurement device but independent of the input. For a fixed Gram matrix, this bound is obtained from an exact semidefinite program, with complex multi-input cases treated in block-real form and checked through the corresponding dual certificate. Photon counting alone is phase blind for fixed-modulus phase encoding and therefore certifies no worst-case randomness. Continuous-variable receivers give the highest certified entropy at moderate energy, while under the nominal source calibration a hybrid receiver performs better at low energy when the beacon-region label is retained in the output. The same receiver statistics also provide receiver-level comparisons for discrete-modulated continuous-variable quantum key distribution, quantum reading, covert communication and quantum-signature verification, without replacing the full security analysis required for each protocol.

</details>

### [An algebraically closed family of informational n-qubit purity invariants](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24987v1)

**Authors:** Markus Frembs, Giovanni Natale, Christopher S. P. Wever, Philipp A. Hoehn  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.24987v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **3/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.24987_figures/2607.24987_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Venn diagram showing the set structure of the rele- vant group elements defining the sets in Def. 1.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a generalized theory for finding purity invariants characterizing $n$-qubit pure states, extending concepts from two qubits. It achieves this by defining specific sets within the Pauli group based on maximal complementarity structures rather than just anti-commuting operators. The result provides a systematic classification and enumeration of these fundamental quantum state constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep structural insights into the geometry of high-dimensional quantum state spaces by identifying conserved quantities (invariants) that are fundamentally linked to the underlying symmetry group structure ($	ext{PSU}(2^n)$).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors aim to find a general family of state-independent invariants for $n$-qubit pure states that successfully generalizes the known 'pentagon identities' from the two-qubit case.

**Main result.** They introduce a new family of quadratic invariants derived from maximal complementarity structures within the Pauli group, proving these sets constitute purity invariants for arbitrary $n$. The number of such distinct invariants is systematically counted.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced algebraic techniques involving the structure of Pauli expectation values and identifying specific structured subsets ($J$) of the Pauli group based on commuting/anti-commuting generators.

**Model / system.** The system under study consists of $n$-qubit quantum states, whose geometry is characterized by the pure state manifold $	ext{CP}^{2^n-1}$. The diagnostics are built upon the Pauli group $	ilde{\mathbb{P}}_n$ and the Brukner-Zeilinger information measure.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the purity invariant $I_\psi(S)$, which must remain constant for all pure states $\psi$. Other key elements include the expectation values of Pauli operators ($\alpha_P$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits, $n$, is the main parameter governing complexity; results are explicitly derived and compared for $n=2$ and $n>2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on specific algebraic closure relations concerning sets defined by maximal Abelian subgroups within the Pauli group structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a Venn diagram detailing the set structure defining the invariant set $J$; later sections contain large tables explicitly listing the 28 distinct Pauli string sets for the $n=3$ case.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the need for generalization beyond simple anti-commutation rules (failure at $n>2$). It then defines the invariant set $J$ based on complementarity structures, proves its invariance property using algebraic tools, and finally enumerates and catalogs these invariants for small systems like $n=3$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a family of quadratics in Pauli expectation values, and prove that they constitute state-independent invariants for all n-qubit pure states. This family generalises the two-qubit `pentagon identities', discovered in the reconstruction programme of [P. A. Höhn, Quantum 1, 38 (2017), P. A. Höhn and C. S. P. Wever, Phys. Rev. A 95, 012102 (2017)], where they characterise the space of pure states, as well as the unitary group, and are interpreted as complementarity equalities in the Brukner-Zeilinger information measure. The generalisation to arbitrarily many qubits is nontrivial as it requires new tools which in turn reveal novel structural properties that are absent in the two-qubit case. A thorough analysis of these properties, and their relation with mutual unbiasedness and complementarity in the n-qubit Pauli group, can be found in two companion papers.

</details>

### [Bipartite Bound Information Exists](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25838v1)

**Authors:** Jef Pauwels, Nicolas Gisin, Renato Renner  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.25838v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **3/5**

<img src="2607.25838_figures/2607.25838_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Resource analogy and proof mechanism. (a) Bound entanglement and bound information express the same operational irreversibility: positive creation cost (entanglement cost EC, formation cost Iform) with zero distillable yield (distillable entanglement ED, secret-key rate S). (b) In our source (Table I), a latent bit L is correlated with (X, Y ) through Eq. (5) and passed through two noisy channels. Eve’s observation Z reveals L half of the time and is blank otherwise. The variable J, held by nobody, is L flipped with probability 1/5. Given J, the variables X and Y are independent, so an eavesdropper holding J would preclude any key. Because 1</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2607.25838_figures/2607.25838_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. One state family, two classicalizations. Top: the qutrit states σα = 2 7 ΠΦ + α</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proves the existence of 'bound information,' which is the classical counterpart to bound entanglement. It demonstrates that certain shared classical correlations cost resources to create but yield no extractable secret key. This finding establishes a resource analogy, showing that such phenomena are not exclusive to quantum mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends the concept of resource theory beyond pure quantum entanglement into classical correlations, providing a rigorous mathematical framework for understanding non-quantum forms of 'hidden' or costly information.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if a classical analogue of bound entanglement exists: a correlation that costs secret bits to create but from which no secret key can be extracted.

**Main result.** The authors prove the existence of such 'bound information' using an explicit source distribution, demonstrating that positive intrinsic information can exist even when the distillable key rate is zero.

**Method.** The analysis involves comparing three quantities—secret-key rate ($S$), intrinsic information ($I_{\downarrow}$), and formation cost ($I_{	ext{form}}$)—using techniques from information theory applied to tripartite source distributions.

**Model / system.** The problem is modeled using a tripartite source distribution over finite alphabets, specifically involving two bits and one trit. The analysis draws analogies between quantum bound entanglement (qutrits) and classical correlations.

**Key observables.** Secret-key rate $S(X; Y \parallel Z)$, Intrinsic Information $I(X; Y \downarrow Z)$, Formation Cost $I_{	ext{form}}(X; Y | Z)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis uses parameters $\alpha$ governing a family of qutrit states, and the source is defined over finite alphabets (e.g., two bits and one trit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analogy established holds at the level of resources, not necessarily for individual quantum states or their measurement outcomes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the resource analogy ($	ext{EC} > 0, 	ext{ED} = 0$ vs. $I_{	ext{form}} > 0, S = 0$). Figure 2 shows a family of qutrit states $\sigma_\alpha$ illustrating different entanglement regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the analogy by first establishing the classical bound information using an explicit source (Table I). It then analyzes this phenomenon in relation to quantum systems, examining specific families of qutrit states ($\sigma_\alpha$) and proving that measurements on these states can yield positive intrinsic information despite zero distillable key.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bound entanglement is an extreme irreversibility of quantum theory: certain states cost entanglement to create, yet no singlet can be distilled from them. Twenty-five years ago, Gisin and Wolf asked whether classical cryptography admits the same phenomenon. Are there correlations, shared by two parties and an eavesdropper, that cost secret bits to create although none can be extracted? We show that such bound information exists and give an explicit example, a distribution of two bits and a trit. The proof exploits a gap between two ways of comparing eavesdroppers: one can be better informed than another in every mutual-information comparison and nevertheless unable to simulate the other's data. We further prove that the distributions which motivated the conjecture, standard-basis measurements of bound-entangled qutrit states, are not themselves examples: a secret key is extractable from them whenever their creation costs any secrecy. Other measurements of their purifications, in contrast, do yield bound information, even for the separable states among them. The analogy is thus one of resources, not of individual states and their measurement outcomes.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates the mechanism behind long-range photocarrier transport in topological insulator nanoribbons by comparing measurements under an external electric field against free-carrier theory. By observing that the required field to suppress photocurrent far exceeds theoretical limits for free charges, and by showing diffusivity violations in time-domain measurements, the authors conclude that excitons—a charge-neutral correlated state—are responsible for the observed transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a clear experimental distinction between single-particle transport and collective, correlated excitations (excitons) using field-dependent optical probes, which is highly relevant for understanding strongly interacting quantum matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine the physical mechanism responsible for anomalously long-range photocarrier transport observed in topological insulators at cryogenic temperatures.

**Main result.** The evidence strongly indicates that this long-range photoresponse is not carried by free charge carriers but rather by a charge-neutral correlated state, such as an exciton condensate.

**Method.** The researchers combined Scanning Photocurrent Microscopy (SPCM) and ultrafast Transient Photovoltage (TPV) measurements, using the application of a transverse electric field as a discriminating probe.

**Model / system.** The physical system is bulk-insulating Sb-doped Bi2Se3 nanoribbons. The analysis compares experimental results against theoretical models for both free charge carrier diffusion/drift and exciton dissociation dynamics (Poole-Frenkel type).

**Key observables.** Photocurrent suppression threshold field ($E_{th}$), photocarrier diffusivity, and the ratio $(D/\mu)/(k_B T/q)$ derived from TPV measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cryogenic temperatures (e.g., 12 K), transverse electric fields ($\sim 33 	ext{ mV}/\mu	ext{m}$ vs $\sim 0.7 	ext{ mV}/\mu	ext{m}$ benchmark), and excitation fluence.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the transverse field acts as a discriminator between charged (deflected) and neutral (undeformed) carriers, and that the exciton dissociation follows a Poole-Frenkel type relationship.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate SPCM maps showing field dependence of photocurrent suppression, comparisons to free carrier simulations, and TPV analysis plots detailing photovoltage decay and diffusivity ratios.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the anomalous transport using SPCM (field dependence), then providing independent confirmation via TPV measurements (diffusivity violation), and finally synthesizing these results across temperature and fluence dependencies to rule out free carriers in favor of excitons.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photoexcited charge carriers in topological insulators display anomalously long-range transport at cryogenic temperatures, but the underlying mechanism remains under debate. Here we use a transverse electric field as a discriminating probe of photocarrier dynamics in bulk-insulating Sb-doped Bi2Se3 nanoribbons, combining scanning photocurrent microscopy (SPCM) with ultrafast transient photovoltage (TPV) measurements. SPCM shows that suppression of the photocurrent requires a transverse electric field nearly 50 times larger than that needed to deflect free carriers, yet comparable to the expected exciton dissociation field. TPV measurements at 12 K and low excitation fluence reveal that the photocarrier diffusivity exceeds the value implied by the Einstein relation and the measured drift mobility by more than an order of magnitude, a bound that holds even at the lower end of the fitting uncertainty. Together, these observations indicate that the long-range photoresponse is not carried by free charge carriers but by a charge-neutral correlated state.

</details>

### [Aharonov-Casher phase in twisted bilayer graphene](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25660v1)

**Authors:** Igor Kuzmenko, Y. B. Band, Yshai Avishai  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.25660v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the Aharonov-Casher phase experienced by electrons in twisted bilayer graphene under an external electric field. The authors find that this quantum phase exhibits sharp peaks precisely at the material's magic angles. This provides a novel, measurable signature to probe the topological properties and electronic structure of correlated 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between topological phases (Berry curvature/AC phase) and electronic structure features like flat bands at magic angles provides a powerful diagnostic tool for characterizing correlated electron systems in 2D materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the Aharonov-Casher (AC) phase acquired by an electron in twisted bilayer graphene (TBG) when subjected to a perpendicular electric field.

**Main result.** The AC phase is predicted to be linear with the applied electric field and exhibits sharp peaks precisely at the magic angles, where the renormalized Fermi velocity vanishes or changes significantly.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving an effective low-energy Hamiltonian using perturbation theory in the moiré potential and calculating the resulting gauge-dependent AC phase factor via a gauge transformation.

**Model / system.** The system is Twisted Bilayer Graphene (TBG) lying in the x-y plane, modeled by incorporating kinetic energy, a Moiré potential, and Rashba spin-orbit coupling. The low-energy physics is described by an effective Hamiltonian involving Dirac fermions.

**Key observables.** Aharonov-Casher phase ($\phi_{AC}$), which is measured via the probability $P(	heta)$ of tunneling into a polarized tip.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The perpendicular electric field ($E$), the twist angle ($	heta$), and the renormalized Fermi velocity $	ilde{v}_F(	heta)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes small twist angles ($	heta \ll 1$ rad) and relies on approximations derived from perturbation theory in the moiré potential.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of the renormalized Fermi velocity $v_F(	heta)$ on $	heta$, showing vanishing points at magic angles, and plot the AC phase $\phi_{AC}(	heta)$ versus $	heta$ and $E$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical system (TBG with perpendicular E-field) to deriving the low-energy effective Hamiltonian incorporating spin-orbit coupling. It then calculates the resulting gauge potential, leading to the prediction of the AC phase dependence on $	heta$ and $E$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Aharonov-Casher (AC) effect is a quantum mechanical phenomenon in which the wave function of a particle with a magnetic moment moving in a region subject to an electric field develops a phase shift due to spin-orbit interaction, even if no classical force acts on it. This phase also depends on the medium through which the particle moves. Here we focus on the AC phase of an electron moving in twisted bilayer graphene (TBG) lying in the $x$-$y$ plane, subject to a uniform electric field perpendicular to the plane of the graphene, ${\bf E}=E{\hat{\bf z}}$. The AC phase is determined by an $SU(2)$ vector potential ${\bf A}$ from which a phase factor is generated, and used to perform a gauge transformation of the Hamiltonian. We find that the AC phase is linear with $E$ and exhibits sharp peaks at the magic angles.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes strongly-connected percolation on directed lattices, determining that its critical behavior belongs to a unique universality class different from standard undirected percolation. Using advanced simulation techniques and graph algorithms, the authors compare various bond arrangements and establish formal equivalences between different lattice models. The work is significant for understanding connectivity thresholds in anisotropic or directional physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical percolation, the rigorous mathematical analysis of connectivity thresholds and universality classes provides foundational tools applicable to understanding phase transitions in complex quantum systems or network reliability studies.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates percolation on lattices with directed bonds, specifically focusing on the properties of strongly-connected clusters.

**Main result.** Strongly-connected percolation exhibits critical behavior in a universality class distinct from traditional undirected percolation, although all tested bond arrangements fall into the same universality class.

**Method.** The authors employed extensive numerical simulations and developed advanced graph algorithms (like Tarjan's) to calculate high-precision values for critical exponents and thresholds.

**Model / system.** The model is bond percolation on two-dimensional square lattices with directed bonds, including specific arrangements like the Manhattan lattice, L-lattice, random diode, and square ice models.

**Key observables.** Percolation thresholds ($p_c$), critical exponents, fractal dimensions, crossing probabilities, and the hull dimension ($d_h$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond occupation probability ($p$) and lattice size ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study focuses on cases where bonds point in a single direction, excluding bidirectional (resistor-diode) configurations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate strongly-connected clusters for various models; one figure maps bond percolation on the L-lattice to site percolation on a Manhattan lattice at 45 degrees.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the definition of strong connectivity, details algorithmic approaches (Tarjan's algorithm) for cluster detection, presents results comparing critical exponents across different directed lattices, and discusses formal mappings between models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study percolation on lattices with directed bonds, focusing on the behavior of strongly-connected percolation clusters -- clusters in which every site is reachable from every other along a directed path. We consider the two-dimensional square lattice and various globally isotropic arrangements of the directions of the bonds. Performing simulations using a range of algorithmic approaches, we calculate high-precision values for critical exponents, fractal dimensions, crossing probabilities, and percolation thresholds for bond percolation with each bond arrangement. We find that the critical behavior is in a distinctly different universality class from that of traditional undirected percolation, but that all bond arrangements appear to fall in the same universality class.

</details>

### [QCOEM: Quantum Cloud Orchestration with Evolutionary Multi-Objective Optimization](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25358v1)

**Authors:** Tam N. Pham, Hoa T. Nguyen, Quan Le-Trung  
**Type:** experiment · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.25358v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `QC/QI experiment` **2/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.25358_figures/2607.25358_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Fig. 1. System overview of the proposed QCOEM framework. Periodic QNode heartbeats asynchronously update the scheduler’s cached state; each orchestration round uses this state to compute a batch assignment, which is then dispatched to QNodes for FIFO execution.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QCOEM, a framework that uses multi-objective evolutionary optimization to schedule quantum tasks across noisy, heterogeneous cloud hardware. By jointly optimizing mean completion time, error rate, and load balance, it significantly outperforms simple heuristics. This work provides a crucial tool for achieving stable, high-fidelity execution in practical quantum cloud computing settings.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on cloud orchestration rather than fundamental physics, the core challenge involves optimizing complex resource allocation under realistic noise constraints, which has implications for designing robust quantum control protocols or understanding system-level decoherence effects in large arrays of qubits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Dynamically orchestrating quantum workloads across heterogeneous cloud backends while managing time-varying noise profiles, qubit topologies, and queue congestion.

**Main result.** The QCOEM framework achieves about 30% higher mean fidelity than noise-agnostic heuristics and eliminates task rescheduling in a simulated environment.

**Method.** It formulates the problem as a Multi-Objective Optimization Problem (MOOP) solved using NSGA-II/III, followed by preference selection via an Augmented Achievement Scalarization Function (AASF).

**Model / system.** The system models a heterogeneous quantum cloud environment composed of multiple Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) era Quantum Processing Units (QPUs), managed via a centralized scheduler.

**Key observables.** Mean Task Completion Time, Mean Error Rate (Fidelity), Load Imbalance (standard deviation of completion times), and Scheduling Overhead.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Calibrated hardware snapshots (error rates, coupling maps), user priority weights ($w$), and batch sizes ($M$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The problem is NP-hard, necessitating heuristic approximation via MOEAs; it assumes stable hardware snapshots and FIFO queueing on QNodes.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare performance across different optimization objectives (Load-Focus vs. Quality-Focus) against baseline heuristics, illustrating trade-offs between time efficiency, fidelity, and task distribution.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the QCOEM framework architecture, details the MOOP formulation with three objectives, presents the use of NSGA-II/III for Pareto front approximation, and concludes with extensive performance evaluation comparing its results against classical scheduling methods.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum cloud platforms need to dynamically orchestrate workloads across heterogeneous quantum computation backends whose noise profiles, qubit topologies, and queues vary over time. Existing orchestrators use noise-agnostic heuristics that ignore backend-specific errors, causing reduced execution fidelity, load imbalance, and frequent rescheduling. To address these challenges, we propose QCOEM - a Quantum Cloud Orchestration framework that leverages Evolutionary algorithms for Multi-objective optimization of quantum task scheduling. We compare NSGA-II and NSGA-III for jointly minimizing mean completion time, execution error rate, and load imbalance. To select schedules from a non-convex Pareto front, we apply an Augmented Achievement Scalarization Function (AASF) as a preference-based decision rule that maps the Pareto set to a single dispatchable schedule aligned with user priorities. Our extensive performance evaluation in a heterogeneous quantum cloud environment shows zero task rescheduling and about 30% higher mean fidelity than noise-agnostic heuristics, while maintaining bounded scheduling overhead. The experiment results indicate that our QCOEM framework can deliver stable, high-fidelity execution and lightweight resource management for quantum cloud computing.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel approach to fault-tolerant distributed quantum computing that bypasses resource-intensive state distillation. By exploiting an engineered error bias in photonic links—where phase errors are common but bit-flips are rare—the required ancillary qubits per node are drastically reduced. This makes the performance limited by the high-quality local memory rather than the noisy communication channel.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides concrete, resource-efficient protocols for scaling quantum computation across physically separated modules, directly impacting the feasibility of large-scale quantum networks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving fault-tolerant distributed quantum computation while minimizing the required number of ancillary qubits per node.

**Main result.** By engineering a communication error bias (phase errors dominant), the need for dedicated state distillation can be avoided, significantly reducing resource overhead and relaxing link fidelity requirements.

**Method.** The authors design syndrome-extraction circuits that exploit this phase noise bias, allowing simple measurement repetition to suppress errors using the error-correcting code itself rather than complex purification protocols.

**Model / system.** The system models distributed quantum nodes interconnected via optical links. Each node utilizes high-fidelity nuclear spins for data qubits and low-coherence electron spins for communication qubits.

**Key observables.** Logical error rate, error correction thresholds (e.g., Honeycomb code threshold up to pz ~ 11%), and the required number of ancillary qubits per node.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Phase error probability ($p_z$), residual noise strength ($p_{	ext{res}}$), and the ratio between these two error types, which defines the exploitable bias.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the dominant noise contribution is phase noise affecting generated Bell pairs, allowing it to be treated as a measurement error rather than propagating directly to data qubits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate implementing Floquet QEC protocols using minimal ancillary qubits and show how repeating measurements significantly raises the threshold for phase errors in both Honeycomb and Hyperbolic codes compared to single measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theory by first identifying the resource bottleneck (distillation) and proposing a solution based on error bias. It then analyzes performance thresholds using specific codes (Floquet/stabilizer) and discusses stability improvements through measurement repetition, concluding with detailed circuit implementations for state preparation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Distributed quantum computing interconnects small, high-quality nodes through optical links, but this architecture carries a pronounced asymmetry: in-node gates and measurements are cheap and high-fidelity, whereas inter-node communication relies on a low-coherence communication qubit and faulty photonics. Previous approaches overcame the noisy link by placing several high-quality data qubits in each node and consuming them for Bell pair and GHZ state distillation. Here we show that distillation can be avoided altogether. The key observation is that we can engineer a communication error bias, where photonic Bell pairs suffer frequent phase errors but only rare bit-flip errors. We design the syndrome-extraction circuits so that this phase noise appears solely as a measurement error that does not propagate to the data qubits, and is therefore suppressed by simply repeating the measurement; letting the error-correcting code itself, rather than a dedicated distillation subroutine, to purify the link. This dramatically reduces the need for ancillary nuclei: Floquet codes require only a single data qubit per node, while general stabilizer codes require just one additional ancilla. We demonstrate high error-correction thresholds throughout this regime, and we identify lattice surgery as inherently robust for this setting, enabling logical operations at a threshold close to that of quantum memory. As a result, the performance of the quantum computer is limited by the high-quality data qubits, while the requirements on photon indistinguishability and coherence of the communication qubit are substantially relaxed.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces TempLat, a versatile C++ engine designed for high-performance lattice field theory simulations in arbitrary dimensions. It uses advanced techniques like expression templates and Kokkos to ensure portability across CPUs and GPUs while maintaining compile-time optimization. The framework successfully benchmarks complex physics models, demonstrating state-of-the-art scaling performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on lattice QFT, the underlying technology—a highly portable, expression-template-based framework for massively parallel computation—is directly relevant to simulating complex quantum many-body systems beyond standard Hamiltonians.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The need for an efficient, versatile, and high-performance software framework capable of simulating lattice field theories across various physical regimes.

**Main result.** TempLat provides a dimension-agnostic C++ engine that achieves excellent strong and weak scaling on both CPU clusters and modern GPUs by decoupling mathematical expressions from hardware implementation.

**Method.** The framework uses expression templates to translate high-level mathematical expressions almost verbatim into highly optimized, device-specific C++ kernels, supporting hybrid MPI/shared-memory parallelization via Kokkos.

**Model / system.** It is designed for lattice field theories, simulating systems ranging from QCD and early-universe dynamics ($	ext{SU}(2)$ Yang–Mills) to classical statistical models like the $\phi^4$ theory. It supports various gauge groups and complex field types.

**Key observables.** Wall-clock time per lattice site per step; scaling behavior (strong/weak); average values of fields ($\langle \phi angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice dimensions ($d$), coupling constants ($\lambda$), simulation time steps ($dt$), and hardware resources (CPU cores, GPU count).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core engine assumes that assignment triggers the necessary evaluation across the lattice; performance comparisons highlight differences between pencil vs. slab decomposition strategies for DFTs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate scaling behavior for $SU(2)$ YM fields on GPUs, comparing TempLat to HILA; Figure 7 benchmarks ParaFaFT's FFT performance against established libraries (FFTW3/cuFFTMp) across various parallelization schemes.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the framework by detailing its mathematical abstraction capabilities and hardware portability. It then demonstrates applications through benchmarking diverse physical systems, including scalar field dynamics ($\phi^4$) and gauge theories ($SU(2)$ YM), culminating in detailed performance analyses of core components like DFTs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present TempLat, a C++ framework for lattice field theory simulations in arbitrary dimensions. Its symbolic language, built on expression templates, translates mathematical expressions almost verbatim into C++ while compiling to highly optimized kernels, in contexts ranging from classical-statistical to Monte Carlo simulations. TempLat is performance-portable: building on Kokkos, it supports large CPU clusters, as well as NVIDIA and AMD GPUs. Its abstraction of hardware into a device concept makes TempLat extensible to future architectures. We demonstrate excellent strong and weak scaling on both CPUs and GPUs. We also present ParaFaFT, a standalone parallel discrete Fourier transform library supporting arbitrary dimensions on all of the above hardware, and benchmark its scaling. Both are open source and available on GitHub, and power a new release of CosmoLattice, a widely used early-universe simulation library now available on GPUs.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an advanced bosonic Ring Polymer Molecular Dynamics (RPMD) method to calculate real-time correlation functions for interacting bosons. This technique bypasses the severe dynamical sign problem inherent in direct quantum simulations. It successfully enables the first direct simulation of finite-temperature, real-time dynamics for large bosonic condensed phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, non-perturbative tool for simulating real-time quantum dynamics in bosonic many-body systems, overcoming major limitations associated with path integral methods.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing a method to accurately approximate real-time correlation functions and quantum transport coefficients for bosonic condensed phases.

**Main result.** The authors developed a bosonic Ring Polymer Molecular Dynamics (RPMD) method that enables the direct simulation of finite-temperature, real-time dynamics for large bosonic systems using RPMD for the first time.

**Method.** They extend standard RPMD by incorporating bosonic exchange symmetry into the classical ring polymer Hamiltonian and utilizing the Kubo-transformed correlation function estimator.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on bosonic condensed phases, benchmarked on harmonic and anharmonic model systems, and applied specifically to the Lieb-Liniger gas (1D bosons with contact interactions).

**Key observables.** Real-time correlation functions (e.g., density-density), quantum transport coefficients, and the Dynamic Structure Factor (DSF).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($eta$), time ($t$), momentum transfer ($k/k_F$), and the number of ring polymer beads ($P$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is exact in the harmonic, short-time, and high-temperature limits; it relies on the assumption that correlated observables are symmetric under particle exchange for bosons.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare bosonic vs. distinguishable particle dynamics (e.g., position autocorrelation), showing how the bosonic symmetry manifests dynamically, and illustrate the accuracy of the method across different anharmonic potentials.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general problem (sign problem in real-time quantum simulation) before developing the specific bosonic RPMD formalism. It then benchmarks this method on simple models (harmonic/anharmonic traps) to validate its theoretical limits and finally applies it to complex systems like the Lieb-Liniger gas.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a method for approximating real-time correlation functions and quantum transport coefficients of bosonic condensed phases. The direct evaluation of quantum real-time correlation functions in the path integral formulation is impossible due to a severe dynamical sign problem. Evaluating imaginary-time correlation functions and inverting them using analytic continuation is famously ill-posed. Ring polymer molecular dynamics (RPMD) provides an alternative approach resulting in accurate approximations for real-time correlation functions of condensed phases but neglects exchange effects. In this work, we develop a bosonic RPMD method, which is exact in the harmonic, short time, and high temperature limits. A critical enabling observation is that the Kubo-transformed correlation function for bosons is real only when the correlated observables are symmetric under exchange. We benchmark the method on harmonic and anharmonic model systems and then use it to directly obtain the Lieb-Liniger gas density-density real-time correlation functions at various temperatures and momentum transfers. This work enables the direct simulation of finite-temperature, real-time dynamics of large bosonic condensed phases using RPMD for the first time.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents OmniQEC, a novel AI framework designed to discover practical Quantum Error-Correcting codes by simulating their performance on real quantum hardware. It employs a synergistic slow-fast loop where LLMs coordinate code generation and circuit evaluation. The results demonstrate that this physically informed approach yields superior error suppression compared to traditional methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the gap between theoretical code design and physical implementation feasibility in quantum computing. The use of LLMs as an 'AI scientist' to guide complex scientific discovery is highly relevant to open quantum systems research that requires integrating multiple simulation/optimization modules.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge of discovering Quantum Error-Correcting (QEC) codes that are truly practical for deployment, as performance depends on the complex interplay between code structure, hardware constraints, syndrome extraction, and decoding.

**Main result.** OmniQEC discovers QEC codes that show steadily improving logical error suppression compared to standard reference codes when physical qubit budgets increase. The framework proves that circuit-level evaluation is superior to traditional code-level metrics for assessing practical performance.

**Method.** The authors introduce OmniQEC, an AI scientist system orchestrated by LLMs that uses a slow–fast synergistic workflow. This combines fast, inexpensive code-level screening with a slow, physically grounded circuit-level evaluation loop.

**Model / system.** The focus is on QEC codes, specifically evaluating qLDPC construction families, designed for implementation on modern quantum processors. The process models the full cycle from abstract code design to physical circuit execution and decoding.

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rate (LER) measured via $-\log_{10}(	ext{pLER})$, alongside code-level metrics like logical qubits ($k$), distance ($d$), and $\Phi_{code}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Total physical-qubit budget ($N$), qubit connectivity, and the specific LLM backend used for orchestration.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that an iterative, AI-guided search process can systematically navigate the vast design space to find hardware-friendly codes. A key limitation noted is the computational expense of the slow, circuit-level evaluation loop.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the dual-loop workflow (fast proxy screening $ightarrow$ slow circuit evaluation $ightarrow$ evidence feedback). Figure 2 shows a performance frontier comparing discovered codes against reference codes based on LER vs. qubit budget. Figure 3/4 detail error suppression metrics and cost analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the OmniQEC framework, detailing its LLM-orchestrated, dual-loop methodology (fast proxy search feeding into slow circuit simulation). It presents comparative results showing outperformance over benchmarks, validates the necessity of the full loop, and concludes with future directions for multi-agent system integration.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error correction (QEC) is indispensable for scalable fault-tolerant quantum computing. However, discovering QEC codes that remain effective is challenging, as logical performance depends on the interplay between code structure, hardware, syndrome extraction, and decoding, which often impose competing requirements. Here we introduce OmniQEC, an efficient AI scientist for discovering QEC codes suited to deployment on modern quantum processors. OmniQEC formulates QEC design as an iterative discovery process in which an orchestrator, implemented by advanced large language models (LLMs), coordinates code generation, code-level screening, syndrome-extraction synthesis, and decoder-based circuit evaluation. At its core, OmniQEC combines a self-evolving reasoning mechanism with a slow--fast synergistic workflow: a fast loop explores candidates using inexpensive code-level proxies, whereas a slow loop performs physically grounded circuit-level evaluation and feeds the resulting evidence back into the search. We evaluate OmniQEC across four qLDPC construction families, three LLM backends, and $14$ total-physical-qubit budgets per backend. The discovered codes show steadily improving logical-error suppression with increasing physical-qubit budgets and outperform the BB codes with $[\![72,12,6]\!]$ and $[\![144,12,12]\!]$ under complete-implementation budgets of 98 and 240 physical qubits, respectively. The discovered codes are hardware-friendly and may be of independent interest for practical QEC implementation. These findings pave the way towards LLM-assisted QEC discovery grounded in physically informed code--circuit--decoder co-design.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper revolutionizes the concept of Quantum Reference Frames by generalizing them from simple subsystems to covariant quantum instruments. By imposing strict operational constraints based on complete covariance, it derives fundamental physical principles, offering novel explanations for particle statistics and time evolution in complex, interacting quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep operational foundation for defining physical observables (like time or particle labels) when standard subsystem decompositions fail due to interactions or global symmetries, which is crucial for advanced many-body theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work generalizes Quantum Reference Frames (QRFs) beyond simple tensor factor subsystems to covariant quantum instruments, aiming for a unified operational understanding of perspectival quantum mechanics.

**Main result.** This generalized framework provides explanations for the symmetrization postulate, entanglement description between bosons and fermions, and allows relational clocks to reproduce the Schrödinger equation even in interacting systems.

**Method.** The authors reconstruct and generalize the perspective-neutral approach by imposing the operational constraint that only completely covariant operations can be physically implemented on a system.

**Model / system.** The framework analyzes quantum systems using QRFs, extending from subsystem descriptions to general covariant quantum instruments. Applications include modeling indistinguishable particles and relational time evolution in interacting many-body settings.

**Key observables.** Completely covariant operations, constraint equations (e.g., C|ψ> = 0), and the resulting effective Hamiltonians for relative motion.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the symmetry group G (generalized from finite to compact Lie groups) and interaction coupling constants K.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that physical reality must be describable solely through completely covariant operations, which dictates the allowed measurements and dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper first reconstructs the perspective-neutral framework operationally before generalizing it to covariant instruments. It then details applications concerning indistinguishable particles, entanglement, and relational time evolution in interacting systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We generalize the notion of quantum reference frames (QRFs) to cases where the frame does not necessarily correspond to a tensor factor subsystem, but to a covariant quantum instrument. This unlocks a variety of physical applications: "frames of labeling" for indistinguishable particles, suggesting explanations for the symmetrization postulate and the absence of parastatistics, and yielding a transparent description of the entanglement of bosons and fermions; and relational clocks reproducing the Schrödinger equation exactly even when all subsystems are interacting or when there are frequency superselection sectors. Our work generalizes the perspective-neutral approach to QRFs pioneered by Höhn and co-authors, which we reconstruct from a simple operational scenario. We give a resource-theoretic grounding of this framework, and show how the notion of completely covariant operations explains the relevance of the charge-zero sector and the pure-state transformation behavior across perspectives. This also suggests operational clarifications of some aspects of constraint quantization, e.g. of the meaning of constraint equations such as $C|ψ\rangle=0$. Some of our results, such as our generalization of relationalization maps to instruments, apply more broadly to other QRF frameworks too, and they contribute to bridging the gap between operational quantum information theory and the internal QRF research program.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel method to find bounds on multivariate homogeneous polynomials by reformulating the problem in terms of quantum mechanics. It replaces the polynomial with an effective Hamiltonian operator and estimates its extreme eigenvalues via matrix diagonalization. This technique provides powerful, quantum-derived heuristics for solving difficult problems in mathematical physics and optimization theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful mathematical bridge between classical optimization theory and quantum mechanics. For AMO/open quantum systems researchers, it suggests using spectral analysis of large quantum operators as a systematic heuristic tool to constrain the behavior of complex physical observables that are hard to analyze classically.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is finding rigorous lower and upper bounds for multivariate homogeneous polynomials, which is mathematically equivalent to determining the extreme eigenvalues of their coefficient tensors.

**Main result.** The authors propose a novel quantum-inspired variational method where bounding the polynomial is recast as diagonalizing an auxiliary, large quantum operator (Hamiltonian). This approach successfully yields conjectured sharp inequalities for classical polynomial optimization problems.

**Method.** The technique involves replacing the classical polynomial $P(\vec{x})$ with a quantum mechanical operator $\hat{H}$ acting in a high-dimensional state space. The extreme eigenvalues of the tensor are then estimated by finding the extreme eigenvalues of this resulting matrix representation of $\hat{H}$.

**Model / system.** The analysis is applied to general multivariate homogeneous polynomials, often modeled via Hamiltonians derived from quantum systems like those involving bosonic creation/annihilation operators (e.g., Lowest Landau Level or resonant Hamiltonian classes).

**Key observables.** The extreme eigenvalues ($\lambda_{\min}, \lambda_{\max}$) of the coefficient tensor, which correspond to the minimum and maximum energy eigenvalues ($E_{\min}, E_{\max}$) of the quantum Hamiltonian in specific sectors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the state space (which can be very large), the particle number sector $N$, and coupling constants ($\sigma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the semiclassical approximation that the expectation value of the quantum operator tracks the classical polynomial, and it serves as a heuristic guide for proving analytic inequalities.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the difficulty of bounding polynomials via tensor eigenvalues. It then introduces the quantum variational method by mapping the problem to Hamiltonian diagonalization in finite sectors (fixed particle number $N$). Finally, it applies this framework to specific physical models (like LLL or resonant systems) to derive concrete, conjectured bounds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The problem of finding lower and upper bounds on multivariate homogeneous polynomials is both difficult and important given its applications to questions ranging from dynamical stability in complex potential landscapes to data analysis. From the standpoint of tensor eigenvalue theory, the question is equivalent to finding the smallest and the largest eigenvalues of the coefficient tensor corresponding to the given polynomial. Standard approaches outlined in the literature amount to running nonlinear iterations in search for the optimal rays along which the growth of the polynomial is fastest or slowest. Unlike the case of matrices (or their corresponding multivariate quadratic forms) convergence of such algorithms for higher-rank tensors is capricious due to the complex topography of polynomial objective functions. In this essay, a very different strategy, inspired by quantum-mechanical variational methods, is introduced for finding bounds on polynomials. The original polynomial is replaced by an operator acting in a suitably chosen (large) space of states, such that in an appropriate "classical" limit this operator approaches the original polynomial expression made of commutative variables. As a result, approximating the smallest and largest eigenvalues of the coefficient tensor, and thus finding bounds on polynomials, amounts to diagonalizing the resulting quantum operator, represented as a large matrix, and then inspecting the smallest and largest eigenvalues of this matrix. This approach is then successfully applied to standard test examples from tensor eigenvalue literature and other problems of interest in mathematical physics including Strichartz-type inequalities.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a kernel-based estimator for the Density of States in large quantum systems by adapting classical signal processing techniques. It uses the 'Rodeo response' measured via single-ancilla circuits and averaging over random states to reconstruct the DoS, which is convolved with an analytically determined spectral filter. The method is validated on spin models, demonstrating its ability to recover thermodynamic properties like specific heat.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, resource-efficient technique to extract global spectral information (DoS) from local measurements achievable with near-term quantum hardware, bypassing the exponential scaling barrier of full diagonalization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is reconstructing the Density of States (DoS), g(E), for quantum many-body systems whose Hilbert spaces are too large to be diagonalized directly.

**Main result.** The authors show that averaging the Rodeo response over Haar-random input states yields a DoS convolved with an analytically derived spectral kernel, providing a direct quantum analogue to classical Kernel Polynomial Methods (KPM).

**Method.** The method adapts KPM by using the time evolution measurement via the 'Rodeo response' and averaging this response over random initial states. This transforms the problem into a signal processing estimation task.

**Model / system.** The analysis is general for quantum many-body systems described by a Hamiltonian, but validation is performed on specific models like the one-dimensional transverse-field Ising and spin-1 ring model.

**Key observables.** Density of States g(E), spectral amplitude $\hat{g}(E)$, mean energy per spin $\langle e angle$, and specific heat $c_B$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ancilla dimension ($d_a$), the number of random states sampled ($R$), the number of time steps ($N_t$), and the spectral kernel parameters (e.g., Gaussian width $\sigma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include assuming Haar-random input states to ensure uniform spectral weight, and treating the total variance as a sum of statistical errors from random state sampling and temporal sampling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum circuit for the Rodeo cycle and show numerical comparisons between reconstructed DoS ($\hat{g}(E)$) versus exact results for various magnetic fields in the spin-1 model. Figure 5 compares derived thermodynamic quantities like specific heat ($c_B$) against exact diagonalization.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by deriving the estimator, establishing the connection between signal processing window functions and quantum reconstruction kernels, and then validates this method using numerical simulations on known models (Ising/spin-1).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The density of states (DoS) encodes the thermodynamic and spectral properties of quantum many-body systems, yet its reconstruction becomes intractable for Hilbert spaces too large to diagonalize. Classically, the kernel polynomial method (KPM) addresses this by combining stochastic trace estimation with a smoothing kernel. Here we show that the Rodeo algorithm---one of the simplest eigenvalue-location protocols for near-term quantum hardware---provides a direct quantum analogue of this approach. Averaging the Rodeo response over Haar-random input states yields the DoS convolved with a spectral kernel fixed entirely by the distribution of evolution times: the random states play the role of stochastic trace estimation, and the temporal sampling distribution that of the damping kernel. The construction requires only the standard single-ancilla circuit, and quantum typicality suppresses the statistical error as the Hilbert-space dimension grows. We derive the estimator and its uncertainties, establish an explicit dictionary between signal-processing window functions and quantum reconstruction kernels, and validate the method on the one-dimensional transverse-field Ising and spin-1 models.

</details>
