**Summary.** This paper details a computational methodology to simulate anisotropic spin systems at finite temperatures by employing the Finite-Temperature Lanczos Method (FTLM). It overcomes severe scaling limitations by designing and implementing a highly parallelized, triple-hybrid high-performance computing scheme utilizing MPI, OpenMP, and GPUs. The work demonstrates its feasibility on complex molecular magnets like $	ext{Mn}_9$, providing insights into their magnetic properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses simulating quantum magnetism in large molecular clusters using state-of-the-art high-performance computing techniques, which is crucial for understanding magnetic materials beyond simple lattice models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To apply the finite-temperature Lanczos method (FTLM) to study anisotropic spin systems whose Hilbert spaces are too large for exact diagonalization.

**Main result.** The authors successfully sketched a framework utilizing a triple-hybrid HPC scheme (MPI/CPU/GPU) to tackle the computational demands of FTLM for these complex magnetic models, demonstrating feasibility on systems like Mn9.

**Method.** The core method is the Finite-Temperature Lanczos Method (FTLM), which approximates thermodynamic expectation values using trace estimators. The implementation heavily relies on parallelizing sparse matrix-vector multiplications across MPI ranks, CPU threads, and GPU engines.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on anisotropic spin systems governed by a Hamiltonian including Heisenberg exchange, single-ion anisotropies, Zeeman terms, and dipolar interactions. Examples include fictitious ring molecules and $	ext{Mn}_9$ clusters.

**Key observables.** Heat capacity ($C$), Magnetic Susceptibility ($\chi_M T / B$), and low-energy density of states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta = 1/k_B T$) and applied magnetic field strength ($B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is the use of FTLM itself. The authors also discuss using an improved, symmetric form ($s$FTLM) despite its increased computational cost.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the parallel steps (MPI-step 1 and 2) of matrix-vector multiplication on a distributed memory architecture. Other figures show calculated heat capacity and susceptibility for model systems like the ring molecule and $	ext{Mn}_9$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical problem, details the mathematical framework (FTLM approximations), outlines the necessary computational infrastructure (triple-hybrid HPC scheme), and then presents results on specific spin models ($	ext{Mn}_9$, ring system) to validate the approach.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Lanczos concepts are often realized on supercomputers. In view of modern architectures of high-performance computing triple-hybrid schemes employing MPI, CPU-openMP as well as GPU-openMP should be utilized. The present article sketches how such a scheme could be utilized in order to meet the demands of the finite-temperature Lanczos method for anisotropic spin systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reveals that the stacking sequence in $	ext{V}_{1/3}	ext{NbS}_2$ fundamentally dictates its magnetic ground state and anomalous Hall response. By combining advanced diffraction, transport measurements, and theoretical modeling of RKKY interactions, the authors demonstrate a pathway to engineer bulk properties in layered quantum magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the concept of engineering bulk properties through precise control over interlayer coupling (analogous to controlling quantum interfaces) is relevant for designing novel correlated electronic materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the stacking sequence of magnetic ions ($	ext{V}^{3+}$) in $	ext{V}_{1/3}	ext{NbS}_2$ dictates its macroscopic magnetic properties, specifically focusing on anisotropic altermagnetism.

**Main result.** Two distinct polytypes (AB and ABC) exist, leading to different easy axes of magnetization and anomalous Hall responses. The stacking sequence dramatically controls the bulk magnetism by stabilizing quasi-collinear A-type antiferromagnetic order.

**Method.** The research combines single crystal diffraction (X-ray/neutron), transport measurements (Anomalous Hall Effect), inelastic neutron scattering, and theoretical modeling using RKKY interactions and Mean-Field Theory.

**Model / system.** The system is the layered van der Waals material $	ext{V}_{1/3}	ext{NbS}_2$, which forms a superlattice structure. The magnetism is modeled by Heisenberg Hamiltonians incorporating exchange couplings ($J_{ij}$) and single-ion anisotropy terms.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall resistivity ($\Deltaho_{xy}$), magnetization ($M$), spin wave excitations (INS spectra), and the magnetic ordering temperature ($T_N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The stacking sequence (AB vs. ABC) is critical; RKKY interactions extend up to 1 nm, and the system exhibits distinct transition temperatures ($\approx 50	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that magnetic properties are highly sensitive to structural details like stacking order, allowing for engineering of bulk electronic states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate diffraction evidence distinguishing polytypes and magnetic transitions; INS data probes spin wave dispersions across different stacking arrangements (AB vs. ABC).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from experimental characterization using diffraction and transport measurements to detailed theoretical modeling of the exchange interactions via RKKY fitting, culminating in a quantitative link between structural disorder/stacking and the resulting anomalous Hall effect.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report profound impacts of the stacking sequence of triangular lattices of magnetic transition metal ions intercalated between the layers of the van der Waals material NbS$_2$. Using single crystal x-ray and neutron diffraction, and transport and magnetization measurements, we show there are two distinct polytypes of $\rm V_{1/3}NbS_2$ with disparate easy axes of magnetization and different anomalous Hall responses. Self-consistent analysis of inelastic neutron scattering data provides evidence for oscillatory RKKY interactions that extend to 1 nm and stabilize quasi-collinear A-type altermagnetic orders in both polytypes though with perpendicular easy axes. The detailed stacking sequence of a bulk polytype crystal dramatically impact its macroscopic anomalous Hall response and magnetism, which suggests a new path to engineer the bulk properties of a layered three dimensional solid.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies a sophisticated variational method to calculate the instantaneous elastic moduli and sound velocities of Yukawa fluids. By comparing results across weak, strong, and transitional coupling regimes, it validates its theoretical framework against molecular dynamics simulations. The work provides detailed insights into how interparticle interactions govern the mechanical stability and wave propagation in these strongly correlated plasma-like systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluids, the rigorous application of advanced statistical mechanics techniques (like variational principles) to determine collective excitations (sound waves) in strongly interacting systems is relevant for understanding many-body dynamics in quantum plasma or ultracold atomic mixtures where mean-field approximations are used.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to evaluate instantaneous elastic moduli (shear, bulk) and related sound velocities for Yukawa fluids across different coupling regimes.

**Main result.** The variational approach yields expressions for these moduli that show excellent agreement with results from QLCA and MD simulations, particularly in the strong coupling limit where configurational contributions dominate.

**Method.** A variational approach based on the Bogoliubov inequality is employed, using the hard-sphere model as a reference system to minimize free energy functionals.

**Model / system.** The physical system modeled is a one-component plasma interacting via the repulsive screened Coulomb potential (Yukawa fluid). The analysis covers transitions between weakly and strongly coupled regimes.

**Key observables.** Instantaneous shear modulus ($G_\infty$), bulk modulus ($K_\infty$), longitudinal/transverse sound velocities ($c_l, c_t$), and the adiabatic sound velocity ($	ext{c}_s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling parameter ($\Gamma$), screening parameter ($\kappa$), packing fraction ($\eta$), and temperature ($T$). The analysis focuses on strong vs. weak coupling limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The variational approach relies on the hard-sphere reference system, and in the strong coupling limit, kinetic energy contributions are considered negligible.

**Figures summary.** Table I compares configurational components of longitudinal and transverse sound velocities calculated via QLCA versus the Variational method across various $(\kappa, \Gamma)$ states. Figure 1 plots these sound velocities against $\kappa$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops expressions for elastic moduli using the variational approach, derives a generalized Cauchy identity relating moduli to pressure, and then compares calculated sound velocities derived from both instantaneous moduli and thermodynamic definitions across different coupling regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The variational approach based on the Bogoliubov inequality using the fluid of hard spheres as a reference system is implemented to evaluate instantaneous shear, bulk and longitudinal elastic moduli, as well as related sound velocities of Yukawa fluids. The remarkable accuracy of this method is documented. In addition, we evaluate the adiabatic sound velocity from an appropriate equation of state and discuss its relation to the longitudinal and bulk sound velocities obtained from the corresponding instantaneous elastic moduli. The transition between weakly coupled and strongly coupled regimes is analyzed in detail.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper uses AI techniques to discover new, highly efficient linear-optical circuits for generating path-entangled NOON states. These newly found 'modular comb' families significantly surpass the success probability scaling of existing methods. This advances the theoretical understanding and experimental feasibility of creating large-scale photonic entanglement resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful demonstration of using automated discovery methods (AI) to advance fundamental quantum optics resource state generation, which is crucial for scalable photonic quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is deterministically generating large, high-probability heralded entangled states of light (path-entangled NOON states) due to weak photon interactions.

**Main result.** The authors discovered a new, scalable family of linear-optical circuits that provide exponential or super-exponential improvements in success probability compared to previously known schemes for creating multi-mode NOON states.

**Method.** They employed AI techniques within an automated search framework (JAX) to discover optimal photonic circuit designs and analyzed the scaling of success probabilities using asymptotic comparisons.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on path-entangled quantum states, specifically multi-mode NOON states ($	ext{NOON}_N$), generated using linear optics involving beam splitters and heralded detection mechanisms.

**Key observables.** Success probability ($p_{	ext{succ}}$), fidelity ($F$), and the scaling ratio of success probabilities compared to existing constructions (e.g., PW, ZPM).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of entangled photons ($N$) and the dimensionality/number of modes ($d$). Detector efficiency ($\eta$) is also a key parameter in comparisons.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the discovered schemes can be realized using passive linear optics, although limitations regarding optimal solutions for specific $N$ are noted.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate compact heralded circuits (e.g., NOON$_9$), and plots quantify the asymptotic improvement ratios of the new modular-comb family over older methods as $N$ increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the difficulty of state generation, then introducing the AI-driven search for scalable circuit families (the 'modular comb'), demonstrating its superior scaling via mathematical analysis and comparison to prior art, and finally extending this methodology to multi-mode generalizations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entangled states of light lie at the heart of photonic quantum technologies, from distributed quantum communication to quantum-enhanced measurement and information processing. Their practical generation, however, remains constrained by the weak interactions between photons, which make the deterministic assembly of large multiphoton entangled states a central challenge in quantum optics. In this work, we use AI techniques to discover heralded linear-optical schemes for path-entangled states and show that the resulting solutions can be elevated from individual circuits to a new scalable family. This family contains previously known constructions as special cases while generally providing exponential and super-exponential improvements over those, and its extension to broader classes of target states shows how automated discovery can reveal transferable physical understanding. By presenting compact experimental proposals for large path-entangled states, our results provide both a theoretical advance in photonic heralding and a route towards a substantial leap in experimentally accessible multiphoton entanglement.

</details>

### [Classically Augmented Zero-Noise Extrapolation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25746v1)

**Authors:** Timon Scheiber  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.25746v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.25746_figures/2607.25746_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1: Schematic of CA-ZNE. Standard ZNE estimates the noise-free expectation value by evaluating a noisy quantum circuit at several amplified noise levels and extrapolating the resulting expectation values to the zero-noise limit. Because Richardson extrapolation amplifies statistical fluctuations, the resulting estimator can have large sampling variance and a potential extrapolation bias. In CA-ZNE, nodes above a cutoff noise level are replaced by classically simulated estimates, which act as zero-sampling-variance anchor points for the extrapolation. This can stabilize the estimator and reduce variance, at the cost of a possible deterministic simulation bias.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2607.25746_figures/2607.25746_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>

The shaded regions indicate the sampling uncertainty of the quan- tum estimates at fixed shot budget. At the points where the Pauli propagation curves intersect the shaded region the simulation bias becomes smaller than the expected sampling error. The higher the cutoff in the Pauli prop- agation the more exact the simulation will become and the intersection occurs earlier.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2607.25746_figures/2607.25746_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>

The spacings are a linear node spacing and the two spacings closely aligned with the Chebyshev nodes, namely the extrema and the roots of the polynomial of degree n. As highlighted by the figure the Chebyshev nodes cluster at the end points of the interpolation interval.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2607.25746_figures/2607.25746_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>

The histogram shows the distribution of the obtained expectation values from independent ZNE runs for CA-ZNE and ZNE. For ZNE we obtain a MSE of around 0.65 whereas for CA-ZNE we obtain 0.3. Our results show a bias of 0.129 for ZNE and 0.141 for CA-ZNE.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 5</summary>

<img src="2607.25746_figures/2607.25746_fig5.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 5: Convergence of CA-ZNE with the Pauli-truncation budget for the transverse-field Ising model. The x-axis denotes the maximum number of Pauli terms retained in the Pauli-propagation simulation. Left: empirical bias relative to the noiseless reference value. For small truncation budgets, CA-ZNE exhibits a large truncation-induced bias, which decreases rapidly as more Pauli terms are retained and approaches the fixed-order ZNE baseline. Shaded regions indicate the spread over repeated estimates. Right: corresponding mean-squared error (MSE). As the truncation bias is reduced, the CA-ZNE MSE drops and approaches the improvement set by the reduced estimator variance, eventually...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Classically Augmented Zero-Noise Extrapolation (CA-ZNE), a hybrid method for error mitigation in noisy quantum circuits. It replaces high-noise measurements with deterministic classical simulations, which significantly reduces the estimator's variance. The technique offers an exponentially faster reduction in estimation error compared to purely quantum extrapolation methods, provided the introduced classical bias is controlled.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses practical limitations in quantum computation by offering a concrete, theoretically bounded technique to improve measurement fidelity beyond what is achievable purely through increased sampling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is mitigating high noise rates inherent in current quantum hardware to estimate ideal, noise-free expectation values.

**Main result.** Classically Augmented Zero-Noise Extrapolation (CA-ZNE) can achieve an exponentially reduced sampling variance compared to standard ZNE by replacing high-noise measurements with deterministic classical simulations.

**Method.** The authors propose a hybrid error mitigation technique that combines quantum measurements at low noise levels with classical simulation estimates for high noise levels, analyzed via Richardson extrapolation.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on general noisy quantum circuits characterized by a noise strength $\lambda$, often modeled using the Sparse Pauli Lindblad (SPL) noise model. The goal is to estimate the ideal expectation value $\langle O angle_0$.

**Key observables.** Ideal expectation value ($\langle O angle_0$), Mean-Squared Error (MSE), and variance reduction factors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise strength ($\lambda$), total shot budget ($N_{	ext{shots}}$), number of quantum nodes ($c$), and the crossover point where classical bias equals sampling error.

**Assumptions / limitations.** CA-ZNE introduces a deterministic simulation bias, which must be kept smaller than the variance reduction achieved to yield overall improvement. The analysis assumes specific noise models (e.g., SPL) for bounding errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic comparison between standard ZNE and CA-ZNE, and show the crossover condition where classical simulation bias becomes negligible compared to quantum sampling error.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of standard ZNE due to high variance overhead. It then develops the CA-ZNE method by replacing nodes with classical estimates, deriving formulas for reduced variance, analyzing the MSE trade-off, and finally providing rigorous bounds on the resulting bias using Pauli propagation techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate a hybrid quantum-classical approach to quantum error mitigation. We propose Classically Augmented Zero-Noise Extrapolation, a hybrid error-mitigation method in which high-noise Richardson extrapolation nodes are replaced by classically simulated estimates. These classical nodes have negligible sampling variance but introduce deterministic simulation bias. We derive the resulting variance reduction under optimal shot allocation and show that, for linear node spacings and fixed index cutoff, the coefficient-level reduction can be exponential. We validate the prediction numerically using Pauli-propagation simulations and demonstrate a reduction in mean-squared error when the truncation bias is sufficiently small.

</details>

### [Open-ended innovation in zero-sum games](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25677v1)

**Authors:** Matteo Marsili  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.25677v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.25677_figures/2607.25677_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Main figure: Value of the game as a function of n1 for n2 = 103. Inset: plot of u(x) versus √x. Both results are obtained solving the equations in BE for random zero-sum games where ai,j is drawn i.i.d. from a distribution with mean zero and unit variance.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical note examines zero-sum games where players can continuously innovate by introducing new strategies. It models this process mathematically and concludes that if the cost of innovation is low, the system enters an everlasting 'arm race.' This continuous competition ultimately drives the achievable payoffs toward zero.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on game theory, the concept of diminishing returns and resource depletion due to continuous technological advancement mirrors concepts studied in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics or complex adaptive systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes zero-sum games where players can introduce new strategies, investigating whether this process leads to an endless cycle of innovation.

**Main result.** Under generic conditions and low innovation costs, the system predicts an everlasting innovation arm race, leading to vanishingly small payoffs for both players in the limit of infinite strategies.

**Method.** The analysis uses mathematical modeling based on large random zero-sum games, employing derivatives to examine how marginal payoff changes with increasing strategy sets.

**Model / system.** The model is a two-player zero-sum game defined by a payoff function $\pi(s_1, s_2)$, where strategies are probability distributions over a simplex. The analysis focuses on the large limit of the number of available strategies ($n_1, n_2$).

**Key observables.** The value of the game $V(n_1, n_2)$ and the function $u(x)$ characterizing its dependence on the strategy ratio $x=n_1/n_2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Innovation costs ($c_1, c_2$) and the limit where these costs approach zero.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The setting assumes generic conditions for open-ended innovation and relies on modeling strategy expansion as adding dimensions to the payoff matrix.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates $V$ versus $n_1$ for fixed $n_2$, with an inset showing $u(x)$ plotted against $\sqrt{x}$, highlighting concavity.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the mathematical framework of open-ended zero-sum games, derives approximations for the game value in large limits, and then analyzes the condition for sustained innovation by balancing marginal gains against costs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This note discusses zero-sum games with open-ended innovation, whereby each player may introduce new strategies. The innovation process is modelled as a draw of new strategies form a distribution. It is argued that, under generic conditions, this setting can lead to an everlasting innovation arm race, because the introduction of new technologies of one player increases the marginal utility for technological innovation of the opponent.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Fermi Sets, a novel neural network architecture designed to represent fermionic wavefunctions accurately. It combines this ansatz with fixed-phase Diffusion Monte Carlo (FPDMC) to systematically calculate the ground state energy of strongly correlated electron systems like quantum dots and 2DEG. The results confirm that this ML approach provides an asymptotically exact method for solving these notoriously difficult many-body problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, scalable machine learning tool for tackling highly complex many-body physics problems that are central to condensed matter theory, particularly those involving strong correlations and topological phases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is solving the electronic Schrödinger equation for interacting many-electron systems, which is computationally intractable due to the infinite-dimensional Hilbert space and complex fermionic constraints.

**Main result.** The proposed Fermi Sets architecture, combined with fixed-phase Diffusion Monte Carlo (FPDMC), demonstrates systematic convergence towards the exact ground state energy for both quantum dots and 2D electron gases as network size increases.

**Method.** A two-stage variational approach: first, optimizing a complex-valued neural network wavefunction (Fermi Sets) to enforce fermionic antisymmetry; second, projecting out residual errors using fixed-phase DMC.

**Model / system.** The study applies to paradigmatic strongly correlated systems, including electrons in parabolic quantum dots under magnetic fields and the 2D Uniform Electron Gas (UEG) with long-range Coulomb interactions.

**Key observables.** The residual gap ($\Delta E = E_{VMC} - E_{DMC}$), which must collapse monotonically to zero for convergence; ground state energy ($E_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field strength (e.g., $B=8$ T); electron density parameter ($r_s$); network capacity/size of the Fermi Sets.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of a fixed-phase constraint in DMC, which is necessary for complex wavefunctions and reduces to the standard fixed-node approximation in the real limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the two-stage framework (VMC $ightarrow$ FPDMC) and detail the architecture of the Fermi Sets wavefunction used for assessment across different physical systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of traditional methods, proposes the Fermi Sets ansatz, details the two-step variational/DMC procedure, and then applies this framework to benchmark calculations on quantum dots and 2DEG.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we show that Fermi Sets---a provably universal neural network architecture for fermionic wavefunctions---can be systematically scaled up to find interacting ground states through energy minimization in a variational Monte Carlo framework. By further performing fixed-phase diffusion Monte Carlo (DMC) on the optimized neural network wavefunction, we demonstrate that as the network size increases, the variational energy systematically decreases while the energy improvement from DMC collapses monotonically to zero, indicating convergence to the ground state. We illustrate the scaling of Fermi Sets accompanied by the DMC assessment for interacting electrons in jellium and in a quantum dot under high magnetic fields.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes particle motion in $\kappa$-Minkowski spacetime, treating momentum space as curved de Sitter geometry. By using a deformed Casimir invariant, they derive exact energy spectra for charged particles in a magnetic field. The key finding is that the curvature imposes a maximal momentum, resulting in a finite Landau spectrum with a Highest Landau Level.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on high-energy spacetime physics, the concept of a fundamental UV cutoff leading to discrete, bounded spectra (like the HLL) has parallels in effective field theories or condensed matter systems where confinement or quantization occurs at an intrinsic scale.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates particle dynamics in $\kappa$-Minkowski spacetime, linking the geometry of curved momentum space to physical observables like energy spectra.

**Main result.** The curvature imposes a fundamental upper bound on invariant momentum, leading to a finite Landau spectrum characterized by a Highest Landau Level (HLL).

**Method.** The authors employ Poisson gauge theory and geometric methods, deriving the $\kappa$-Poincaré Casimir operator from de Sitter geometry.

**Model / system.** The model considers charged scalar and spin-1/2 particles moving in a constant magnetic field within the framework of non-commutative $\kappa$-Minkowski spacetime.

**Key observables.** Exact energy spectra, Highest Landau Level (HLL), and the deformed Dirac equation constraint.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The deformation parameter $\kappa$ acts as an ultraviolet cutoff scale; the magnetic field strength $B$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on constructing gauge-invariant dynamics using Poisson brackets, and specific choices for operator ordering are noted in the quantization process.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the energy $E(|\vec{p}|)$ versus $|\vec{p}|$ for different regimes, showing the transition from commutative to $\kappa$-Minkowski energies up to the boundary $|\vec{p}| = \kappa$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the formalism by first establishing the geometric structure of momentum space via the Casimir invariant ($C_\kappa$), then applying this framework to derive gauge-invariant equations of motion for scalar and spin-1/2 particles, culminating in the calculation of the finite Landau spectrum.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

It is obtained the $κ$-Poincare Casimir from the de Sitter geometry of momentum space and employed as the dynamical constraint governing charged particles within the framework of Poisson gauge theory. The resulting formalism is applied to investigate both scalar and spin-1/2 particles in a constant magnetic field. Exact energy spectra are obtained, including all orders in the deformation parameter $1/κ$. The curvature of momentum space implies a maximal invariant momentum, which in turn leads to a finite Landau spectrum characterized by the existence of a Highest Landau Level (HLL). In the fermionic case, the truncation becomes spin dependent, resulting in a polarized HLL. Possible implications of this ultraviolet truncation and its relation to anomaly-related phenomena are briefly discussed.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces KQFuzz, a novel knowledge-guided fuzzer designed to test the reliability of quantum computing libraries. It enhances LLM-based testing by incorporating deep codebase knowledge and advanced mutation strategies. The resulting framework significantly improves bug discovery and code coverage across major quantum frameworks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on software testing rather than physical dynamics, the rigorous validation techniques developed for quantum circuit execution are crucial for building reliable simulation tools necessary for studying complex many-body physics or open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Ensuring the reliability and correctness of quantum libraries is critical, but existing fuzzing methods lack flexibility and efficiency.

**Main result.** KQFuzz significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods, improving code coverage by up to 18.44% and discovering multiple confirmed bugs in major quantum libraries.

**Method.** The authors propose KQFuzz, a knowledge-guided fuzzer that uses codebase knowledge to ground LLM test generation, combined with fitness-guided evaluation and two-level mutations.

**Model / system.** The work targets software implementations of quantum computation within popular libraries like Qiskit, PennyLane, and Cirq, which operate on the quantum circuit model involving gates and qubits.

**Key observables.** Code coverage improvement (up to 18.44%), bug discovery rate (13 bugs found), code validity, and execution time/token consumption.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The two-level mutation strategy involves parameter-level and gate-level structural mutations; the fitness function incorporates gate diversity and entangled qubits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that increased output token count in KQFuzz represents meaningful semantic content leading to higher quality tests, rather than mere redundancy.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the scope of different fuzzer types; Figure 3 shows empirical comparison of validity rates for classical vs. quantum domains; Table 8 and 9 detail computational cost analysis across various stages and models.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of current fuzzing techniques, then introduces KQFuzz with its knowledge corpus construction (static metadata, API associations, semantic models), followed by detailed descriptions of the generation/mutation strategies. It presents empirical results comparing against baselines on multiple quantum libraries and concludes with an efficiency analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

As quantum computing continually improves, ensuring the reliability and correctness of quantum libraries has become increasingly critical. To this end, many LLM-based fuzzing approaches towards quantum libraries have been proposed to uncover potential bugs. However, these methods still suffer from limitations such as insufficient flexibility and low efficiency, which hinder the progress of the quantum computing field. To address these challenges, we propose KQFuzz, a novel knowledge-guided fuzzer for quantum libraries. It leverages comprehensive codebase knowledge to ground LLM-based test generation, synergizing this with fitness-guided evaluation and two-level mutations to explore complex execution paths and trigger potential bugs. Firstly, KQFuzz introduces a novel prompting scheme tailored to quantum programs, which strategically incorporates knowledge of the codebase to efficiently generate high-quality quantum seed programs. Moreover, we develop evaluation and mutation strategies to handle the generated seed programs, facilitating efficient fuzzing execution while further enriching the diversity of the resulting test cases. We implement KQFuzz and conduct fuzzing on three popular quantum libraries, including Qiskit, PennyLane, and Cirq. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach significantly outperforms other state-of-the-art methods, with coverage improved by up to 18.44%. During the development of KQFuzz, we discovered 13 bugs, all of which have been confirmed and 12 have already been fixed by the developers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the quantum mechanics of a charged particle on a cubic surface pierced by a magnetic monopole. It rigorously handles the gauge structure using overlapping patches and classifies eigenstates based on the cube's rotational symmetry group. The key findings are the splitting of Landau levels due to discrete symmetry and the emergence of corner-localized gap states in the lattice approximation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete realization of how global topology (monopole flux) interacts with discrete geometric symmetry (the cube), leading to spectral splitting effects analogous to those seen in quantum Hall systems but constrained by crystal geometry.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the quantum mechanics of a charged particle confined to the surface of a cube enclosing a magnetic monopole.

**Main result.** The spectrum exhibits Landau-level-like manifolds whose degeneracies are split by the discrete cubic symmetry, and additional gap states localized near the cube corners appear in the tight-binding limit.

**Method.** The analysis uses continuum formulation with overlapping gauge patches (Wu–Yang construction) and is complemented by a gauge-covariant finite-difference discretization for numerical computation.

**Model / system.** The system models a charged particle on the surface of a cube subjected to a magnetic field ($\vec{B}$) representing a monopole flux. The Hamiltonian involves the vector potential $\vec{A}(\vec{r})$ and is analyzed using symmetry groups like the cubic rotation group $O$.

**Key observables.** Landau-level-like energy spectra, gap states localized at cube corners, and the classification of eigenstates by irreducible representations of $O$ or $2O$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The magnetic flux ($\Phi_B$), which must satisfy the Dirac quantization condition; the size parameter ($L$) of the cube.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on using two overlapping gauge patches to handle the non-trivial topology imposed by the monopole flux, and that the physical symmetry is restored via gauge-modified rotation operators.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate wavefunction probability densities $|\Psi|^2$ and phase plots ($\arg(\Psi)$) across different symmetry multiplets ($A_1, T_1$, etc.) for varying monopole charges ($M$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the necessity of multi-patch gauge constructions to analyzing the resulting symmetries using group theory, culminating in numerical computation and visualization of the energy spectrum and wavefunctions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the quantum mechanics of a charged particle confined to the surface of a cube enclosing a magnetic monopole. The magnetic field is chosen to have a constant magnitude on each face and to point along the outward normal, preserving the rotational symmetry of the cube. We formulate the continuum problem using two gauge patches on the cube surface and show that consistency of the wavefunction gives the Dirac quantization condition. Since an explicit vector potential does not remain invariant under ordinary rotations, we construct gauge-modified rotation operators and use them to classify the eigenstates. Even monopole charges are described by the irreducible representations of the cubic rotation group O, while odd monopole charges require the spinorial representations of the binary octahedral group 2O. We compute the spectrum with a gauge-covariant finite-difference discretization and find Landau-level-like manifolds whose degeneracies are split by the discrete cubic symmetry. We also study the corresponding tight-binding Hofstadter problem on the discretized cube. The resulting spectrum contains the usual magnetic subband structure together with additional gap states localized near the cube corners.

</details>

**Summary.** This work tackles the asymmetry between electron and hole doping in Mott insulators by modeling the system using the t-t'-J Hamiltonian. It reveals that the ground state has two coupled components: a coherent quasiparticle and an incoherent composite part. The relative dominance of resonance versus coherent propagation dictates whether low-energy excitations are concentrated at the node (hole-doped) or the antinode (electron-doped).

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed momentum-space separation of spectral weight based on doping sign provides a concrete theoretical mechanism explaining the observed nodal-antinodal dichotomy in cuprates, which is crucial for understanding high-Tc superconductivity mechanisms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work addresses the observed asymmetry in low-energy single-particle excitations between electron-doped and hole-doped cuprates, which challenges a unified description within standard doped Mott insulator frameworks.

**Main result.** The ground state exhibits a two-component structure; for hole doping, resonance dominates at the node, while for electron doping, coherent quasiparticle propagation is selectively enhanced in the antinode, causing momentum-space separation.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis using the t-t'-J model combined with Variational Monte Carlo (VMC) simulations for single-hole excitations and mean-field treatments of fractionalization.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on cuprates modeled by the t-t'-J Hamiltonian, investigating doping effects in both electron ($t'>0$) and hole ($t'<0$) regimes. The analysis involves decomposing the ground state into coherent quasiparticle and incoherent composite components.

**Key observables.** Low-energy single-particle spectral weight $Z_k$, momentum-space distribution of excitations (nodal vs. antinodal), and kinetic energy contributions from different component interactions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping level ($\delta$), hopping parameters ($t, t', J$), and the sign of $t'$ which dictates the doping type (hole vs. electron).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The ground-state wave function is assumed to possess a generic two-component structure; approximations are made in mean-field treatments involving gauge fields and path integrals.

**Figures summary.** FIG. ES12 compares the quasiparticle spectral weight $Z_k$ for single holes across different doping regimes, showing distinct momentum dependence based on the sign of $t'$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the two-component nature of the ground state in the t-t'-J model. It then analyzes the physics separately for hole-doped ($t'<0$) and electron-doped ($t'>0$) systems, detailing how resonance or coherent propagation dominates in each case to explain experimental asymmetries.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Experimental studies reveal a striking asymmetry in low-energy single-particle excitations between electron- and hole-doped cuprates. Electron-doped cuprates display a nontrivial dichotomy: Fermi-liquid-like behavior (suggesting weaker electronic correlations) coexists with correlation-driven features typical of hole-doped systems. This dual nature challenges a unified description within a doped Mott insulator framework. The present work addresses this issue by establishing that the ground-state wave function of the $t$-$t'$-$J$ model generically possesses a two-component structure, comprising a coherent quasiparticle and an incoherent composite component. The kinetic energy arises from both the intrinsic propagation of the coherent quasiparticle and the resonance between these components. Using variational Monte Carlo at the level of a single hole, we show that for hole doping ($t'<0$), this resonance between components dominates and concentrates in the nodal region at low energies. This emergent propagation induced by resonance can be physically interpreted as originating from the recombination of fractionalized degrees of freedom, which drives various phenomena associated with strong correlations. Conversely, for electron doping ($t'>0$), the coherent quasiparticle propagation, which exhibits conventional properties of a Fermi liquid, is selectively enhanced in the antinodal region at low energies. This produces a separation in momentum space for systems with electron doping: the antinodal spectral weight at low energies is governed by the coherent quasiparticle, fundamentally differing from the nodal region, which remains dominated by the incoherent composite component. Motivated by such a structure and guided by experimental observations, we propose a phenomenological Green's function at finite doping, yielding spectral features consistent with experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a general, symmetry-based framework to classify emergent moiré Hamiltonians in twisted van der Waals heterostructures. By analyzing hundreds of bilayers using first-principles calculations, the authors move beyond simple single-orbital models. The resulting 'valley-orbital-symmetry framework' provides a systematic guide for engineering correlated and topological quantum phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical dictionary for designing strongly correlated quantum materials by linking fundamental crystal symmetry and orbital physics directly to the required Hubbard model parameters.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a general organizing principle for understanding the diverse emergent low-energy moiré Hamiltonians arising from twisted van der Waals heterostructures, moving beyond conventional single-orbital/K-valley paradigms.

**Main result.** The authors established a comprehensive 'valley-orbital-symmetry framework' that classifies emergent moiré physics based on parent material properties and symmetry constraints. This framework reveals multiple routes to correlated phases, including honeycomb, kagome, and multi-orbital flat bands.

**Method.** The study combines fully relaxed first-principles calculations (DFT) with advanced analysis techniques like band unfolding, elementary band representation (EBR), and detailed symmetry group theory applied across hundreds of twisted bilayers.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the moiré superlattice formed by twisting van der Waals bilayers. The theoretical framework aims to map the parent material's electronic structure ($\mathbf{k}_0, \mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{k}_0}$) onto an effective low-energy Hamiltonian defined by symmetry ($G_M$) and orbital content.

**Key observables.** Wannier characterization (W), band connectivity (C), topological classification (I), and the resulting $Q$-lattice structure are key diagnostics used to classify emergent models.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis spans over 600 commensurate twisted bilayers across all 2D lattice classes, focusing on low-energy manifolds derived from parent band edges.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the physics can be systematically organized by mapping parent band edge properties and symmetry constraints to the emergent moiré Hamiltonian structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the design map connecting parent band edge properties to the resulting moiré Hamiltonians, while other figures detail specific flat-band manifolds realized in various lattice classes.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces a general organizing map linking momentum location ($\mathbf{k}_0$), orbital character ($\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{k}_0}$), and symmetry ($G_M$) to the resulting moiré Hamiltonian. It then systematically applies this framework across numerous material examples, demonstrating how different symmetries enforce specific types of correlated models (e.g., honeycomb vs. kagome).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Moiré flat bands in van der Waals bilayers are usually discussed through a small set of mechanisms associated with the $Γ$ and $K$ valleys of hexagonal crystals, and more recently with $M$-valleys systems. Here we show that this view is incomplete. The momentum-space location and effective local orbital character of the monolayer's band edge, in conjunction with the moiré symmetry and the symmetry representations of the resulting bands, provide a general set of organizing variables for the emergent low-energy moiré Hamiltonian. Applying fully relaxed first-principles calculations, band unfolding and symmetry-representation analysis to more than 600 commensurate twisted bilayers spanning all 2D lattice classes, we identify several routes to moiré quantum matter beyond the conventional single-orbital paradigm. The resulting flat bands realize trigonal, honeycomb, square, checkerboard and kagome-like Hubbard models with single-orbital, multi-orbital and multi-site Hilbert spaces; spin-orbit-coupled multi-orbital flat bands exhibit symmetry-indicated topology beyond the conventional $K$-valley setting; and nonsymmorphic moiré symmetries enforce semimetallic flat-band connectivity. Analogous quasi-one-dimensional flat-band structures are found in $M$-valley hexagonal systems and $X$-valley square or rectangular systems resulting from emergent momentum-space nonsymmorphic symmetries. Separately, coupled multi-valley manifolds with kagome-like connectivity are identified in several systems whose parent band edges lie at non-high-symmetry points. These results establish a valley-orbital-symmetry framework for connecting parent-material electronic structure to emergent moiré Hamiltonians relevant to correlated, topological and symmetry-enforced moiré phases.

</details>

**Authors:** Howard Su, Huan-Hsin Tseng, Chi-Sheng Chen, Lance Bai  
**Type:** both · **Category:** numerical methods · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.25162v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper introduces a Quantum Transformer solver that uses Multi-Layer Fully-Connected Variational Quantum Circuits (FC-VQC) to solve complex, high-dimensional Partial Differential Equations reformulated as BSDEs. By integrating causal self-attention into the quantum circuit structure, the model achieves state-of-the-art performance compared to classical and non-attentive quantum baselines on several physical benchmarks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is numerical PDE solving, the core innovation lies in mapping complex functional approximation tasks onto hybrid quantum-classical neural network architectures (QTransformer/FC-VQC), which has implications for simulating quantum dynamics or high-dimensional physical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is solving high-dimensional parabolic partial differential equations (PDEs) by reformulating them into Backward Stochastic Differential Equations (BSDEs). This is crucial in fields like stochastic control and physics.

**Main result.** The proposed Quantum Transformer solver consistently improves upon non-attentive quantum circuit baselines, demonstrating that combining causal attention with FC-VQC provides an effective architecture for high-dimensional BSDE trajectory learning.

**Method.** The authors propose a Quantum Transformer model where trainable parameters are confined within Multi-Layer Fully-Connected Variational Quantum Circuits (FC-VQC) modules integrated into the standard Transformer structure. Causal self-attention is used to process time-coordinate tokens derived from the state trajectory.

**Model / system.** The method targets solving BSDEs associated with semilinear PDEs (e.g., Black–Scholes, Burgers-type). The quantum component uses FC-VQC circuits to parameterize the necessary mappings within the Transformer architecture.

**Key observables.** Mean Trajectory Relative L2 Error (LRE) and Relative Mean Absolute Error (RelMAE), with lower values indicating better performance across three benchmark PDEs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Model depth ($L \in \{3, 5, 7\}$) and hidden dimension ($d_m$). The comparison highlights the effect of attention versus non-attentive circuits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that structural operations (like attention mechanisms) can remain classical and parameter-free while confining all trainable parameters to scalable FC-VQC modules.

**Figures summary.** Table I compares LRE across various model configurations (DNN, FC-VQC, Transformer, QTransformer). Figure 1 illustrates the integrated Quantum Transformer architecture using FC-VQC modules in key layers.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of solving PDEs via BSDEs. It then details the novel QTransformer architecture integrating FC-VQC into the Transformer framework. Results are presented by comparing performance against classical and non-attentive quantum baselines on multiple benchmarks, concluding with an analysis of architectural components.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Solving high-dimensional parabolic partial differential equations (PDEs) is important in engineering, physics, and stochastic control. Deep BSDE methods reformulate semilinear PDEs as backward stochastic differential equations and admit a model-based reinforcement learning interpretation, where trajectories are generated from known stochastic dynamics while a trainable model learns the gradient-related control process. We propose a Quantum Transformer BSDE solver based on Multi-Layer Fully-Connected Variational Quantum Circuits (FC-VQC). The method treats the normalized state trajectory as time--coordinate tokens and applies causal self-attention to learn interactions in the adapted BSDE gradient process. All trainable model parameters are contained within the FC-VQC embedding, projection, feed-forward, and decoder modules, while attention and structural operations remain classical and parameter-free. Experiments on three d=36 PDE benchmarks show that QTransformer consistently improves over the non-attentive FC-VQC baseline and outperforms the classical Transformer at compact hidden widths, while the wider classical Transformer achieves the best overall accuracy. These results demonstrate that combining causal attention with FC-VQC provides an effective quantum architecture for high-dimensional BSDE trajectory learning.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies a machine learning approach to model working-age population growth using local municipal social statistics. It formulates this relationship as a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) problem and solves it using quantum annealing techniques. The framework provides an interpretable tool for exploring potential policy configurations, though its results are limited to model-based exploration rather than causal prediction.

**Why it may be interesting.** While applied to social science, the core methodology involves mapping complex combinatorial optimization problems onto QUBO Hamiltonians for solving with quantum annealers, which is relevant to general NISQ algorithm development.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop an interpretable, optimization-ready framework connecting municipal social indicators to working-age population growth rates in aging societies.

**Main result.** The QUBO formulation successfully models the non-linear interactions between social indicators, and quantum/simulated annealing methods consistently find the optimal configuration predicted by exact solvers.

**Method.** A quadratic surrogate model is fitted using regression on discretized social data, then converted into a QUBO Hamiltonian to be minimized via quantum or simulated annealing optimization.

**Model / system.** The system models working-age population growth rate ($y$) as a function of ten discrete social indicators ($\vec{x}$), formulated as a quadratic objective function $G(\vec{x})$ subject to binary constraints.

**Key observables.** Test-set correlation coefficient (0.84) and average R-squared value (0.76); the optimal indicator configuration vector $\vec{x}_{opt}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The penalty coefficient ($\alpha=3$) used for enforcing one-hot constraints in the QUBO formulation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework is explicitly an exploratory tool, not a causal estimate of policy interventions; the model assumes quadratic relationships between indicator levels.

**Figures summary.** Figures show observed vs. fitted growth rates, heatmaps of the coefficient matrix $Q$ detailing pairwise interactions, and local sensitivity analyses showing predicted changes based on single-indicator flips.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for modeling population decline; it then details fitting a quadratic surrogate model to social data; this model is converted into a QUBO problem; finally, optimization using quantum/simulated annealing is performed and validated against exact solvers.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The decline of the working-age population is a major challenge for regional sustainability, particularly in ageing societies such as Japan. We present a methodological demonstration of a quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO)-based framework for exploring social-indicator configurations associated with working-age population growth. Using Japanese municipal data, we regressed the 2010-2020 working-age population growth rate on ten discretized social indicators. The resulting quadratic surrogate model showed reasonable predictive performance, with a test-set correlation coefficient of 0.84 and an average R-squared value of 0.76. Its coefficient matrix provides an interpretable representation of individual indicator-level contributions and pairwise associations. We converted the fitted model into a QUBO formulation with one-hot constraints and optimized it using quantum annealing, simulated annealing, and Gurobi. All three methods identified the same optimal feasible configuration, while the annealing-based samplers also generated feasible suboptimal configurations with different predicted growth rates. Municipality-level single-indicator analyses showed that changing one indicator can increase or decrease the predicted growth rate depending on the other indicators. The framework provides an interpretable and optimization-ready approach for connecting municipal social statistics, nonlinear interactions, and model-based scenario generation. It should be regarded as an exploratory tool for policy discussion rather than as a causal estimate of policy interventions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents Verifiable Blind Probabilistic Error Cancellation (VBPEC), a novel protocol that secures Quantum Error Mitigation (QEM) when running computations on untrusted quantum hardware. It achieves this by formally integrating PEC into composable security, allowing the active cancellation of noise rather than just tolerating it. This establishes a crucial pathway toward reliable and practical delegated quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical gap between theoretical quantum algorithms and noisy, untrusted hardware by providing rigorous cryptographic verification for error mitigation techniques.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is establishing end-to-end security guarantees for quantum computations performed on untrusted, noisy hardware by integrating Quantum Error Mitigation (QEM) into verifiable protocols.

**Main result.** The paper introduces Verifiable Blind Probabilistic Error Cancellation (VBPEC), the first secure protocol to integrate PEC within a composable security framework, providing cryptographic reliability for QEM.

**Method.** It extends trap-based verification from deterministic checks to statistical tests that actively cancel noise rather than merely tolerating it, formalizing delegated mitigation as a cryptographic resource.

**Model / system.** The work addresses the abstract model of quantum computation delegated to an untrusted server. The underlying physical processes involve observable estimation ($	ext{Tr}[ho O]$) on near-future quantum hardware.

**Key observables.** Observable expectation value $	ext{Tr}[ho O]$, empirical estimator $\mu$, and security error (must be exponentially small).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Estimation error $\epsilon$ and confidence level $\delta$; the number of samples $N_c$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol assumes access to a fully malicious adversary model, requiring cryptographic guarantees for noise cancellation.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail, but the notes imply schematics illustrating the integration of PEC into verifiable estimation protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from standard observable estimation theory to Probabilistic Error Cancellation (PEC), extends this to a blind setting (BDPEC), and culminates in the universal framework (VBPEC) that provides cryptographic security guarantees for QEM.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error mitigation (QEM) is an essential tool for mitigating hardware noise without incurring space overhead. Yet, its reliability depends on modeling, calibration, and implementation, leaving end-to-end security on untrusted quantum hardware unresolved. We address this problem by introducing verifiable blind probabilistic error cancellation (VBPEC), the first secure verification protocol against a fully malicious adversary that integrates QEM. VBPEC brings probabilistic error cancellation (PEC), a widely studied QEM technique, within the scope of composable security by formalizing delegated mitigation as a cryptographic resource in the abstract cryptography framework. The protocol performs PEC with perfect blindness and an exponentially small security error. VBPEC retains the absence of quantum-space overhead from recent statistically-secure verified quantum computation protocols and from PEC. The only overhead takes the form of additional repetitions due to the QEM procedure. To achieve this, we extend trap-based verification from deterministic pass/fail checks to statistical tests that benefit from QEM and develop a new proof technique that integrates the corresponding additional deviation sources. Rather than merely tolerating honest noise below a fixed threshold, VBPEC actively cancels it, enabling correctly mitigated estimates to be accepted with high probability without compromising security. Our framework thus establishes an essential route towards secure, reliable, and practical delegated quantum computation on near-future quantum hardware: VBPEC fundamentally improves the practicality of verification.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a rigorous spectral proof of the Hypergraph Moore Bound conjecture. It uses advanced linear algebra and graph theory tools—specifically analyzing weighted, memory-augmented graphs derived from hypergraphs—to relate the density of edges to the minimum size of an 'even cover.' The result establishes fundamental combinatorial bounds applicable across various dimensions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While purely combinatorial/graph theoretical, the reliance on spectral methods (eigenvalues, walk counting) applied to structured matrices shares deep mathematical parallels with techniques used in quantum information theory for analyzing entanglement or graph properties via adjacency operators.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove Feige's conjecture on the hypergraph Moore bound, establishing absolute constants for the size of an even cover in a $k$-uniform hypergraph.

**Main result.** It proves that any sufficiently dense $k$-uniform hypergraph must contain an even cover whose size is bounded by $A\ell\log(en/\ell)$, or alternatively, the number of hyperedges must be below a specific threshold dependent on $n, k,$ and $\ell$.

**Method.** The proof employs advanced spectral graph theory techniques applied to specialized matrices (Kikuchi matrices) derived from lifting the hypergraph structure into a memory-augmented graph. It uses trace methods and walk counting arguments.

**Model / system.** The mathematical model is an abstract $k$-uniform hypergraph $H$ on $n$ vertices, analyzed via its associated Kikuchi graph defined on an $\ell$-slice of the hypercube. The analysis involves constructing a 'lifted graph with memory' to track parity constraints.

**Key observables.** Minimum size of an even cover ($	ext{gev}(H)$), average degree ($ar{d}$) of the associated Kikuchi graph, and spectral norms of derived operators ($K$ or $L$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The uniformity $k$, number of vertices $n$, hierarchy level $\ell$, and parameters $A$ and $C$ defining the bounds.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof relies on assuming no small even cover exists to derive spectral bounds, and it handles technical difficulties for odd vs. even uniformity using specific weighting schemes ($\delta_e$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the argument by first defining the problem via spectral analysis of the Kikuchi graph, then tackling the even and odd uniformity cases separately, establishing bounds on $ar{d}$ based on the non-existence of small even covers, and finally combining these results to prove the main theorem.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A nonempty subfamily of a $k$-uniform hypergraph is an \emph{even cover} if every vertex lies in an even number of its hyperedges; for $k=2$ these are edge-disjoint unions of cycles, so the minimum size of an even cover is the natural hypergraph analogue of girth. We prove Feige's 2008 conjecture on the hypergraph Moore bound: there are absolute constants $A$ and $C$ (independent of $k$) such that for every $k\ge3$ and every $1\le\ell\le n$, any $k$-uniform hypergraph on $n$ vertices with more than $C\,n^{k/2}/\ell^{k/2-1}$ hyperedges contains an even cover of size at most $A\,\ell\log(en/\ell)$. Our proof is based on sharp spectral bounds for Kikuchi matrices, which we expect to be of independent interest; we apply them to the refutation of random constraint satisfaction problems in a companion paper.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a systematic normalization method to prove the mathematical equivalence of several different potentials derived from exponential functions. By enforcing specific boundary conditions (minimum at $r_e$, finite limit at infinity), the authors show that complex, multi-parameter models collapse into a single, minimal functional form. This suggests significant redundancy in how molecular interaction potentials have been parameterized in previous research.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful mathematical tools for analyzing molecular interactions, suggesting that physical complexity can often be reduced to simpler underlying functional dependencies, which is crucial for modeling bond energies and scattering processes in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate that various molecular potentials based on exponential functions are mathematically equivalent.

**Main result.** The authors show that several complex, seemingly distinct potentials (five-parameter and six-parameter models) reduce to a single, minimal functional form dependent on only three independent parameters.

**Method.** A systematic normalization method is employed, requiring the potential to have a minimum and be finite at infinity. This leads to rewriting the potential into a 'normal form' defined by sharing the same function $f(r)$.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on molecular potentials $V(r)$ used in quantum mechanics, specifically analyzing models like the Möbius-square potential and generalized exponential forms.

**Key observables.** Dissociation energy ($D_e$), equilibrium distance ($r_e$), and force constant ($k = V''(r_e)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $D_e$, $r_e$, $\alpha$ (related to the force constant).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The potential must exhibit a minimum at some finite distance $r_e$ and approach a finite value as the radial variable $r 	o \infty$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the potential calculated using one specific model (Eq. 10) against the derived normal form equation (Eq. 13), confirming their mathematical identity.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the systematic normalization method, applies it to known potentials like the Möbius-square and five/six-parameter models, demonstrates the reduction of these complex forms to a minimal three-parameter structure, and concludes by showing parameter redundancy in previous literature.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

By means of a simple and systematic normalization method we show that some apparently different potentials based on exponential functions are equivalent. Present normalization method only requires that the potential exhibits a minimum and is finite when the radial variable tends to infinity.

</details>

**Authors:** Yanqiao Wang, Yixuan Liang, Hongjia Chen, Jin-Peng Liu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.25812v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<sub>Low-resolution page preview, page 6</sub>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Linear Combination of Hermitian Matrices (LCHM) formulations to efficiently compute functions of general, non-normal matrices $g(A)$ in the quantum domain. By developing both Vanilla and Weyl LCHM methods, the authors achieve optimal circuit depth for various quantum linear algebraic tasks. This work provides a powerful theoretical framework for advancing quantum simulation beyond standard Hermitian models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of robust, near-optimal quantum linear algebra tools like LCHM is crucial for implementing complex quantum simulations involving non-Hermitian Hamiltonians or time evolution operators that cannot be easily mapped to standard Hermitian forms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is developing optimal quantum eigenvalue transformation (QET) algorithms for general non-normal matrices, which surpasses the capabilities of standard singular value transformations.

**Main result.** The paper introduces Linear Combination of Hermitian Matrices (LCHM) formulations that achieve near-optimal circuit depth ($\Theta(d)$) and provide exact discrete formulas for matrix powers using Fourier projection techniques.

**Method.** Two complementary LCHM methods—Vanilla and Weyl—are developed to map the general non-normal function $g(A)$ onto integrals or sums involving Hermitian matrices. These are then implemented into quantum circuits achieving optimal gate complexity.

**Model / system.** The theory applies to computing functions $g(A)$ of a general matrix $A$, where $A$ is decomposed using its Hermitian and anti-Hermitian parts ($L, H$). The analysis often uses weighted-shift matrices as test cases to prove quantum advantage.

**Key observables.** Quantum query complexity, circuit depth ($\Theta(d)$), post-selection repetition count, and operator-norm error bounds ($\epsilon_{	ext{circ}}, \epsilon_{	ext{app}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree of the polynomial ($d$), the numerical radius $w(A)$, and approximation parameters like $\epsilon$ or sampling size $N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The methods rely on decomposing $A$ into Hermitian components and assume conditions (like a uniform numerical-radius gap) are met to demonstrate exponential quantum advantage.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the mathematical necessity of LCHM over standard techniques, detailing the Vanilla and Weyl formulations for $g(A)$, deriving exact discrete formulas (e.g., for $A^m$), and concluding with the complexity analysis of the resulting QET algorithms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We discover two complementary linear-combination-of-Hermitian-matrices (LCHM) formulations to achieve a general non-normal matrix eigenvalue transformation $g(A)$. Firstly, for $A=L+\mathrm{i} H$ with Hermitian $L$ and $H$, the vanilla LCHM formula represents $g(A)$ as a kernel integral of $g(\mathrm{i}(H+kL))$, and it contains linear-combination-of-Hamiltonian-simulation (LCHS) [An, Liu, Lin, Phys. Rev. Lett. 2023] as the special case for matrix exponentials. Secondly, for the angular Hermitian $X_θ= \cosθL+\sinθH$, the Weyl LCHM formula expresses $g(A)$ via integrating $g(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}θ} (X_θ\pm\mathrm{i}(I-X_θ^2)^{1/2}))$. For the matrix power $g(A)=A^m$, the Fourier projection of Weyl LCHM gives \[ A^m=\frac{2}π\int_0^π\text{e}^{\text{i} mθ}T_m(X_θ) \text{d}θ= \frac{2}{N}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1} \text{e}^{\text{i} mθ_j}T_m(X_{θ_j}),\quadθ_j=\frac{πj}{N},\quad \text{for every } N>m \] with Chebyshev polynomial of Hermitian $T_m(X_θ)$ and $N$ samples. The discrete formula is exact, introduces no truncation and angular quadrature error, and offers $\mathcal{O}(1)$ post-selection weights.   LCHM formulas lead to new quantum eigenvalue transformation (QET) algorithms. For a degree-$d$ polynomial $p_d(A)$ on $|ψ\rangle$, our QET algorithm can achieve optimal $Θ(d)$ circuit depth and optimal $\mathcal{O}(||p_d||_{\infty}/||p_d(A)|ψ\rangle||)$ post-selection repetitions. LCHM-based QETs unify various quantum linear algebraic problems with near-optimal $\mathcal{\widetilde O}(d\log(d/ε))$ Clifford$+T$ gates, including driven ODEs (reduced to standard LCHS), iterative methods, resolvents, $\log(I+A)$, $(λI+A)^ν$, Sign and ReLU transforms, and Faber approximation on noncircular domains.

</details>

**Authors:** Vincent Gierisch, Nicole Hoess, Ralf Ramsauer, Wolfgang Mauerer  
**Type:** experiment · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.25437v1>

**Summary.** This paper empirically analyzes the structure of the quantum software ecosystem by tracking developer contributions across multiple foundational repositories. Using software engineering techniques, it maps out how expertise flows between projects over time. The findings demonstrate that knowledge transfer is a key feature of this rapidly growing field, suggesting concepts persist even when individual project activity wanes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly focused on physical models, understanding the infrastructure and community dynamics supporting quantum software is crucial for any theoretical physicist needing to implement or test complex algorithms in practice.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether scientific concepts and development influence persist across the boundaries of individual software repositories within the rapidly expanding quantum software ecosystem.

**Main result.** Knowledge transfer is evident through developer migration patterns, suggesting that expertise often moves across repository boundaries rather than being confined to a single project.

**Method.** The authors applied empirical software engineering techniques by analyzing Git commit data and cross-repository contributions to map community evolution.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is the quantum software ecosystem itself, modeled as a network of interconnected repositories (projects) and developers who contribute code across them.

**Key observables.** Contributor migration patterns, cross-project knowledge transfer indications, developer roles (Exclusive vs. Cross-Project; Core vs. Peripheral), and temporal contribution activity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Commit count per developer/project/day, time elapsed between contributions in different projects.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that knowledge transfer occurs primarily through the direct, measurable activity of individual developers moving between projects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the workflow from publications to repository mapping, show the overall ecosystem network linking developers and projects, and display contribution activity over time tracing top contributors across multiple repositories.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure involving defining the problem (concept persistence), detailing the multi-stage MSR pipeline (selection $ightarrow$ extraction $ightarrow$ mapping $ightarrow$ networking $ightarrow$ temporal analysis), presenting empirical evidence of developer roles and migration, and concluding with observed development paths.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Driven by contributions from academia, industry, and open-source communities, the quantum software ecosystem is rapidly growing. Across this ecosystem, new concepts often emerge through software artefacts accompanying scientific publications as well as through sustained development in larger communities. However, many repositories receive development efforts only over a limited period of time, raising the question whether their concepts persist beyond individual repositories. In this paper, we apply established empirical software engineering techniques to analyse a set of foundational quantum software repositories. We combine cross-repository activity with contributor relationships to study the evolution of communities. Our analysis provides empirical evidence of contributor migration patterns and indications of cross-project knowledge transfer. We observe multiple development paths: projects may evolve into sustained communities, contributors may integrate concepts into established ecosystems, or activity may continue through new and follow-up software artefacts. Our observations provide an initial empirical perspective on how concepts and influence persist across repository boundaries in quantum software ecosystems.

</details>

For example, the six Cartesian d func- tions can be associated with x2, y2, z2, xy, xz, and yz. This language is convenient because the three coordinate directions can be handled separately. Spherical functions instead describe pure angular momentum.18,20,21,32 Real spherical functions are often convenient in nonrelativistic molecular calculations because the orbitals and integrals can remain real....</sub>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2607.25245_figures/2607.25245_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>
