The conventional route projects to the real-spherical basis after integral formation, whereas the direct route works in the target shell dimension from the start. A phase- and ordering-controlled real-to-complex transformation then exposes |l, m⟩ pair blocks for low-rank, local-Hamiltonian, and spinor or downfolding applications.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2607.25245_figures/2607.25245_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 3. Four ratios determined by angular representation for equal-l shells from d through h. Panel (a) shows the percentage reduction in final spherical versus Cartesian tensor entries. Panel (b) shows the percentage of complex-spherical entries that satisfy the total-M rule. Panel (c) compares the number of independent Slater–Condon radial parameters with the number of entries in the full spherical tensor. It counts independent inputs rather than retained tensor entries. Panel (d) compares a rough operation count for blockwise pair-space updates with the corresponding dense count. Together, the panels isolate representation effects from implementation-dependent timing.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 5</summary>

<img src="2607.25245_figures/2607.25245_fig5.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 4. Coulomb matrices in pair space for one-center d, f, g, and h shells before and after the reversible real-to-complex spherical transformation. Pair states are sorted by total magnetic quantum number M. In the real-spherical basis, nonzero entries are spread across the matrices. In the complex |l, m⟩basis, they collect into the blocks required by m1 +m2 = m3 +m4. Color shows the base-10 logarithm of each magnitude relative to the largest entry in that matrix. In a molecule, coupling between different blocks would appear as measurable intensity outside the bright block pattern.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces solid-harmonic integral engines to revolutionize the calculation of electron-repulsion integrals in electronic structure theory. By working directly in spherical coordinates, these engines generate smaller, more structured Coulomb tensors than traditional methods. This structural preservation capability creates a vital link between efficient integral generation and complex many-electron algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** The emphasis on preserving angular structure metadata through computation is crucial for any advanced quantum simulation or correlated electron calculation that must map atomic physics onto abstract computational bases (like qubits).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is efficiently calculating electron-repulsion integrals (ERIs) for electronic structure theory, which are computationally intensive.

**Main result.** Solid-harmonic integral engines working directly in the spherical basis produce a significantly smaller and more structured Coulomb tensor compared to traditional Cartesian methods. This provides a direct bridge between efficient integral generation and subsequent many-electron computations.

**Method.** The paper proposes using 'direct real or complex solid-harmonic integral engines' that operate natively in the target spherical angular space, bypassing large intermediate tensors generated by standard Gaussian basis function transformations from Cartesian coordinates.

**Model / system.** The model is the Coulomb interaction between charge distributions built from orbital basis functions (Gaussian type orbitals), relevant for calculating electronic structure in atoms and molecules. The focus is on managing the tensor representation of these two-electron interactions.

**Key observables.** Final Coulomb tensor size, angular block counts, radial Slater--Condon parameters, and pair-space organization, all quantified relative to Cartesian representations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Angular momentum quantum numbers ($l$), magnetic quantum numbers ($m$), and the ratio of spherical to Cartesian tensor entries ($R_{	ext{tensor}}(l)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes ideal one-center shells for deriving structural rules, though it acknowledges that molecular environments can break perfect rotational symmetry.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 summarizes the history of ERI design across different computational methods. Figure 2 illustrates the data path connecting integral generation to low-rank preprocessing and model transformations, highlighting structure preservation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the bottleneck in traditional Cartesian ERI calculation (generating overly large intermediate tensors) to introducing solid-harmonic engines that work directly in spherical space. It then quantifies the structural advantages using symmetry rules (Total-M rule, radial parameters) and concludes by showing how this structured output supports downstream algorithms like low-rank factorization and spinor transformations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electron-repulsion integrals describe the Coulomb interaction between charge distributions built from orbital basis functions. Most integral algorithms generate these quantities through Cartesian Gaussian functions, whose angular shapes are written as powers of $x$, $y$, and $z$, and then transform the result to spherical functions. This route is effective, but from $d$ shells onward the Cartesian representation contains more functions than the spherical space required by the calculation. Direct real or complex solid-harmonic engines work in that target space from the beginning. They therefore produce a smaller final Coulomb tensor while preserving the ordering, phase, and magnetic-quantum-number labels that describe its angular structure. Following this structure beyond integral evaluation reveals direct connections to the algorithms that use the tensor. Simple analytical counts quantify tensor size, angular blocks, radial Slater--Condon parameters, and pair-space work. These quantities guide low-rank factorization, local Hamiltonian construction, quantum simulation, and transformations to spinor or effective-model bases. In this way, solid-harmonic integral engines provide a direct bridge between efficient integral generation and structured many-electron computation.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using controlled strain engineering as a mechanism to create and program robust chiral topological channels deep within the bulk of a material. By inducing internal topological domain walls, these channels bypass the limitations imposed by physical boundaries and disorder. This opens a pathway toward realizing reconfigurable, all-solid-state topological quantum circuitry.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a purely mechanical paradigm for controlling quantum transport features, offering an alternative to lithography or external fields for realizing complex topological circuits relevant to open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Ideal zero-dissipation topological edge channels are hindered by disorder at physical boundaries, necessitating a method to create robust interior chiral channels.

**Main result.** Strain engineering allows for the creation of topologically protected domain walls in the bulk, generating interior channels immune to boundary disorder and enabling programmable channel geometries like Mach-Zehnder interferometers.

**Method.** The study uses theoretical quantum transport simulations based on tight-binding Hamiltonians modified by controlled mechanical strain fields (gradient strain).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using the Haldane model, realized in ferromagnetic honeycomb insulators. Strain ($\epsilon(y)$) is applied to induce topological domain walls within the bulk material.

**Key observables.** Quantized transmission coefficient ($T=1$), channel width scaling ($\sigma \propto 1/\sqrt{
abla\epsilon}$), and the ability to program channel position and splitting/merging geometries.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strain gradient field $\epsilon(y)$, Chern number $|C|$, and disorder strength $W t_1 /$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on continuum approximations for width scaling, and initial models assume constant phases ($\phi$) during lattice deformation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structure evolution under uniform strain, the formation of interior domain walls via gradient strain, robustness against disorder (quantized T), and controlled splitting/recombination circuits in high-Chern-number phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing uniform strain effects on topological invariants to introducing a continuous linear gradient strain field. This enables the writing of internal domain walls, which are then shown to host robust chiral channels whose properties (width, position) can be quantitatively tuned for circuit design.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Accumulated disorder at physical boundaries prevents the realization of ideal zero-dissipation topological edge channels. Here, we propose a strain engineering mechanism to create a topological domain wall in the pristine material bulk, thereby generating interior chiral channels spatially decoupled from physical boundaries. Quantum transport simulations reveal that these interior channels exhibit exceptional immunity to severe boundary disorder, maintaining an ideal vortex-free transport morphology. Furthermore, the spatial position and confinement of these interior channels can be quantitatively programmed. Under a linear gradient strain field, the channel width obeys an inverse square-root scaling law with respect to the strain gradient. In a high-Chern-number phase (|C|=2), we design the spatial splitting and merging of co-propagating chiral channels, suggesting a topological Mach-Zehnder-like geometry. These results suggest a route toward programmable bulk topological transport and reconfigurable topological circuitry.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models developmental biology using the language of statistical physics, framing cell fate determination within an epigenetic free energy landscape. It shows that both environmental gradients and direct cell-cell signaling can be mathematically unified under this framework. The paper argues that robustness in development emerges through fine-tuning near critical points, which is then refined by specific intercellular interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of free energy landscapes and concepts like criticality ($T ightarrow T_c$) to describe biological pattern formation is highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and understanding emergent order from local interactions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how environmental cues and cell-cell signaling shape developmental trajectories across morphogenetic landscapes.

**Main result.** It establishes that the free energy landscape provides a powerful, unifying framework for describing development by mapping diverse biological mechanisms (like signaling or adhesion) to common physical principles.

**Method.** The authors model cell fate determination using concepts from statistical physics, approximating the probability distribution via an underlying free energy function $\phi(m)$.

**Model / system.** Development is modeled using Waddington's epigenetic landscape concept, simplified into a low-dimensional system analyzed via analogies to the Ising Model and spin systems.

**Key observables.** Cell fate determination (A vs. B identity), developmental regimes (bistable, co-existence, critical), susceptibility ($\chi$), and patterning error ($\delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The effective temperature ($eta$) and parameters governing the free energy function $\phi(m)$, such as $K$ and $\sigma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include assuming a Gaussian distribution for the probability distribution $P(m)$ and using first-order expansions near interfaces.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates Waddington's landscape concept, while Figure 2 demonstrates the Ising model analogy applied to cell commitment based on external fields and signaling modulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general epigenetic landscape framework, detailing how morphogen gradients modify the free energy potential, introducing cell-cell interactions (like Eph-Ephrin) as additional terms in the energy functional, and finally demonstrating the universality of this landscape description across different cellular mechanisms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Despite the variability in gene regulation and environmental conditions, development of an organism occurs through a sequence of highly coordinated patterning processes. Cells integrate different signals to accurately infer their position in order to adopt an appropriate identity. Using a model of epigenetic landscape originally proposed by Waddington to describe cell-fate determination, we establish the critical role played by juxtacrine signaling between cells in determining tissue patterns. Subsequently we systematically coarse-grain the model at the tissue scale to map its patterning to transition between states in a binary spin model having a free energy landscape. We show that such landscapes serve as a powerful unifying framework for describing development of biological systems across distinct spatio-temporal scales.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops an advanced theoretical model to analyze how domain wall velocity changes anisotropically during magnetic bubble expansion. By incorporating both dispersive stiffness and chiral damping into the creep model, the authors provide a more sensitive method for extracting fundamental parameters like the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya Interaction (DMI). The findings emphasize that accurate material characterization requires accounting for all physical contributions simultaneously.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the primary focus is magnetism, the rigorous mathematical separation of multiple interacting energy/dissipation terms in a non-equilibrium system (creep dynamics) mirrors complex modeling challenges found in open quantum systems or driven many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a comprehensive framework for interpreting creep-driven domain expansion that accurately separates the contributions of chiral damping, dispersive domain wall stiffness, and Dzyaloshinskii–Moriya interaction (DMI) to the anisotropic domain wall velocity profile v(theta).

**Main result.** The extended model successfully separates elastic/stiffness effects from dissipative (chiral damping) effects in the angular velocity profile, showing that rigorous DMI extraction requires accounting for both chiral damping and stiffness.

**Method.** The authors developed an extended angular creep model incorporating dispersive domain wall stiffness and chirality-dependent prefactors to analyze measured full angular velocity profiles v(theta).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on ultrathin ferromagnetic heterostructures with perpendicular magnetic anisotropy (PMA), analyzing asymmetric bubble expansion dynamics. The theoretical framework extends the standard creep model by adding terms for dispersive stiffness and chiral damping.

**Key observables.** Angular dependence of the domain wall velocity, v(theta); normalized angular velocity profile; separation into chiral damping term, prefactor, and creep exponential.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya Interaction (DMI) field (H_DMI), chiral damping coefficient ($\alpha_{CD}$), domain-wall stiffness ($	ilde{\sigma}$), and the creep exponent parameter ($\chi_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the bubble domain is large enough to ignore curvature-induced effects, and that independent measurements of DMI from flow regimes are available for comparison.

**Figures summary.** Figures show schematic setups for measuring v(theta) and plots comparing experimental angular velocity profiles against model predictions derived using energy-only vs. full models incorporating stiffness and chiral damping.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for an extended creep model to account for anisotropy in domain wall motion. It then details how this model separates contributions from various physical effects (stiffness, damping) by analyzing v(theta). Finally, it compares these findings with independent DMI estimates derived from flow-regime measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The asymmetric expansion of magnetic bubble domains in ultrathin ferromagnets provides a powerful route to probe the interfacial Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction (DMI). While conventional analyses rely on domain wall velocities measured along selected directions as a function of in-plane field, recent approaches have highlighted the additional insight contained in the angular dependence of the velocity, $v(θ)$. Here, we develop an extended angular creep model that incorporates both the dispersive domain wall stiffness and a chirality-dependent prefactor associated with chiral damping. This generalization captures the full anisotropic dynamics of domain wall motion around a bubble domain. We show that these contributions significantly modify the angular velocity profile and can lead to features not accessible within existing models. Our results establish a more complete framework for interpreting creep-driven domain expansion and provide improved sensitivity for the quantitative extraction of DMI and chiral dynamical effects.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel theoretical framework to calculate proximity effects in layered heterostructures by deriving a dynamical, frequency-dependent coupling operator directly from first-principles DFT. This method overcomes the limitations of static parameter fitting, accurately capturing energy and momentum dependence through virtual hybridization pathways. The results demonstrate that this approach reveals crucial physical details, such as resonant frequency dependencies and bond-resolved couplings, unattainable with simpler models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, first-principles pathway to construct effective low-energy Hamiltonians for complex quantum interfaces, which is crucial input for modeling open quantum systems dynamics in solid-state devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming the limitations of using static parameters to model proximity effects in layered heterostructures, which fails to capture the energy dependence or momentum transfer from virtual hybridization.

**Main result.** The authors developed a method that successfully derives a dynamical proximity operator, showing that virtual hybridization dominates coupling strengths (e.g., >99% for Co/graphene), providing an accurate, fitting-free microscopic foundation for low-energy modeling.

**Method.** They derive the dynamical proximity operator V(k, k'; ω) by performing Wannier downfolding of the full Kohn-Sham Hamiltonian onto a target low-energy subspace using a shifted resolvent approach.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on proximity effects in van der Waals heterostructures like graphene/hBN/Co(0001), graphene/PtSe2, and graphene/WSe2. The method yields an effective Hamiltonian describing the low-energy physics within the target Wannier subspace.

**Key observables.** Dynamical proximity operator V(k, k'; ω); renormalized low-energy spectrum; sublattice-selective intervalley coupling ($	ext{PtSe}_2$); bond-resolved Rashba coupling (WSe$_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Resonant frequency dependence set by substrate $d$ states; $	ext{meV}$-scale splittings vs. $<1\ \mu	ext{eV}$ direct projection.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on projecting the full DFT Hamiltonian onto a fixed, low-energy Wannier subspace, treating the coupling via virtual transitions through higher energy bands.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates graphene on hBN/Co(0001), showing band structures and spectral functions comparing direct projection results to the full dynamical calculation.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the failure of static models, details the derivation of the dynamical proximity operator via Wannier downfolding (using DFT and resolvent techniques), applies this framework to multiple physical systems ($	ext{Co}$, $	ext{PtSe}_2$, $	ext{WSe}_2$), and concludes by establishing the method's utility for various transport theories.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Proximity effects in layered heterostructures are usually represented by static parameters fitted to first-principles bands, which discards the energy dependence of the virtual hybridization, the momentum transfer, and the spatial structure. We overcome this limitation by deriving a dynamical proximity operator $\mathcal{V}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}';ω)$ directly from density functional theory, downfolding the Kohn-Sham Hamiltonian of the heterostructure onto a fixed low-energy target Wannier subspace and reproducing its spectrum exactly within that subspace. The construction separates direct matrix elements from virtual hybridization through all remaining states. In graphene on hBN/Co(0001), virtual hybridization generates more than $99\%$ of the proximity exchange and gives it a resonant frequency dependence set by the Co $d$ states. In graphene/PtSe$_2$ it resolves a sublattice-selective intervalley coupling with a $\sqrt{3}\times\sqrt{3}$ charge modulation, and in graphene/WSe$_2$ a bond-resolved Rashba coupling of $0.24$~meV, against below $1$~$μ$eV for the direct projection alone. Our results expose the limitations of static projections and establish a fitting-free microscopic foundation for low-energy modeling, spin-relaxation theory, and transport calculations.

</details>

**Summary.** This review unifies the study of various two-component degrees of freedom—like spin and valley pseudospin—under a common SU(2) geometric language. It demonstrates that topological invariants are not merely descriptive labels but function as an 'engineering design language.' This allows for the prediction and control of physical responses, such as tunable gaps or spin currents, by engineering wavefunction continuity constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a unifying mathematical and physical framework (SU(2) geometry) to understand how symmetry constraints dictate macroscopic electronic properties like spin currents and optical responses across different quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a common geometric language for understanding the winding of various two-component degrees of freedom (spin, pseudospin, valley) that share the SU(2) geometry.

**Main result.** Topology is argued to be not just a classification tool but an 'engineering design language' enabling engineered functionality in quantum materials.

**Method.** The review develops this framework by analyzing Berry phase, Berry curvature, and related geometric tensors across multiple physical systems.

**Model / system.** The analysis uses low-energy effective Hamiltonians for graphene, topological insulators (TIs), and Weyl semimetals (WSMs) to model two-component wavefunctions whose underlying geometry is SU(2).

**Key observables.** Chern number ($C$), Berry curvature ($\Omega$), intrinsic transverse conductivity ($\sigma^{int}$), and photocurrent ($J_{CPGE}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Symmetry preservation, pseudospin matching, gate voltage (for gap tuning), and magnetic field strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that symmetry preservation dictates wavefunction continuity constraints, which govern physical responses like transmission or torque generation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate topological winding textures in momentum/real space for various systems (graphene, TIs, Weyl nodes) and show how breaking symmetries controls transport phenomena like Klein tunneling and spin currents.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the common SU(2) geometric language using Berry phase to applying this framework across diverse examples: graphene, TIs, WSMs, and skyrmions, culminating in the argument for topology as a design principle.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin, pseudospin, valley, polarization, and other two-component degrees of freedom share the geometry of SU(2), yet their topological manifestations are usually discussed as separate phenomena. This review develops a common geometric language for their winding in momentum and real space,beginning with Berry phase and the Dirac Hamiltonian and extending to graphene, topological insulators, Weyl semimetals, and magnetic skyrmions. We argue that the common thread is not merely topology itself, but the continuity constraints imposed on two-component wavefunctions. Whenever the relevant symmetry is preserved, winding determines which states can continuously connect across an interface or deformation, thereby governing transmission, torque generation, optical selection rules, and other physical responses. We then ask a practical question: what does topology buy an engineer? In skyrmions, winding partitions magnetic configuration space and stabilizes ultrasmall information carriers with tunable dynamics. In graphene, pseudospin matching governs Klein tunneling, enabling a gate-controlled transmission gap without sacrificing the massless Dirac dispersion. In topological insulators and Weyl semimetals, spin-momentum locking and Berry-curvature engineering generate electrically selectable spin currents, while helicity-dependent optical transitions produce circular photogalvanic responses. Together, these examples suggest that topology is not merely a classification of quantum matter, but a design language in which symmetry-protected wavefunction continuity can be engineered for memory, switching, actuation, and sensing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper extends the topological characterization of Majorana zero modes from Hermitian to non-Hermitian superconductors by defining a nonlocal Majorana polarization. Using biorthogonal eigenstates, the authors show this metric can distinguish between true MZMs, trivial states, and exceptional points in NH systems. The key finding is that non-Hermiticity enhances MZM robustness, providing a quantitative measure for this effect.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, generalized diagnostic tool for characterizing topology in open quantum systems, which is highly relevant for understanding superconducting devices where dissipation and gain are present.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work extends the concept of nonlocal Majorana polarization (MP), a topological indicator for Majorana zero modes (MZMs) in Hermitian systems, to the non-Hermitian (NH) realm.

**Main result.** The nonlocal MP successfully distinguishes between MZMs, trivial zero-energy states (TZESs), and exceptional points (EPs) in NH superconductors. Furthermore, non-Hermiticity was shown to enhance MZM robustness via this polarization measure.

**Method.** The authors generalized the definition of nonlocal MP by incorporating biorthogonal eigenstates and introduced a 'nonlocal MP sensitivity' ($R$) metric to quantify the contribution of non-Hermiticity.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes non-Hermitian superconducting systems, specifically utilizing models like the Hatano-Nelson-Kitaev (HNK) chain and the NH Rashba superconductor.

**Key observables.** Nonlocal Majorana Polarization ($P_{ab}$), Nonlocal MP Sensitivity ($R$), which are used to diagnose topological features.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis explores different regimes, including the sweet spot ($\mu=0$) and finite chemical potential ($\mu 
eq 0$), as well as varying non-Hermiticity parameters (e.g., $\Gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the nonlocal MP formalism can be robustly generalized from Hermitian to the biorthogonal framework required for NH systems.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows schematics of various non-Hermitian superconducting chains studied (HNK, Rashba). Figure 2 illustrates how the nonlocal MP and sensitivity indicators ($R^2$, $P_{ab}^2$) vary with non-Hermiticity ($\Gamma$) and chemical potential ($\mu$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework by generalizing the nonlocal MP using biorthogonal eigenstates. It then applies this tool to specific NH models (HNK, Rashba) to diagnose topological states, concluding with quantitative results showing how non-Hermiticity affects MZM robustness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The nonlocal Majorana polarization, defined as the product of the expectation values of the particle-hole operator at opposite halves of the system, has been shown to be a reliable topological indicator that determines the presence and quality of Majorana zero modes in Hermitian topological superconducting setups. In this work, we extend the concept of nonlocal Majorana polarization to the non-Hermitian realm by taking into account the biorthogonal eigenstates and demonstrate its utility by exploring distinct non-Hermitian superconducting systems. In particular, we show that the Majorana polarization can distinguish between Majorana zero modes, trivial zero-energy states, and exceptional points in non-Hermitian superconductors. Also, we introduce the concept of nonlocal Majorana polarization sensitiviy for characterizing the contribution of non-Hermiticity to Majorana polarization. As a byproduct, we find that non-Hermiticity enhances Majorana zero modes robustness, a property captured by the nonlocal Majorana polarization.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel, threshold-free two-dimensional criterion based on the variance of recombination rate and relative quantum yield to quantify luminescence blinking in semiconductor quantum dots. By mapping different physical blinking mechanisms onto this parameter space, it offers a robust tool for assessing emission stability without relying on ambiguous ON/OFF state definitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, quantitative metric for characterizing open quantum systems dynamics (blinking) that moves beyond simple state counting, which is highly relevant to understanding decoherence and stability in solid-state emitters.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Luminescence blinking remains a major obstacle for colloidal quantum dots, and existing quantitative assessment methods based on arbitrary intensity thresholds are ambiguous.

**Main result.** The authors propose a robust, threshold-free two-dimensional criterion using the variance of the recombination rate and relative quantum yield to quantitatively classify different blinking mechanisms.

**Method.** They derive and apply this 2D criterion $(\hat{\xi}, \langle\hat{Q}angle^{(s)})$ by analyzing time-correlated single-photon counting data, comparing it against simulations covering A-type, Trapping Mechanism (TM), and Hot Carrier (HC) blinking.

**Model / system.** The system is colloidal semiconductor quantum dots exhibiting luminescence blinking. The analysis models the dynamics using instantaneous quantum yield $Y(t)$ and recombination rate $\Gamma(t)$, which are extracted from single-photon counting data.

**Key observables.** Variance of recombination rate ($\hat{\xi}$), self-consistent relative quantum yield ($\langle\hat{Q}angle^{(s)}$), ON/OFF time distributions, and the two-dimensional parameter space map.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The criterion distinguishes regimes: $\hat{\xi} \approx 1$ (constant rate), $\hat{\xi} = 0$ (two-state switching), and $(\hat{\xi} \approx 1, \langle\hat{Q}angle^{(s)} \approx 1)$ for complete suppression.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The self-consistent estimator for quantum yield is preferred over naive estimators due to bias reduction; the analysis relies on extracting time-dependent rates from binned data.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the 2D blinking criterion diagram color-coding different mechanisms (Blue, White, Green, etc.). Other figures compare statistical diagnostics like ON/OFF distributions and Power Spectral Density for simulated TM blinking.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of existing threshold methods. It then introduces the two-dimensional $(\hat{\xi}, \langle\hat{Q}angle^{(s)})$ criterion, deriving the necessary estimators by comparing naive vs. self-consistent approaches. Finally, it validates this criterion by simulating and mapping known blinking mechanisms (A-type, TM, HC).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Colloidal semiconductor quantum dots are promising materials for numerous applications due to their tunable emission and high quantum yields. However, emission instability in the form of luminescence blinking remains a significant obstacle to their practical implementation. While recent advances in synthesis and surface engineering have demonstrated partial or complete blinking suppression, the quantitative assessment of the "quality" of this suppression remains challenging. Existing criteria typically rely on threshold-based classification into ON and OFF states, which becomes inherently ambiguous owing to the continuous distribution of emission intensities in single quantum dots. Here, we propose a threshold-free two-dimensional quantitative criterion based on the variance of the recombination rate and the relative quantum yield, both of which can be extracted from standard time-correlated single-photon counting measurements. We validate the criterion using simulated blinking trajectories covering all major blinking mechanisms and demonstrate that it cleanly separates them into distinct regions of the two-dimensional parameter space while providing a quantitative measure of the degree of suppression. This approach directly quantifies the temporal stability of emission without requiring arbitrary state definitions, thereby providing a robust metric for comparing different blinking suppression strategies.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides element-specific visualization of magnetism in few-layer $	ext{CrSBr}$ using XMCD/XLD-PEEM. It reveals that magnetic order is critically dependent on the layer parity and that twisting the layers fundamentally alters the magnetic anisotropy, opening avenues for designing novel moiré spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter magnetism, the study's focus on emergent phenomena (moiré magnetism) and the use of advanced spectroscopic probes to map spin structure at interfaces are highly relevant to understanding quantum materials for future quantum technologies.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the lack of element-specific, nanoscale characterization of spin structure and layer-parity effects in atomically thin van der Waals (vdW) antiferromagnets.

**Main result.** The magnetic order is found to be strongly dependent on layer parity, with odd layers exhibiting net magnetization while even layers remain compensated AFM. Furthermore, the twist angle significantly modifies the magnetic anisotropy energy (MAE), suggesting novel moiré magnetism pathways.

**Method.** The research combines advanced experimental techniques like X-ray Magnetic Circular/Linear Dichroism Photoemission Electron Microscopy (XMCD/XLD-PEEM) with theoretical calculations using DFT and Green's function methods to model exchange interactions.

**Model / system.** The physical system is atomically thin $	ext{CrSBr}$ in various layer numbers ($1	ext{L}-4	ext{L}$) and twisted bilayer configurations. Theoretical modeling utilizes the Heisenberg Hamiltonian and calculates MAE using the force theorem, incorporating spin-orbit coupling.

**Key observables.** XMCD asymmetry (magnetic moments), XLD contrast (crystal field effects/anisotropy), Net magnetization direction, Magnetic Anisotropy Energy (MAE), Exchange coupling constants ($J_{ij}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Layer number (parity dependence), Twist angle ($	heta$), Temperature ($50	ext{ K}$ to $200	ext{ K}$), External magnetic field strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed magnetism is governed by intrinsic layer-dependent coupling mechanisms, and DFT calculations provide an accurate description of exchange interactions across different lattice parameters/twists.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate graphene encapsulation effects on XMCD, layer-by-layer magnetic switching behavior (odd vs. even layers), and how the $	ext{XMCD}$ signal is suppressed or altered in orthogonally twisted bilayer regions based on the azimuthal angle ($\phi$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for nanoscale characterization to presenting experimental evidence of layer-parity dependence, then detailing the unique magnetic response observed in twisted bilayers, and finally supporting these findings with detailed DFT calculations of exchange interactions and MAE.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Van der Waals (vdW) based antiferromagnets (AFMs) are an ideal platform for probing and understanding thickness- and twist-angle-dependent emergent spin phenomena. However, element-specific nanoscale characterization of the spin structure in atomically thin vdW-based AFMs systems and layer-parity effects remain elusive, making them crucial for both fundamental insight into low-dimensional magnetism and the rational design of spintronic devices based on these materials. Here, we utilize X-ray magnetic circular and linear dichroisms paired with photoemission electron microscopy to resolve the magnetic order in atomically thin CrSBr. Our comprehensive measurements reveal CrSBr magnetic structure at the nanoscale and its dependence on the layer number, surface encapsulation, temperature, and applied field. Moreover, in the orthogonally twisted bilayer configuration, obtained by twisting two CrSBr ferromagnetic monolayers by 90$^\circ$, the magnetic easy axis fundamentally differs from the individual monolayers, unlocking a new pathway for moiré magnetism.

</details>

**Summary.** This thesis comprehensively analyzes electronic and spin transport in complex graphene structures exhibiting super- or quasiperiodicity. It demonstrates that disorder can paradoxically enhance conductivity in magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene while identifying unique signatures of quasicrystalline order. Overall, it unifies the understanding of how geometry and structural imperfections govern quantum transport phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a detailed framework for understanding how geometric frustration and randomness interact with quantum coherence, which is central to open quantum system dynamics and localization studies.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how structural complexities like superperiodicity, quasiperiodicity, and disorder fundamentally shape electronic and spin transport in graphene-based materials.

**Main result.** The work establishes that moderate disorder can counterintuitively enhance the mean free path in magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene, while quasicrystalline order exhibits distinct sub-ballistic localization signatures fragile to structural perturbations.

**Method.** The research employs large-scale real-space quantum-transport methods, utilizing Kubo-type formulations and tight-binding descriptions incorporating curvature effects.

**Model / system.** The primary systems studied are graphene, specifically in its twisted multilayer form (including magic-angle interfaces), quasicrystal approximants, and suspended monolayer sheets for spin transport analysis.

**Key observables.** Mean free path, quantum metric derived from optical conductivity, sub-ballistic exponent, and spin lifetimes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder strength (Anderson disorder), structural periodicity (super/quasiperiodicity), and interlayer proximity effects.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on established transport formalisms and assumes that flat-band features remain robust enough to exhibit specific disorder-induced delocalization behavior.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the work builds upon establishing controlled transport fingerprints distinguishing various periodic modulations.

**Paper structure.** The thesis progresses from fundamental concepts (superperiodicity/quasiperiodicity) to computational methods, analyzing controlled models, then focusing sequentially on disorder effects in twisted multilayer graphene and spin transport in corrugated monolayer systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This thesis investigates how superperiodicity, quasiperiodicity, and disorder shape electronic and spin transport in graphene-based systems, with an emphasis on experimentally relevant length scales and realistic atomistic modeling. Using large-scale real-space quantum-transport methods, it first establishes controlled transport fingerprints that distinguish conventional Bloch propagation in periodic structures from the anomalous dynamics induced by quasiperiodic modulations. Building on this framework, the thesis analyzes magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene and shows that, within a finite disorder window where flat-band features remain robust, moderate Anderson disorder can counterintuitively enhance the mean free path. This disorder-induced delocalization is further linked to changes in the quantum metric extracted from optical conductivity, revealing a direct connection between transport, electronic geometry, and the real-space extent of the underlying states. The study then turns to graphene quasicrystal approximants and hybrid multilayer stacks, identifying sub-ballistic transport and self-similar localization patterns as signatures of quasicrystalline order, while also demonstrating their strong fragility against disorder and interlayer proximity effects. Finally, the thesis addresses spin transport in suspended monolayer graphene, showing that atomic-scale corrugations generate short-range fluctuating Rashba fields that can limit spin lifetimes to the nanosecond range even when charge transport remains close to ballistic. Taken together, these results provide a unified picture of how geometry, disorder, and structural complexity govern transport phenomena in twisted and corrugated graphene systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the relative hybridization coordinate $Z$ as a powerful diagnostic tool to bridge the gap between abstract global topological invariants and concrete, sector-specific microscopic details of electronic bands. By analyzing the momentum-space texture of $Z$, researchers can robustly calculate quantities like the Chern number while explicitly tracking which physical components (e.g., orbital or spin sectors) contribute to the observed topology.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state topology, the concept of using a local coordinate system ($Z$) derived from projection operators to diagnose global properties is highly relevant for characterizing entanglement structures or effective Hamiltonians in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Global topological diagnostics (like Berry curvature) characterize band geometry but fail to specify the underlying microscopic sectors responsible for these invariants.

**Main result.** The relative hybridization coordinate $Z$ provides a sector-resolved framework that connects global topology and geometry directly to local, microscopic degrees of freedom within the occupied Bloch subspace.

**Method.** The authors introduce $Z$, defined as a projector-level diagnostic (Grassmann graph coordinate) relating the occupied subspace to chosen reference sectors ($A$ vs. $B$). Topological invariants are then read from defects in the momentum-space texture of $Z$.

**Model / system.** The framework is applied generally to Bloch subspaces in condensed matter systems, with specific tests on the Qi-Wu-Zhang (QWZ) Chern insulator and the lattice BHZ model.

**Key observables.** Relative hybridization coordinate $Z$, quantum geometric tensors ($T_{ij}$), chart viability field ($\sigma_{\min}(P_{AA})$), and topological invariants like the first Chern number derived from defect winding.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension matching between sectors ($	ext{dim } A$ vs. $N_{	ext{occ}}$) is crucial for defining the coordinate $Z$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on fixing a microscopic partition of the Hilbert space ($H = A \oplus B$) and assuming the existence of open patches where key projections are invertible.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates that the magnitude of $Z$ measures local mixing strength, while its phase retains coherent information about sector-resolved hybridization.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the mathematical formalism for $Z$, derives geometric tensors from it, and then applies this machinery to calculate known topological invariants (Chern number via defect counting) in benchmark models like QWZ and BHZ.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Global diagnostics such as Berry curvature and quantum metrics characterize the geometry and topology of an occupied Bloch subspace, leaving the microscopic sectors that carry this structure implicit. We introduce the relative hybridization coordinate $Z$ as a projector-level diagnostic connecting these global quantities to local degrees of freedom. As the Grassmann graph coordinate relative to a chosen sector, $Z$ reconstructs the local projector and retains the phase and matrix orientation absent from ordinary weight or fat-band descriptions. On valid chart patches, its momentum-space texture encodes Berry curvature, quantum metric, Berry phases, and Wilson loops, while chart obstructions appear as rank-drop defects whose balanced-chart winding of $\det Z$ gives the first Chern number. In the QWZ model this defect inventory reproduces the Chern phase diagram. In the lattice BHZ model, matrix $Z$ diagnoses the orbital $E|H$ partition as a robust matched chart for the QSH geometry, while the spin partition remains essential to the block and $\mathbb Z_2$ interpretation and shows rank deficiency as a matched chart in the spin-conserving limit. The relative hybridization coordinate thus provides a sector-resolved framework for relating band geometry and topology to microscopic structure.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes that nonlinear third-order electrical transport in centrosymmetric materials is dictated by Quantum Metric Quadrupoles derived from the quantum geometry of electron wavefunctions. The authors theoretically model this effect using Dirac fermions and experimentally validate it by measuring robust third-harmonic generation in elemental bismuth thin films at room temperature. This demonstrates that third-order nonlinearity serves as a high-precision diagnostic tool for probing wavefunction geometry under ambient conditions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the explicit link between macroscopic measurable quantities (third-harmonic current) and fundamental quantum geometric invariants (quantum metric quadrupoles) provides a powerful, non-perturbative probe of electronic structure geometry.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates nonlinear transport properties in solids, linking them directly to the quantum geometry of electronic wavefunctions encoded in the quantum metric tensor.

**Main result.** Third-order nonlinear transport is governed by Quantum Metric Quadrupoles (QMQs), which were experimentally observed as robust third-harmonic generation in elemental bismuth thin films up to room temperature.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical derivation of third-order conductivity tensors with extensive experimental measurements using electrical harmonic detection and THz spectroscopy on patterned thin films.

**Model / system.** The focus is on non-magnetic, centrosymmetric materials hosting relativistic fermions, modeled by bulk Dirac fermions (non-Abelian) and surface states (Abelian). The experiment uses elemental bismuth thin films with polycrystalline structure.

**Key observables.** Third-order nonlinear conductivity tensor ($\sigma_{x;xxx}$), third-harmonic voltage ($U_{x;xxx}$), and the comparison between QMQ and Nonlinear Drude contributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature operation, polycrystallinity (allowing domain averaging survival), and carrier density dependence on $n$-type carriers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The longitudinal third-order response survives domain averaging in polycrystalline films, unlike second-harmonic responses; the QMQ contribution dominates over other nonlinear terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare theoretical contributions (QMQ vs NLD), show BZ structures and surface splitting, and present experimental data on $\sigma_{x;xxx}$ dependence on current and temperature for bismuth thin films.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the quantum geometric origin of nonlinear transport to developing models for bulk/surface contributions, culminating in detailed electrical and THz measurements on elemental bismuth to validate the QMQ diagnostic tool.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The nonlinear transport properties of solids are deeply rooted in the quantum geometry of their electronic wavefunctions, which is encoded in the quantum geometric tensor. Its real part, known as the quantum metric, has been recently identified as a primary origin of nonlinear transport in quantum materials where time-reversal and inversion symmetries are not simultaneously present. Consequently, the influence of the quantum metric on the largest class of materials -- non-magnetic and centrosymmetric systems -- has remained entirely elusive. Here, we demonstrate that third-order transport in centrosymmetric materials hosting relativistic fermions is governed by quantum metric quadrupoles (QMQs). We show that these QMQs can originate from both the non-Abelian quantum geometry of bulk three-dimensional Dirac fermions and the Abelian quantum geometry of spin-orbit-coupled surface states. In stark contrast to all zero-field nonlinear transport signatures known to date, the current driven by these QMQs persists as a robust, non-vanishing observable even in highly scalable polycrystalline thin films. We experimentally validate this quantum metric footprint by measuring nonlinear transport in thin films of elemental bismuth, observing a robust, surface-dominated, and broadband third-harmonic generation that persists up to room temperature. Our findings uncover a hidden role of the quantum metric in polycrystalline systems, establishing third-order nonlinear transport as a high-precision diagnostic tool of wavefunction geometry under ambient conditions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that Van Hove singularities in twisted double bilayer graphene lead to a giant Nernst signal, which serves as a sensitive probe of the material's Fermi surface topology. The large enhancement is theoretically linked to Lifshitz transitions occurring near these density of states peaks. By combining theory and experiment, the authors establish a universal thermoelectric signature for vHSs in moiré systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state transport, the concept of using topological features (like vHSs) to engineer measurable quantum responses (Nernst effect) is relevant for understanding exotic electronic states in low-dimensional or engineered quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate how Van Hove singularities (vHSs) influence thermal transport, specifically generating a large Nernst signal in moiré materials.

**Main result.** The authors show that vHSs in twisted double bilayer graphene generate an unusually large and tunable Nernst signal, which is attributed to Lifshitz transitions around these singularities.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements of thermopower and the Nernst effect with theoretical calculations based on semiclassical Boltzmann transport equations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is twisted double bilayer graphene (tDBLG), a moiré material whose electronic structure features vHSs. Theoretical analysis uses continuum theory and minimal tight-binding models to describe band dispersion and DOS.

**Key observables.** Nernst signal ($S_{yx}$), Seebeck coefficient ($S_{xx}$), Density of States (DOS) peaks, and the evolution of Hall density ($n_H$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angles ($	ext{D}1$, $	ext{D}2$), out-of-plane displacement field ($D$), magnetic field ($B$), and carrier filling fraction ($n_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical calculations sometimes neglect DOS inhomogeneity due to angle variation in real devices, and some models use minimal band structures that may not capture the full complexity of tDBLG.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate measurement setups, show $R_{xx}$ and $R_{xy}$ mapping vHSs via density divergence, and compare calculated vs. measured Nernst signals ($S_{yx}$) peaking sharply at the vHS locations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the physical problem (vHS effect on thermal transport) to presenting experimental evidence of large $S_{yx}$ peaks, followed by theoretical derivations linking this enhancement to Lifshitz transitions and Fermi surface topology changes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Twisted graphene layers host van Hove singularities (vHSs), peaks in the electronic density of states, thought to drive exotic phases in moiré materials, but their effect on thermal transport has remained unclear. Here we show that vHSs in twisted double bilayer graphene (tDBLG) generate an unusually large Nernst signal-the transverse voltage produced by a longitudinal temperature gradient in a magnetic field. The pronounced Nernst peaks at the vHSs of the conduction and valence bands of tDBLG are tunable by an electric field with a maximum value of $\sim 40$ $μV K^{-1} T^{-1}$ at $\sim 1$ $K$, which is comparable to the best-known Nernst materials. Our theoretical calculations show that the large enhancement of the Nernst signal arises from the Lifshitz transitions around the vHSs. These findings establish the Nernst effect as a sensitive probe of Fermi-surface topology in moiré materials, and identify a universal thermoelectric signature of van Hove singularities.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive computational framework to map the complex moiré band topology in twisted 2D semiconductors. By analyzing thousands of structures, the authors reveal that the resulting electronic properties are systematically governed by the parent band's valley character and the interlayer stacking symmetry. This establishes a crucial materials-level design principle for engineering flat and topological quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The systematic classification of topological phases based on crystal symmetry and electronic structure (valley/stacking) provides a powerful, generalizable framework for engineering quantum states in novel solid-state platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is establishing systematic, predictive principles for organizing the material dependence of topological moiré bands in twisted semiconductors.

**Main result.** The low-energy electronic structure is organized primarily by the valley character ($\Gamma$, K, M) and stacking symmetry, providing a materials-level design principle for flat/topological moiré bands.

**Method.** A high-throughput computational framework integrating structural relaxation (MLFFs), first-principles DFT calculations, and topological analysis was developed and applied to numerous bilayer prototypes.

**Model / system.** The system studied is twisted two-dimensional (2D) semiconductors forming moiré superlattices. The analysis covers 43 monolayers and 91 symmetry-inequivalent bilayers.

**Key observables.** Moiré electronic band structures, miniband width scaling ($W(	heta)$), topological invariants ($\mathbb{Z}_2$ indices, Chern numbers $|C_K|$), and valley character.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), stacking symmetry (AA vs. AB), and the parent band edge's valley character ($\Gamma$, K, M).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on classifying behavior based on the inherent valley structure of the constituent monolayers; $M$-valley physics is noted as an exception to standard classification.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the high-throughput workflow and show twist-angle dependence heatmaps for moiré bandwidths and topological characteristics, categorized by valley type ($\Gamma$, K, M) and stacking configuration.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a computational framework (workflow $ightarrow$ relaxation $ightarrow$ calculation). It then analyzes results based on the parent band's valley character ($\Gamma$-valley showing quadratic scaling; K-valley showing hybridization control; M-valley being material-specific) to derive universal design rules.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Twisted two-dimensional semiconductors provide a route to flat and topological moiré minibands, but systematic principles for organizing their material dependence have remained unclear. Here, we establish a high-throughput framework that integrates structural relaxation, first-principles electronic structure calculations, and moiré band topology. We apply this framework to 43 experimentally realized monolayers and 91 symmetry-inequivalent bilayer prototypes, yielding over 1,000 angle-resolved moiré electronic band structures. This database reveals that the low-energy moiré electronic structure is organized primarily by the valley character of the parent band edge together with stacking symmetry. In $Γ$-valley systems, the miniband width usually follows a nearly quadratic twist-angle scaling, consistent with a folding-dominated kinetic-energy scale. In $K$-valley systems, stacking-controlled interlayer hybridization governs whether parent Berry curvature is redistributed into isolated valley Chern minibands. By contrast, $M$-valley systems form a more material-specific class associated with anisotropic and symmetry-constrained band folding. The same valley-and-stacking hierarchy rationalizes the emergence or suppression of $\mathbb{Z}_2$ minibands, and surface termination in Janus bilayers provides a microscopic knob for changing the relevant valley character. These results establish a materials-level organizing principle for designing flat and topological moiré bands in twisted semiconductors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores the mathematical parallels between the Gibbs phenomenon in Fourier analysis and Friedel oscillations in electron density. Although they look similar due to sharp truncations, the authors rigorously argue that their physical origins are different. The work is valuable for creating a common conceptual language between electrical engineers and condensed-matter physicists.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a valuable educational bridge between electrical engineering concepts (Fourier series convergence) and condensed matter physics (electron screening), offering new ways to build intuition for complex many-body phenomena.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper compares the Gibbs phenomenon, arising from partial Fourier series approximations of discontinuous signals, with Friedel oscillations observed in electron density near impurities.

**Main result.** While both phenomena exhibit striking mathematical similarities due to sharp truncations in Fourier space, they are fundamentally distinct; Friedel oscillations arise from electronic screening and Fermi surface geometry, not merely Fourier truncation artifacts.

**Method.** The authors use theoretical analysis involving partial sums of Fourier series (for Gibbs) and summation over discrete energy levels derived from the non-interacting electron gas model (for Friedel).

**Model / system.** The primary models are a 1D electronic gas confined by a square potential, analyzed using both partial Fourier representations and Fermi-Dirac statistics at zero temperature.

**Key observables.** Oscillatory overshoot/undershoot in local electron density ($ho(x)$) near boundaries or impurities; the magnitude of this overshoot is compared to the universal 8.95% limit of the Gibbs phenomenon.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fermi wavelength ($\lambda_F$), system length ($L$), temperature ($T$), and the number of retained Fourier/energy modes ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on treating the system as semi-infinite or using approximations for large $N$ to compare the mathematical structures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Gibbs overshoot using a square wave via partial Fourier series, and show standing-wave patterns characteristic of Friedel oscillations in a 1D electron gas near an impurity.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first defining the two phenomena separately (Gibbs vs. Friedel) and then performing a detailed comparative analysis, culminating in arguments that highlight their structural parallels while maintaining physical distinction based on underlying mechanisms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We explore the similarities and differences between the Gibbs phenomenon in partial Fourier representations of discontinuous signals and Friedel oscillations in the electron density of a solid near an anomaly. Inspired by the apparent similarities of the two phenomena, we perform a detailed exploration of both from the viewpoint of an engineer being introduced to a concept from solid-state physics. Focusing on the density of an one-dimensional electronic gas confined by a square potential, we show that, despite the similarities, Friedel oscillations cannot be attributed to the inability of a partial Fourier series to describe a discontinuity. Nevertheless, the two phenomena do exhibit similarities, which can be exploited to develop intuition. By adopting an educational style, we hope to establish some common language between electrical engineers and condensed-matter physicists, hoping that this can further inspire the two communities to seek intuition and further comprehension within neighbouring but different disciplines.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel mathematical framework to topologically classify physical vector fields that suffer from amplitude zeros, which break standard normalization techniques. By embedding these $n$-dimensional fields into $(n+1)$-dimensional normalized spheres, the authors derive robust topological invariants applicable to diverse systems like magnetism and optics. This provides a unified tool for analyzing defects in complex physical media.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical field theory topology, the concept of robust topological invariants derived from singular or decaying fields is highly relevant for understanding defects in quantum materials (e.g., skyrmions) and phase transitions in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of topologically classifying physical vector fields whose amplitudes vanish at certain points, rendering conventional normalization-based methods invalid.

**Main result.** A general framework is introduced by embedding non-normalizable $n$-dimensional vector fields into $(n+1)$-dimensional normalized vector fields, allowing for a robust topological characterization via homotopy groups.

**Method.** The core methodology involves rescaling the original field to lie within a unit disk ($D^n$) and then constructing an embedding into the next higher dimension ($S^n$) using a specific lifting function $\Gamma(v)$.

**Model / system.** The framework is applied to various physical systems, including magnetic textures, ferroelectrics, and electromagnetic fields, demonstrating its utility for analyzing 1D, 2D, and 3D vector field configurations.

**Key observables.** Topological charges ($Q_{1D}, Q_{2D}, Q_{3D}$), which are derived from the resulting map's classification by homotopy groups ($\pi_m(S^n)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the field ($n$), the base space structure (compactifiable to $S^m$), and the choice of the lifting function $\Gamma(v)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes the base space can be compactified to a sphere, and that the target space is a closed star-body with respect to the origin.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mapping process: showing the original field ($	ilde{v}$), its rescaled version ($v$) onto a disk, and finally the normalized embedding ($V$) onto a sphere for 1D, 2D, and 3D examples.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the general mathematical framework first, then explicitly demonstrates its application to 1D, 2D, and 3D cases, emphasizing how the choice of the embedding function $\Gamma$ dictates the resulting topological invariant.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological classification of physical vector fields conventionally relies on field normalization and homotopy-based invariants. However, when field amplitudes vanish, normalization becomes ill-defined, preventing a direct topological characterization. Here, we introduce a general framework for the topological classification of non-normalizable $n$-dimensional vector fields with compactifiable base spaces by transforming them into $(n+1)$-dimensional normalized vector fields. This construction extends homotopy-based classification to fields containing amplitude zeros. We explicitly demonstrate the approach for one-, two-, and three-dimensional non-normalized vector fields and derive the corresponding topological invariants. The resulting topological charges are robust under continuous deformations and can change only when the embedding structure becomes singular. Our framework provides a unified route to the topological characterization of non-normalizable fields and opens the door to the study of topological phenomena in a broad range of systems, including magnetic textures, ferroelectrics, electromagnetic fields, and wave systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the complex behavior of multi-peaked waveforms undergoing self-focusing collapse in a 2D Nonlinear Schrödinger model. The authors demonstrate that while many stable-looking configurations exist near critical points, they are all dynamically unstable compared to the single-peak collapse. They employ advanced mathematical and computational techniques to map out this rich stability landscape.

**Why it may be interesting.** This paper provides deep insights into pattern formation and collapse dynamics in nonlinear wave equations, which are foundational to understanding energy localization and instability in quantum optics and BECs.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the self-focusing, collapse, and stability of multi-peaked waveforms governed by a nonlinear Schrödinger equation with general power-law nonlinearity in two dimensions.

**Main result.** A wide range of multi-peaked states bifurcate from the critical cubic nonlinearity threshold, but all such states are found to be less stable than the single-peak collapsing solution, which exhibits symmetry-breaking dynamics.

**Method.** The research combines analytical techniques (force balance modeling, bifurcation analysis) with advanced numerical methods (time integration, Newton's method, spectral stability analysis using high-performance computing libraries).

**Model / system.** The model is the two-dimensional Nonlinear Schrödinger (NLS) equation with a general power-law nonlinearity ($\sigma$), relevant to nonlinear optics and Bose-Einstein condensates.

**Key observables.** Blowup rate ($G$), eigenvalues (real and imaginary), peak positions ($X_i$), and the stability characteristics of multi-peaked vs. single-peak solutions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonlinearity exponent ($\sigma$), spatial dimension ($d=2$), and the blowup rate $G$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often treats the problem as a bifurcation from a critical threshold, and numerical stability relies on advanced continuation methods like pseudo-arclength tracking.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize numerical results showing the evolution of wave intensity ($|v|$), peak distances ($\|X_i\|_{L^2}$) versus $G$, and theoretical vs. computed eigenvalue dependencies on $G$.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses by establishing the physical model, deriving an effective 'interacting particle system' force balance, analyzing the bifurcation structure of multi-peaked states, and concluding with a detailed spectral stability analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the present work, we explore the self-focusing and resulting collapse of two-dimensional waveforms involving multiple pulses in a nonlinear Schroedinger equation with a general power-law nonlinearity. We find that a wide range of multi-peaked states bifurcate from the critical threshold of the cubic nonlinearity, thus representing ``bifurcations from infinity'', i.e., the relevant pulses start at infinite distance in the critical limit and draw nearer, as the nonlinearity exponent increases past that threshold. We identify the resulting ``interacting particle system'' as amounting to a force balance between the exponentially interacting tails (modulated by a suitable power law) and a linear phase-induced force. The equilibria emerging from this force balance are found to be in excellent agreement with the identified steady states of the partial differential equation. The spectral stability of multi-peaked configurations is analyzed, leading to the conclusion that all the relevant states are less stable than the single-peak collapsing solution whose stability was analyzed earlier. Indeed, we reveal both symmetry-breaking, as well as motion-inducing destabilizing dynamics, with the former ones among them being dominant and ultimately leading to a single dominant collapse spot. Moreover, we characterize systematically both the real and imaginary eigenvalues of multi-peaked configurations, partitioning them in groups of different sizes, described by powers of the solution's blowup rate G.

</details>

**Summary.** This research uses Shubnikov-de Haas spectroscopy on tetralayer graphene to quantitatively map out six distinct Fermi-surface topologies and their degeneracies. By combining magnetic and electric fields, the authors demonstrate a novel method to lift the valley degeneracy, providing a powerful tool for characterizing multiband quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete experimental framework using quantum oscillations to map complex electronic band structures (topology) while simultaneously quantifying subtle symmetry breaking effects like valley splitting, which is crucial for understanding topological quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is quantitatively reconstructing both the topology of Fermi pockets and their associated flavor degeneracies across Lifshitz transitions in multiband quantum systems.

**Main result.** The study successfully mapped six distinct Fermi-surface topologies in tetralayer graphene and demonstrated that combining a perpendicular magnetic field with an electric displacement field can lift the valley degeneracy via orbital-Zeeman coupling.

**Method.** Shubnikov-de Haas (SdH) spectroscopy is used to probe extremal areas, while tight-binding models are employed for theoretical comparison of degeneracies and splitting mechanisms.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a high-mobility, dual-gated Bernal-stacked tetralayer graphene (B-4LG) device. The electronic structure is modeled using an extended tight-binding Hamiltonian incorporating interlayer hopping and external fields.

**Key observables.** Fermi-surface topologies (gully, annular, etc.), oscillation frequencies ($f_i$), spin/valley degeneracies ($g_i$), and the valley splitting energy ($\Delta E_{	ext{valley}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Carrier density ($n$), perpendicular magnetic field ($B_\perp$), and displacement field ($D/\epsilon_0$). The splitting can reach several meV.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the applicability of the Lifshitz–Kosevich formalism for SdH oscillations, and theoretical calculations use specific tight-binding approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the evolution of six Fermi-surface topologies (Fig. 1), transport signatures confirming these regimes (Fig. 2), quantitative mapping of outer/inner contour frequencies vs. density (Fig. 3), and calculated single-particle Landau level spectra demonstrating valley splitting (Fig. 4, Fig. 6).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the experimental challenge using SdH spectroscopy on B-4LG to map topological transitions across six regimes. It then details how tuning fields ($D$ and $B$) allows for quantitative reconstruction of degeneracies. Finally, it uses advanced tight-binding calculations to theoretically explain the large valley splitting observed.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum oscillations offer a direct probe of Fermi-surface topology and electronic degeneracy, yet disentangling both simultaneously across the Lifshitz transitions of multiband systems has remained an open experimental challenge. Here, we use Shubnikov-de Haas spectroscopy on a high-mobility, dual-gated Bernal-stacked tetralayer graphene (B-4LG) device to quantitatively reconstruct the complete sequence of six distinct Fermi-surface topologies-gully, annular, singly connected, and multiband pockets. The extracted oscillation frequencies determine the extremal momentum-space areas and their spin, valley, and gully-resolved degeneracies, in quantitative agreement with our tight-binding calculations. We further show that a perpendicular magnetic-field, combined with displacement-field lifts the valley degeneracy through an orbital-Zeeman coupling, producing a single-particle valley splitting of several $meV$, far larger than in bilayer or trilayer graphene. Our work demonstrates a framework for tracking Fermi-surface topologies and their flavor degeneracies in multiband quantum materials.

</details>
